# Bacterial Surfaces and Barriers: Biogenesis and Pathogen-Host Interactions.
**Authors:** Bartholomew-Schoch J, Finnie JK, Gahlmann A, Columbus L
**Journal:** Chem Rev (2026)
**DOI:** [10.1021/acs.chemrev.5c01068](https://doi.org/10.1021/acs.chemrev.5c01068)
**PMC:** [PMC13721041](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC13721041/)

## Abstract

1. Chem Rev. 2026 Oct 28;126(20):11082-11151. doi: 10.1021/acs.chemrev.5c01068.

Bacterial Surfaces and Barriers: Biogenesis and Pathogen-Host Interactions.

Bartholomew-Schoch J(1), Finnie JK(1), Gahlmann A(1), Columbus L(1).

Author information:
(1)University of Virginia , Charlottesville, Virginia22904, United States.

The bacterial surface represents the primary interface between pathogens and
hosts, yet its chemical and architectural complexity challenges conventional
classification. This Review examines structural organization, molecular
composition, and biogenesis of bacterial surface components across the
Gracilicutes and Terrabacteria clades, linking mechanistic detail to pathogenic
function. We describe lipid diversity, integral membrane protein biogenesis, and
lipoprotein processing at the inner membrane and how these components support
surface remodeling, secretion, and virulence. We then examine the peptidoglycan
layer and its role in immune recognition and surface protein display, teichoic
acids and their contributions to antimicrobial resistance and immune evasion,
and S layers, whose crystalline arrays mediate adhesion and immune modulation.
The outer membrane section covers lipopolysaccharide biosynthesis, outer
membrane protein biogenesis and folding, lipoprotein trafficking, and
contributions to pathogenicity, while the mycobacterial section addresses the
mycolyl-arabinogalactan-peptidoglycan complex and virulence factors. We discuss
bacterial capsule diversity, biosynthesis, and use as vaccine antigens, along
with adhesive surface structures such as fimbriae, pili, and curli fibers and
their roles in colonization and biofilm formation, before covering secretion
systems types I through XI and their contributions to virulence. This Review
provides a framework for understanding how bacterial surface diversity shapes
infection and identifies chemical vulnerabilities for therapeutics.

1IntroductionBacteria adapt to and
evolve with their environment, with their
secreted proteins and extracellular surface proteins evolving the
fastest. Throughout this Review, the outer
or extracellular surface refers to all structures that are exposed
to or protrude into the extracellular environment, including the outermost
membrane or layer, surface appendages, capsular polymers, and surface-associated
secreted proteins. The bacterial extracellular surface controls how
bacteria interact with other organisms, from communication within
communities to direct contact between pathogens and hosts. Many bacteria
dynamically remodel their surface properties in response to environmental
changes, and this chemical diversity challenges our understanding
of how membrane proteins fold, insert, and function. The outer surface
of bacteria can be composed of lipoproteins, LPS, lipids, PG, and
membrane proteins (MPs) with the composition varying among organisms.
Each component can be modified to introduce additional chemical variability,
enabling the adaptation of the outer bacterial surface to specific
environmental niches and host interactions.Bacteria are classified
into two clades: Gracilicutes and Terrabacteria. The Gracilicutes clade contains only diderm bacteria,
and the Terrabacteria clade contains both diderm
(i.e., Gram-negative) and monoderm (i.e.,
Gram-positive) bacteria; both architectures can be found within the
same phylum. The classic monoderm architecture, exemplified by model
organisms such as Bacillus subtilis (Terrabacteria, Firmicutes), consists of an inner (cell) membrane
(IM) and a PG layer. The IM is the first lipid bilayer barrier closest
to the cytoplasm. The PG layer lies on the extracellular side of the
IM and consists of alternating sugar residues (N-acetylglucosamine
and N-acetylmuramic acid) cross-linked by short peptides into a rigid,
meshlike layer. The classic diderm bacterial architecture, exemplified
by Escherichia coli (Gracilicutes, Proteobacteria), adds a second lipid-containing
OM beyond the IM and PG layer.For many bacteria, membrane organization
deviates considerably
from the two classical monoderm and diderm architectures. For example,
some monoderm bacteria possess additional outer layers yet lack the
machinery to synthesize LPS or insert outer membrane proteins (OMPs),
for these bacteria, a new classification within Terrabacteria, neoderm, was recently proposed. Some monoderm and diderm bacteria
additionally possess an outermost proteinaceous surface (S) layer.
Some bacteria have an IM and an S-layer but lack a PG layer, such
as Dehalococcoides mccartyi, whereas cyanobacteria
and Atribacter laminatus possess additional cytoplasmic
membrane structures. Beyond the membrane layers themselves, many bacteria
project capsular polymers, surface appendages, and multiprotein secretion
machines that extend through or beyond the cell envelope to mediate
direct contact with the environment and the host.This review
examines the structural organization, molecular composition,
and biogenesis of bacterial surface layers and appendages (Figure

each component contributes to pathogen–host interactions. The
Review is organized from the innermost membrane to the outermost layers
and appendages. We begin with the IM (Section

of integral membrane proteins (IMPs), lipoprotein processing, and
the roles of IM components in pathogenesis. We then discuss the PG
layer (Section

including its biosynthesis, modification by sortase enzymes, and recognition
by host immune receptors. Next, we describe teichoic acids (Section
) and their roles
in surface charge modulation, antimicrobial resistance, and host interactions.
The S-layer (Section
) is next, with sections describing secondary cell wall polysaccharide
anchoring, S-protein biogenesis, and virulence functions. In Section
 we examine the
outer membrane (OM) of diderm bacteria (Section
) in depth, encompassing LPS biosynthesis
and immune recognition, OMP biogenesis and folding, LPS-OMP interactions,

to pathogenicity. We treat the mycobacterial cell envelope (Section
) as a distinct
architecture, discussing mycomembrane composition, PE/PPE protein
biogenesis, and the roles of mycobacterial surface components in immune
evasion and nutrient acquisition. In Section
, we cover bacterial capsules (Section
), including their chemical

their structural diversity, assembly mechanisms, and roles in adhesion,
colonization, and immune activation. Finally, in Section
, we describe secretion systems
from Type I through Type XI, with emphasis on their architecture,
mechanisms, and contributions to pathogenesis. Together, these sections
provide an integrated view of the bacterial surface as a chemically

Introduction

Bacteria adapt to and
evolve with their environment, with their
secreted proteins and extracellular surface proteins evolving the
fastest. Throughout this Review, the outer
or extracellular surface refers to all structures that are exposed
to or protrude into the extracellular environment, including the outermost
membrane or layer, surface appendages, capsular polymers, and surface-associated
secreted proteins. The bacterial extracellular surface controls how
bacteria interact with other organisms, from communication within
communities to direct contact between pathogens and hosts. Many bacteria
dynamically remodel their surface properties in response to environmental
changes, and this chemical diversity challenges our understanding
of how membrane proteins fold, insert, and function. The outer surface
of bacteria can be composed of lipoproteins, LPS, lipids, PG, and
membrane proteins (MPs) with the composition varying among organisms.
Each component can be modified to introduce additional chemical variability,
enabling the adaptation of the outer bacterial surface to specific
environmental niches and host interactions.

Bacteria are classified
into two clades: Gracilicutes and Terrabacteria. The Gracilicutes clade contains only diderm bacteria,
and the Terrabacteria clade contains both diderm
(i.e., Gram-negative) and monoderm (i.e.,
Gram-positive) bacteria; both architectures can be found within the
same phylum. The classic monoderm architecture, exemplified by model
organisms such as Bacillus subtilis (Terrabacteria, Firmicutes), consists of an inner (cell) membrane
(IM) and a PG layer. The IM is the first lipid bilayer barrier closest
to the cytoplasm. The PG layer lies on the extracellular side of the
IM and consists of alternating sugar residues (N-acetylglucosamine
and N-acetylmuramic acid) cross-linked by short peptides into a rigid,
meshlike layer. The classic diderm bacterial architecture, exemplified
by Escherichia coli (Gracilicutes, Proteobacteria), adds a second lipid-containing
OM beyond the IM and PG layer.

For many bacteria, membrane organization
deviates considerably
from the two classical monoderm and diderm architectures. For example,
some monoderm bacteria possess additional outer layers yet lack the
machinery to synthesize LPS or insert outer membrane proteins (OMPs),
for these bacteria, a new classification within Terrabacteria, neoderm, was recently proposed. Some monoderm and diderm bacteria
additionally possess an outermost proteinaceous surface (S) layer.
Some bacteria have an IM and an S-layer but lack a PG layer, such
as Dehalococcoides mccartyi, whereas cyanobacteria
and Atribacter laminatus possess additional cytoplasmic
membrane structures. Beyond the membrane layers themselves, many bacteria
project capsular polymers, surface appendages, and multiprotein secretion
machines that extend through or beyond the cell envelope to mediate
direct contact with the environment and the host.

This review
examines the structural organization, molecular composition,
and biogenesis of bacterial surface layers and appendages (Figure
), focusing on how
each component contributes to pathogen–host interactions. The
Review is organized from the innermost membrane to the outermost layers
and appendages. We begin with the IM (Section
) and cover lipid biosynthesis, the biogenesis
of integral membrane proteins (IMPs), lipoprotein processing, and
the roles of IM components in pathogenesis. We then discuss the PG
layer (Section
),
including its biosynthesis, modification by sortase enzymes, and recognition
by host immune receptors. Next, we describe teichoic acids (Section
) and their roles
in surface charge modulation, antimicrobial resistance, and host interactions.
The S-layer (Section
) is next, with sections describing secondary cell wall polysaccharide
anchoring, S-protein biogenesis, and virulence functions. In Section
 we examine the
outer membrane (OM) of diderm bacteria (Section
) in depth, encompassing LPS biosynthesis
and immune recognition, OMP biogenesis and folding, LPS-OMP interactions,
lipoprotein trafficking, and the contributions of these OM components
to pathogenicity. We treat the mycobacterial cell envelope (Section
) as a distinct
architecture, discussing mycomembrane composition, PE/PPE protein
biogenesis, and the roles of mycobacterial surface components in immune
evasion and nutrient acquisition. In Section
, we cover bacterial capsules (Section
), including their chemical
diversity, biosynthetic pathways, and anti-phagocytic and complement-evasive
functions, as well as their exploitation as vaccine targets. From
surfaces, we then move on to a discussion of pili, fimbriae, and curli
(Section
) regarding
their structural diversity, assembly mechanisms, and roles in adhesion,
colonization, and immune activation. Finally, in Section
, we describe secretion systems
from Type I through Type XI, with emphasis on their architecture,
mechanisms, and contributions to pathogenesis. Together, these sections
provide an integrated view of the bacterial surface as a chemically
diverse and complex environment.

2Overview of the Components and Organization
of the IMThe IM is a phospholipid bilayer that forms the
primary permeability
barrier in all bacteria, separating the cytoplasm from the external
environment. The IM is composed of chemically diverse glycerophospholipids
whose specific composition varies across species and in response to
environmental conditions. Embedded within or peripherally associated
with this bilayer are three major classes of proteins: IMPs, which
are predominantly α-helical and span the membrane via hydrophobic
transmembrane segments; lipoproteins, which are anchored to the membrane
surface through covalently attached lipid modifications; and peripheral
MPs, which associate transiently with either face of the bilayer through
electrostatic or hydrophobic interactions. Together, these components
house the essential machinery for energy generation, nutrient transport,
signal transduction, protein secretion, and cell envelope biosynthesis.2.1Overview and Structure of LipidsBacterial IM lipids are predominantly glycerophospholipids, which
have a glycerol-3-phosphate backbone that is esterified at the sn-1 and sn-2 positions with fatty acyl
chains, and they have a polar head group attached via a phosphodiester
bond at the sn-3 position. Head group identity defines
the lipid class: phosphatidylethanolamine (PE) has a zwitterionic
headgroup, phosphatidylglycerol (PG) has a negatively charged headgroup,
and cardiolipin (CL) has two phosphatidyl moieties linked through
a central glycerol and, thus, has a dianion headgroup. Additional
classes found across bacterial species include phosphatidylserine
(PS), phosphatidylinositol (PI), and phosphatidylcholine (PC). Fatty
acyl chain composition varies independently of headgroup. Bacterial
membranes are composed of saturated, monounsaturated, and branched
acyl chains through an FASII pathway. Glycolipids, in which one or
more sugar residues replace the phosphate-containing headgroup, are
also present in many bacterial IMs and serve as the lipid retention
molecules in teichoic acid biosynthesis (Section
).2.1.1Overview and Structure of IMPsIM proteins (IMPs) have transmembrane α-helices. The topology
and folding of α-helical IMPs are governed by several physical
and chemical principles. The most fundamental principle is the hydrophobic
effect, which drives the partitioning of hydrophobic transmembrane
segments into the lipid bilayer core. Transmembrane
helices typically contain 18–25 hydrophobic residues, sufficient
to span the ∼30 Å hydrophobic core of the membrane. The
hydrophobicity threshold for membrane insertion can be quantified
by biological hydrophobicity scales, with the Wimley-White scale being
particularly predictive for bacterial IMPs.
The “positive-inside rule” is a critical determinant
of MP topology in bacteria. This principle states that positively
charged residues (lysine and arginine) are preferentially located
on the cytoplasmic side of the membrane, whereas the periplasmic loops
tend to have fewer positive charges.
,
 This charge
bias is likely due to two factors that may be related: the membrane
potential and interactions with the Sec translocon. Experimentally
inverting the charge distribution flips the topology of MPs, demonstrating
the strength of this determinant.
Helix–helix interactions within the membrane are mediated
by specific packing motifs. The GxxxG motif allows close helix–helix
contacts through glycine-mediated backbone interactions, while polar
residues facing away from the helical contact site can form interhelical
hydrogen bonds that stabilize oligomeric structures. Aromatic residues, particularly tryptophan and tyrosine,
preferentially localize to the membrane-water interface regions, serving
as ’anchors’ that position helices at the correct depth
in the membrane.
Lipid-protein interactions
play crucial roles beyond simple hydrophobic
matching. Anionic lipids such as PG and CL interact electrostatically
with positively charged residues on protein surfaces, influencing
both the localization and activity. The thickness and fluidity of
the membrane also impact protein folding, hydrophobic mismatch between
transmembrane helices, and bilayer thickness can drive conformational
changes or protein aggregation. In bacteria
that modulate membrane composition in response to temperature or pH
stress by producing unique lipid species, lipid-protein interactions
become particularly important for maintaining protein function.
2.1.2Overview and Structure of LipoproteinsBacterial lipoproteins are a unique class of membrane-associated
proteins that are anchored to either the periplasmic leaflet of IM
or both the periplasmic and extracellular leaflets of the OM via lipid
modifications (Figure
). Lipoproteins perform diverse cellular functions critical to pathogenesis,
including surface adhesion, signal transduction, and antibiotic resistance.
,
 Lipoproteins have a soluble domain that is anchored to the membrane
at the N-terminus via a lipid moiety. The sequences and structures
of the soluble domains of the mature lipoproteins are diverse. However,
all preprolipoproteins start with an N-terminal signal sequence that
is 10 to 25 residues long and ends with a consensus lipobox sequence,
LAGC, with some variation in the first, second, and third positions.
The signal sequence is cleaved (Figure
) and the mature lipoprotein is formed.2
Lipoprotein biogenesis
and localization in monoderm and diderm
bacteria. Lipoproteins are synthesized as pre-prolipoproteins
with N-terminal signal peptides that direct them to the Sec translocon
(SecYEG) for membrane insertion. Following translocation, the conserved
cysteine residue is lipidated by prolipoprotein diacylglyceryl transferase
(Lgt), adding a diacylglycerol (DAG) moiety. The signal peptide is
then cleaved by signal peptidase II (LspA/Lsp), generating an apolipoprotein.
In diderm bacteria (right panel), an additional N-acylation by apolipoprotein
N-acyltransferase (Lnt) produces triacylated lipoproteins (inset,
chemical structure shown) that are sorted to the OM via the Lol trafficking
system (LolABCDE). In monoderm bacteria (left panel), which lack an
OM, diacylated lipoproteins remain anchored in the cell membrane.
Organisms may contain different proteins that modify the apolipoprotein,
such as lipoprotein intramolecular transacylase (Lit) that catalyzes
intramolecular acyl transfer generating a lyso-lipoprotein. PE, phosphatidylethanolamine;
PG, phosphatidylglycerol. Created with BioRender.com.2.2Biogenesis of the IMThe assembly
of the IM requires coordinated biosynthesis and insertion of three
distinct molecular classes: lipids, IMPs, and lipoproteins. Peripheral
MPs are not covered in this Review due to the extensive scope. Lipids
are synthesized in the cytoplasm and incorporated into the bilayer,
establishing the physical foundations of the membrane. IMPs are inserted
co- or post-translationally through dedicated translocon machinery,
while lipoproteins undergo a conserved series of post-translational
modifications before being anchored to the IM (or trafficked to the
OM in diderm bacteria). Each of these processes is described in the
following subsections.2.2.1Lipid BiosynthesisThe bacterial
type II fatty acid synthesis (FASII) pathway elongates alkyl chains
through repeated cycles of condensation, reduction, and dehydration.
This extensive and complex biosynthetic pathway has been discussed
at length in various review articles; as such, this section will not
cover it in detail. Beyond the canonical
bacterial phospholipids such as phosphoethanolamine (PE), phosphatidylglycerol
(PG), and cardiolipin (CL), bacteria have evolved a remarkable range
of structural variations to adapt to their specific environments.
Certain species produce unusual lipids, such as ornithine lipids (OLs),
hopanoids (HOPs), glycolipids (GLs), and diacylglycerols (DAGs). The
GLs are hypothesized to be glycerophospholipid substitutes under phosphorus-limiting
conditions. OLs, while rare, contribute
to greater bilayer order and contain a negatively charged carboxyl
group rather than a phosphate group in glycerophospholipids. HOPs are structurally analogous to eukaryotic
sterols and are thought to perform similar roles in modulating membrane
fluidity and organization, including the possible formation of lipid
nanodomains. Several lipid classes once
considered exclusive to eukaryotes, such as ceramides, have been identified
in bacteria through emerging biochemical and bioinformatics research.
Thus, the lipid composition of bacteria has been an active area of
research.2.2.2IM Protein Biogenesis and FoldingIM proteins (IMPs) are integrated co-translationally via the Sec
translocon or post-translationally via the YidC insertase. YidC is an insertase that can function independently
or in cooperation with Sec translocon (also referred to as SecYEG).
Some small IMPs with simple topologies insert via YidC alone, bypassing
the Sec machinery entirely. YidC also
assists with the folding of larger IMPs by stabilizing nascent chains
and facilitating membrane integration. The mechanism of YidC-mediated
insertion involves a hydrophilic groove that shields the hydrophobic
transmembrane segments during membrane crossing. However, the Sec pathway is the primary route for most
of the IMPs. The Sec system mediates the translocation and insertion
of polypeptides in and across the IM and is the most conserved and
universally distributed protein export pathway in eukaryotes, prokaryotes,
and archaea. SecYEG is a heterotrimeric
membrane-embedded channel that forms a protein-conducting pore with
a central constriction and a lateral gate opening into the lipid bilayer,
allowing integration of hydrophobic segments into the membrane.
,,
 In addition to IMPs, Sec translocon
substrates include periplasmic enzymes, prematurely unfolded OMPs,
secreted enzymes, cell envelope assembly factors such as BamA and
LptD, and numerous other proteins, which all have an N-terminal signal
peptide that is typically cleaved after translocation.
,
 The Sec pathway was identified through genetic and biochemical studies
in the 1970s and 1980s, and its structural basis was elucidated at
high resolution by X-ray crystallography and cryo-electron microscopy.
These structures revealed multiple conformational states corresponding
to closed, primed, and actively translocating conformations.
,,,

Translocation through the Sec channel is driven by the coordinated
action of ATP hydrolysis and the proton motive force across the IM.
,
 A cytoplasmic peripheral ATPase (SecA) associates with the Sec complex
and drives translocation of the unfolded protein through the Sec channel.
,
 Unusually long stretches of hydrophobic signal sequences or transmembrane
domains are recognized by the signal recognition particle (SRP) as
they emerge from the ribosome, targeting the ribosome-nascent chain
complex directly to the SecYEG translocon at the membrane. Cycles of ATP binding and hydrolysis induce
conformational changes in the cytosolic ATPase that promote directional
insertion of successive polypeptide segments into the channel.
,,
 At the same time, the electrochemical
gradient across the membrane contributes to directional transport,
likely by biasing diffusion of the polypeptide toward the periplasm
and suppressing backward movement.
,
 The channel
itself exhibits pronounced conformational flexibility: a plug domain
occludes the pore in the resting state, the central constriction formed
by hydrophobic residues expands during translocation, and the lateral
gate opens to permit partitioning of transmembrane helices into the
bilayer.
,,,
 Translocation can also occur post-translationally
through the chaperone SecB, which binds newly synthesized preproteins
and stabilizes them in an unfolded state, preventing aggregation. SecB then shuttles and transfers the bound,
unfolded protein across the cytoplasm to the membrane-bound SecA channel
for further translocation. Despite extensive
structural and biochemical characterization of the Sec translocon,
several fundamental questions about its mechanism remain unresolved.
These include how the ATPase conformational cycles are coupled to
substrate movement, what are the relative contributions of power-stroke
versus Brownian ratchet models of translocation, and how is substrate
flux regulated under conditions of high demand.
,−









The substrates of the Sec pathway are initially unfolded at
the
start of translocation; however, some proteins need to be properly
folded in the cytoplasm and translocated across the IM. For these
proteins, an alternative translocation system was used. The Tat (twin-arginine
translocation) system is an additional protein insertion and export
pathway that mediates the insertion or translocation of fully folded
proteins across the IM.
−

 In contrast to the Sec system,
which transports unfolded substrates, the Tat pathway accommodates
proteins that have already acquired tertiary structure, often including
tightly bound cofactors, such as metal centers, complex prosthetic
groups, or assembled multimeric domains. The core Tat architecture comprises a membrane-embedded receptor
complex that recognizes N-terminal signal peptides bearing a conserved
twin-arginine motif, together with a dynamic oligomeric pore-forming
assembly recruited upon substrate engagement.
−

 These components
are distributed within the IM and undergo substrate-induced reorganization
to form a transient translocation conduit.
,,
 In diderm bacteria, translocated Tat substrates
are delivered to the periplasm, where they perform functions such
as redox catalysis, cofactor-dependent enzymatic reactions, cell envelope
biogenesis, and virulence-associated processes.
−

 The Tat pathway
was identified in the 1990s through genetic studies of protein export,
independent of the Sec system. Subsequent structural work using cryoelectron
microscopy, electron tomography, and biochemical reconstitution has
revealed that the system assembles into variable-sized oligomeric
complexes rather than a pre-formed, static channel.
−




The Tat system is driven exclusively by the
proton motive force
across the IM without direct coupling to ATP hydrolysis.
,,
 Substrate proteins bearing the
twin-arginine signal peptide are first recognized by the membrane
receptor complex, which triggers the recruitment and assembly of additional
membrane components into a transient, size-adaptive pore that is capable
of accommodating folded substrates of varying dimensions.
,,
 Current mechanistic models propose
that the proton gradient induces conformational changes and/or local
membrane perturbations that facilitate protein translocation, although
the precise coupling mechanism remains unresolved.
,
 A distinctive feature of the Tat pathway is its apparent ability
to preserve membrane integrity while translocating large, folded proteins,
suggesting that the translocation may be highly dynamic and involve
localized thinning or transient lipid-bilayer disruption.
−

 Despite significant progress in understanding the Tat translocation
mechanism, several key questions remain. These include how the system
discriminates between properly folded and misfolded substrates, how
pore size is regulated in response to substrate geometry, and how
proton motive force is mechanistically coupled to large-scale membrane
remodeling.
,−


Quality
control of the IM is vital in both monoderm and diderm
bacteria. The AAA+ protease FtsH recognizes and degrades misfolded
IMPs, preventing the accumulation of non-functional proteins. The
chaperone DnaK/DnaJ system also plays a role in maintaining IMP integrity,
particularly for proteins with large periplasmic or cytoplasmic domains. In addition to chaperones, phospholipids influence
the IMP folding and stability. Anionic lipids such as CL and PG cluster
around certain IMPs, stabilizing functional oligomers.
,
 The asymmetric distribution of lipids between the inner and outer
leaflets appears to differ between monoderm and diderm bacteria, suggesting
distinct lipid microdomains that may influence the protein topology
and function.
2.2.3IM Lipoprotein BiogenesisIn both
monoderm and diderm bacteria, lipoproteins are matured through a conserved
post-translational processing pathway (Figure
) that consists of a series of enzymes localized
to the IM. The biogenesis of all lipoproteins
(IM or OM) starts with the recognition of the N-terminal signal sequence
by either the Sec or the Tat translocation system, which directs the
nascent lipoprotein to the IM. Once at the membrane, diacylglyceryl
transferase (Lgt) cleaves a DAG moiety from an endogenous PG lipid
and covalently attaches it to a conserved cysteine residue within
the lipoprotein motif. Following this
diacylation, lipoprotein signal peptidase II (LspA) cleaves the N-terminal
signal peptide. Finally, a variable third
acyl modification occurs, which is dependent on the bacterial species
or a specific lipoprotein. This modification may involve N-acylation
by lipoprotein N-acyltransferase (Lnt) or transacylation by intramolecular
lipoprotein transferase (Lit). Upon completion
of this pathway, the mature lipoproteins are competent to perform
their cellular functions. Lgt and LspA are essential in diderm bacteria,
,
 and Lnt and Lit are not essential in certain bacteria. However,
for some bacteria, such as Helicobacter pylori
 and Streptococcus pneumoniae, Lnt is not essential for survival
but is essential for colonization.In monoderm bacteria, mature
lipoproteins either remain associated with the IM to perform their
functions or are secreted into the environment. In diderm bacteria,
many IM lipoproteins play roles in cell viability and overall maintenance,
including membrane homeostasis, envelope integrity, and stress-signaling
responses. Although these functions are
vital for cell survival, they are not directly involved in infection
pathways or pathogenesis. Lipoproteins in diderm bacteria can be transported
to the OM and is described in Section
.2.3Role of IM in PathogenesisThe IM
interacts directly and indirectly with the host pathogens. In monoderm
bacteria, the IM components are more directly exposed to host immune
surveillance and can engage host receptors or modulate immune signaling.
In diderm bacteria, the IM is further shielded by the OM and its pathogenic
contributions are largely indirect. The IM provides the structural
foundation for translocation complexes, secretion systems, lipid trafficking,
and surface-display pathways that arm the bacterial exterior with
virulence factors. Across both architectures, the lipids, integral
proteins, and lipoproteins of the IM collectively shape the outcome
of infection through mechanisms ranging from direct immune activation
to support surface remodeling and toxin delivery.2.3.1IM Lipids in PathogenesisBeyond
proteins and lipoproteins, the lipids comprising the IM bilayer also
contribute significantly to pathogenesis. Bacteria use many lipid
moieties as carriers during the synthesis of surface-layer components
(e.g., PGs, teichoic acids, and polysaccharides). These surface layers
extend into the extracellular space and serve both as structural and
pathogenic functions. Pathogenic bacteria have evolved additional
mechanisms to adapt their membranes to host environments. For instance,
during infection, S. aureus acquires exogenous unsaturated
fatty acids from host cells and incorporates them into its membrane
lipids. These acquired fatty acids increase the production of branched-chain
fatty acids, which reduces lipoprotein-mediated toll-like receptors
(TLR)­2 activation. An example of membrane
reorganization in bacteria in response to antibiotic delivery after
infection is found in Enterococcus faecalis, which
utilizes exogenous oleic acid to increase resistance to the membrane-targeting
antibiotic daptomycin. Daptomycin, and
other membrane-disrupting antimicrobials such as amphotericin B or
polymyxin B, intercalate into microbial membranes, where the partitioning
of these molecules/peptides disrupts the stability of the cell membrane,
leading to cell death.
,
 Daptomycin is positively charged
and partitions into the membrane through interactions with negatively
charged phospholipid head groups, such as PG. Studies have shown that E. faecalis cells that are not readily resistant to daptomycin
scavenge oleic acid and incorporate it into their membranes, leading
to a downstream decrease in PG synthesis and conferring resistance
to the molecule. The obligate bacterium Chlamydia trachomatis relies on scavenging oleic acids from
host cells to form unsaturated fatty acids. Acyltransferase LpaT and acyl-ACP synthetase AasC enable the bacterium
to incorporate and synthesize phospholipids not endogenously produced
by the cell, thereby saving energy during infection for other biosynthetic
pathways.
2.3.2IM Proteins in PathogenesisThe
IM machinery is responsible for supporting PG synthesis, sorting,
and modifying lipoproteins and OMPs, supporting secretion systems,
assembling pili, and much more. Because of this central role, several
components are involved in the pathogenesis. Secretion systems (Section
), such as the
Type III secretion system (T3SS), span the entire cell envelope. A
portion of the complex is embedded in the IM and extends through the
periplasm and the OM to reach the extracellular space, where it secretes
proteins from the cell. Thus, the IM provides a supporting role in
infection by containing anchoring components of the secretion systems. Similarly, the IM supports host-cell interaction
by trafficking LPS to the OM, specifically via the LptFGC protein
complex, which accounts for half of the LPS trafficking complex. Although lipids and IMPs provide a more indirect
link to pathogenesis, some lipoproteins, particularly in monoderm
bacteria, play pathogenic roles.2.3.3IM Lipoproteins in PathogenesisMonoderm IM lipoproteins fulfill diverse roles in pathogenesis, including
the acquisition and transport of extracellular iron. Iron is essential for bacterial growth, yet its limited
bioavailability in the host necessitates specialized uptake systems
for a successful infection. In S. aureus, the number
of lipoprotein-mediated iron uptake systems correlates positively
with pathogenicity.
S. aureus encodes eight lipoproteins involved in iron acquisition and transport,
including the well-characterized proteins FhuD1 and FhuD2. These lipoproteins
bind iron­(III)-hydroxamate siderophores and transfer them to the adenosine
triphosphate binding cassette (ABC) transporter FhuCBG. Bacterial lipoproteins also bind to amino acids,
peptides, and lipids to assist in transport. Other bacterial lipoproteins
are critical to fitness and pathogenicity. For instance, PrsA is a
highly conserved surface lipoprotein found across many monoderm bacterial
species, including S. pneumoniae, B. subtilis, S. aureus, and others. PrsA, a parvulin-type peptidyl-prolyl cis/trans-isomerases (PPIase), catalyzes the structural rotation of proline
residues between cis and trans conformations
and is crucial for post-translational protein folding at the membrane. Although PrsA is highly conserved across monoderm
bacteria, the substrates differ across species. In group A Streptococcus, PrsA inactivation resulted in a significant
decrease in active exotoxin B (SpeB), as well decreased adherence
to host cells, biofilm formation, and virulence.
,
 Like PrsA, several other lipoproteins interact directly with host
proteins and cells.Some bacterial lipoproteins interact with
TLRs on the host cells and engage in cell invasion. Secreted lipoproteins
bind TLR2 through their lipid modification at the N-terminus and trigger
a cascading immune response from the host. A genomic cluster of lipoproteins in S. aureus contributes
to internalization of the bacterium into nonprofessional antigen-presenting
cells and is, thus, directly involved in bacterial virulence. Although there are numerous examples of lipoprotein
directly facilitating host interactions, there are still many lipoproteins
of unknown function that need to be investigated.2.3.4Outer-IM Vesicles in PathogenesisMercade et al. report in multiple publications that
outer-IM vesicles (O-IMVs) are secreted by various pathogenic diderm
bacteria such as Neisseria gonorrhoeae, Pseudomonas aeruginosa, and Acinetobacter baumanii. These O-IMVs contain periplasmic proteins, LPS, and other
OM lipids, cytoplasmic proteins, and other components such as DNA,
ATP, and IM proteins. Proteomic analysis
of O-IMVs from N. gonorrhoeae found that 13.7% of
the proteins were IM and 18% were cytoplasmic proteins. The thiol:disulfide interchange protein DsbD
and pyruvate dehydrogenase were found in the O-IMVs.
Shewanella vesiculosa M7T increases
in O-IMV secretion during the late exponential to early stationary
phase.
,
 In response to prophage-mediated explosive
cell lysis, single- and double-layer O-IMVs were observed.
,
 O-IMVs may facilitate the delivery and secretion of bacterial cytoplasmic
and IM components providing an additional mechanism of host interactions.2.4IM Therapeutic Targets2.4.1IM Antibiotic TargetsThe bacterial
IM houses several essential biosynthetic and assembly machineries
that are either absent from or sufficiently divergent in eukaryotic
cells to permit selective therapeutic intervention. Many IM components
and processes are thus promising antibiotic targets, with inhibition
often resulting in bactericidal activity due to downstream effects
on cell envelope integrity. The bacterial type II fatty acid synthesis
system, a set of discrete monofunctional enzymes distinct from the
mammalian fatty acid synthesis pathway, is an antibiotic target. FabI,
the enoyl-acyl carrier protein reductase, catalyzes the final reductive
step of the fatty acid elongation cycle and is the most advanced target
in this pathway. Triclosan and isoniazid established proof of concept
for FabI inhibition. Afabicin (Debio 1450), a prodrug targeting staphylococcal
FabI, completed a Phase 2 clinical trial for acute bacterial skin
and skin structure infections caused by staphylococci. CG400549, another FabI inhibitor,
is also in clinical development for staphylococcal infections. The mycobacterial FabI homolog, InhA, is inhibited
by isoniazid. However, isoniazid is a prodrug that requires activation
by a catalase-peroxidase. InhA inhibitors that bypass this activation
step are under development to address resistance arising from catalase-peroxidase
mutations. FabH, the β-ketoacyl-ACP
synthase III that initiates each elongation cycle, represents an underexploited
but promising target with diverse inhibitor scaffolds reported in
preclinical studies.
The lipoprotein
processing enzyme LspA (Figure
), is essential for survival in diderm bacteria and is inhibited
by the natural cyclic depsipeptides globomycin and myxovirescin. Crystal
structures of methicillin-resistant S. aureus (MRSA)
LspA bound to both compounds revealed provided a structural framework
for inhibitor design. Computationally
designed cyclic peptide analogues of globomycin with improved stability
exhibit activity against a group of highly virulent, antibiotic-resistant
bacteria responsible for most global hospital-acquired infections.
In mycobacteria, the IM MmpL3 exports
trehalose monomycolate across
the IM as an obligate step in mycolic acid cell wall biosynthesis
(see Section
) and
is the target of multiple structurally diverse compound classes. SQ109,
an ethambutol analog and MmpL3 inhibitor, has completed Phase 2b/3 clinical trials for MDR pulmonary tuberculosis and shows
synergistic activity with front-line agents including isoniazid, rifampicin,
and bedaquiline.
2.4.2IM Vaccine TargetsThe IM is the
most occluded bacterial surface from the extracellular space and does
not present antigens readily recognized by the host immune system,
making it a poor source of vaccine candidates compared with outer-surface
components. Despite this limitation, IM lipoproteins can achieve a
surface exposure. The iron-acquisition lipoprotein FhuD2 of S. aureus similarly achieves surface exposure despite IM
anchoring and has been developed as a vaccine antigen. FhuD2 is included
in the multicomponent 4C-Staph formulation, alongside the conserved
lipoprotein Csa1A and secreted virulence factors. This formulation
showed protection across multiple murine infection models. Beyond their roles as direct antigens, the lipid
modifications added to lipoproteins at the IM by Lgt confer adjuvant
activity that stimulates TLR2. This stimulatory property is exploited
to enhance the immunogenicity of recombinant vaccine antigens by engineering
the lipoprotein signal peptide and lipobox into heterologous proteins.

Overview of the Components and Organization
of the IM

The IM is a phospholipid bilayer that forms the
primary permeability
barrier in all bacteria, separating the cytoplasm from the external
environment. The IM is composed of chemically diverse glycerophospholipids
whose specific composition varies across species and in response to
environmental conditions. Embedded within or peripherally associated
with this bilayer are three major classes of proteins: IMPs, which
are predominantly α-helical and span the membrane via hydrophobic
transmembrane segments; lipoproteins, which are anchored to the membrane
surface through covalently attached lipid modifications; and peripheral
MPs, which associate transiently with either face of the bilayer through
electrostatic or hydrophobic interactions. Together, these components
house the essential machinery for energy generation, nutrient transport,
signal transduction, protein secretion, and cell envelope biosynthesis.

2.1Overview and Structure of LipidsBacterial IM lipids are predominantly glycerophospholipids, which
have a glycerol-3-phosphate backbone that is esterified at the sn-1 and sn-2 positions with fatty acyl
chains, and they have a polar head group attached via a phosphodiester
bond at the sn-3 position. Head group identity defines
the lipid class: phosphatidylethanolamine (PE) has a zwitterionic
headgroup, phosphatidylglycerol (PG) has a negatively charged headgroup,
and cardiolipin (CL) has two phosphatidyl moieties linked through
a central glycerol and, thus, has a dianion headgroup. Additional
classes found across bacterial species include phosphatidylserine
(PS), phosphatidylinositol (PI), and phosphatidylcholine (PC). Fatty
acyl chain composition varies independently of headgroup. Bacterial
membranes are composed of saturated, monounsaturated, and branched
acyl chains through an FASII pathway. Glycolipids, in which one or
more sugar residues replace the phosphate-containing headgroup, are
also present in many bacterial IMs and serve as the lipid retention
molecules in teichoic acid biosynthesis (Section
).2.1.1Overview and Structure of IMPsIM proteins (IMPs) have transmembrane α-helices. The topology
and folding of α-helical IMPs are governed by several physical
and chemical principles. The most fundamental principle is the hydrophobic
effect, which drives the partitioning of hydrophobic transmembrane
segments into the lipid bilayer core. Transmembrane
helices typically contain 18–25 hydrophobic residues, sufficient
to span the ∼30 Å hydrophobic core of the membrane. The
hydrophobicity threshold for membrane insertion can be quantified
by biological hydrophobicity scales, with the Wimley-White scale being
particularly predictive for bacterial IMPs.
The “positive-inside rule” is a critical determinant
of MP topology in bacteria. This principle states that positively
charged residues (lysine and arginine) are preferentially located
on the cytoplasmic side of the membrane, whereas the periplasmic loops
tend to have fewer positive charges.
,
 This charge
bias is likely due to two factors that may be related: the membrane
potential and interactions with the Sec translocon. Experimentally
inverting the charge distribution flips the topology of MPs, demonstrating
the strength of this determinant.
Helix–helix interactions within the membrane are mediated
by specific packing motifs. The GxxxG motif allows close helix–helix
contacts through glycine-mediated backbone interactions, while polar
residues facing away from the helical contact site can form interhelical
hydrogen bonds that stabilize oligomeric structures. Aromatic residues, particularly tryptophan and tyrosine,
preferentially localize to the membrane-water interface regions, serving
as ’anchors’ that position helices at the correct depth
in the membrane.
Lipid-protein interactions
play crucial roles beyond simple hydrophobic
matching. Anionic lipids such as PG and CL interact electrostatically
with positively charged residues on protein surfaces, influencing
both the localization and activity. The thickness and fluidity of
the membrane also impact protein folding, hydrophobic mismatch between
transmembrane helices, and bilayer thickness can drive conformational
changes or protein aggregation. In bacteria
that modulate membrane composition in response to temperature or pH
stress by producing unique lipid species, lipid-protein interactions
become particularly important for maintaining protein function.
2.1.2Overview and Structure of LipoproteinsBacterial lipoproteins are a unique class of membrane-associated
proteins that are anchored to either the periplasmic leaflet of IM
or both the periplasmic and extracellular leaflets of the OM via lipid
modifications (Figure
). Lipoproteins perform diverse cellular functions critical to pathogenesis,
including surface adhesion, signal transduction, and antibiotic resistance.
,
 Lipoproteins have a soluble domain that is anchored to the membrane
at the N-terminus via a lipid moiety. The sequences and structures
of the soluble domains of the mature lipoproteins are diverse. However,
all preprolipoproteins start with an N-terminal signal sequence that
is 10 to 25 residues long and ends with a consensus lipobox sequence,
LAGC, with some variation in the first, second, and third positions.
The signal sequence is cleaved (Figure
) and the mature lipoprotein is formed.2
Lipoprotein biogenesis
and localization in monoderm and diderm
bacteria. Lipoproteins are synthesized as pre-prolipoproteins
with N-terminal signal peptides that direct them to the Sec translocon
(SecYEG) for membrane insertion. Following translocation, the conserved
cysteine residue is lipidated by prolipoprotein diacylglyceryl transferase
(Lgt), adding a diacylglycerol (DAG) moiety. The signal peptide is
then cleaved by signal peptidase II (LspA/Lsp), generating an apolipoprotein.
In diderm bacteria (right panel), an additional N-acylation by apolipoprotein
N-acyltransferase (Lnt) produces triacylated lipoproteins (inset,
chemical structure shown) that are sorted to the OM via the Lol trafficking
system (LolABCDE). In monoderm bacteria (left panel), which lack an
OM, diacylated lipoproteins remain anchored in the cell membrane.
Organisms may contain different proteins that modify the apolipoprotein,
such as lipoprotein intramolecular transacylase (Lit) that catalyzes
intramolecular acyl transfer generating a lyso-lipoprotein. PE, phosphatidylethanolamine;
PG, phosphatidylglycerol. Created with BioRender.com.

acyl chains through an FASII pathway. Glycolipids, in which one or
more sugar residues replace the phosphate-containing headgroup, are
also present in many bacterial IMs and serve as the lipid retention
molecules in teichoic acid biosynthesis (Section
).

2.2Biogenesis of the IMThe assembly
of the IM requires coordinated biosynthesis and insertion of three
distinct molecular classes: lipids, IMPs, and lipoproteins. Peripheral
MPs are not covered in this Review due to the extensive scope. Lipids
are synthesized in the cytoplasm and incorporated into the bilayer,
establishing the physical foundations of the membrane. IMPs are inserted
co- or post-translationally through dedicated translocon machinery,
while lipoproteins undergo a conserved series of post-translational
modifications before being anchored to the IM (or trafficked to the
OM in diderm bacteria). Each of these processes is described in the
following subsections.2.2.1Lipid BiosynthesisThe bacterial
type II fatty acid synthesis (FASII) pathway elongates alkyl chains
through repeated cycles of condensation, reduction, and dehydration.
This extensive and complex biosynthetic pathway has been discussed
at length in various review articles; as such, this section will not
cover it in detail. Beyond the canonical
bacterial phospholipids such as phosphoethanolamine (PE), phosphatidylglycerol
(PG), and cardiolipin (CL), bacteria have evolved a remarkable range
of structural variations to adapt to their specific environments.
Certain species produce unusual lipids, such as ornithine lipids (OLs),
hopanoids (HOPs), glycolipids (GLs), and diacylglycerols (DAGs). The
GLs are hypothesized to be glycerophospholipid substitutes under phosphorus-limiting
conditions. OLs, while rare, contribute
to greater bilayer order and contain a negatively charged carboxyl
group rather than a phosphate group in glycerophospholipids. HOPs are structurally analogous to eukaryotic
sterols and are thought to perform similar roles in modulating membrane
fluidity and organization, including the possible formation of lipid
nanodomains. Several lipid classes once
considered exclusive to eukaryotes, such as ceramides, have been identified
in bacteria through emerging biochemical and bioinformatics research.
Thus, the lipid composition of bacteria has been an active area of
research.2.2.2IM Protein Biogenesis and FoldingIM proteins (IMPs) are integrated co-translationally via the Sec
translocon or post-translationally via the YidC insertase. YidC is an insertase that can function independently
or in cooperation with Sec translocon (also referred to as SecYEG).
Some small IMPs with simple topologies insert via YidC alone, bypassing
the Sec machinery entirely. YidC also
assists with the folding of larger IMPs by stabilizing nascent chains
and facilitating membrane integration. The mechanism of YidC-mediated
insertion involves a hydrophilic groove that shields the hydrophobic
transmembrane segments during membrane crossing. However, the Sec pathway is the primary route for most
of the IMPs. The Sec system mediates the translocation and insertion
of polypeptides in and across the IM and is the most conserved and
universally distributed protein export pathway in eukaryotes, prokaryotes,
and archaea. SecYEG is a heterotrimeric
membrane-embedded channel that forms a protein-conducting pore with
a central constriction and a lateral gate opening into the lipid bilayer,
allowing integration of hydrophobic segments into the membrane.
,,
 In addition to IMPs, Sec translocon
substrates include periplasmic enzymes, prematurely unfolded OMPs,
secreted enzymes, cell envelope assembly factors such as BamA and
LptD, and numerous other proteins, which all have an N-terminal signal
peptide that is typically cleaved after translocation.
,
 The Sec pathway was identified through genetic and biochemical studies
in the 1970s and 1980s, and its structural basis was elucidated at
high resolution by X-ray crystallography and cryo-electron microscopy.
These structures revealed multiple conformational states corresponding
to closed, primed, and actively translocating conformations.
,,,

Translocation through the Sec channel is driven by the coordinated
action of ATP hydrolysis and the proton motive force across the IM.
,
 A cytoplasmic peripheral ATPase (SecA) associates with the Sec complex
and drives translocation of the unfolded protein through the Sec channel.
,
 Unusually long stretches of hydrophobic signal sequences or transmembrane
domains are recognized by the signal recognition particle (SRP) as
they emerge from the ribosome, targeting the ribosome-nascent chain
complex directly to the SecYEG translocon at the membrane. Cycles of ATP binding and hydrolysis induce
conformational changes in the cytosolic ATPase that promote directional
insertion of successive polypeptide segments into the channel.
,,
 At the same time, the electrochemical
gradient across the membrane contributes to directional transport,
likely by biasing diffusion of the polypeptide toward the periplasm
and suppressing backward movement.
,
 The channel
itself exhibits pronounced conformational flexibility: a plug domain
occludes the pore in the resting state, the central constriction formed
by hydrophobic residues expands during translocation, and the lateral
gate opens to permit partitioning of transmembrane helices into the
bilayer.
,,,
 Translocation can also occur post-translationally
through the chaperone SecB, which binds newly synthesized preproteins
and stabilizes them in an unfolded state, preventing aggregation. SecB then shuttles and transfers the bound,
unfolded protein across the cytoplasm to the membrane-bound SecA channel
for further translocation. Despite extensive
structural and biochemical characterization of the Sec translocon,
several fundamental questions about its mechanism remain unresolved.
These include how the ATPase conformational cycles are coupled to
substrate movement, what are the relative contributions of power-stroke
versus Brownian ratchet models of translocation, and how is substrate
flux regulated under conditions of high demand.
,−









The substrates of the Sec pathway are initially unfolded at
the
start of translocation; however, some proteins need to be properly
folded in the cytoplasm and translocated across the IM. For these
proteins, an alternative translocation system was used. The Tat (twin-arginine
translocation) system is an additional protein insertion and export
pathway that mediates the insertion or translocation of fully folded
proteins across the IM.
−

 In contrast to the Sec system,
which transports unfolded substrates, the Tat pathway accommodates
proteins that have already acquired tertiary structure, often including
tightly bound cofactors, such as metal centers, complex prosthetic
groups, or assembled multimeric domains. The core Tat architecture comprises a membrane-embedded receptor
complex that recognizes N-terminal signal peptides bearing a conserved
twin-arginine motif, together with a dynamic oligomeric pore-forming
assembly recruited upon substrate engagement.
−

 These components
are distributed within the IM and undergo substrate-induced reorganization
to form a transient translocation conduit.
,,
 In diderm bacteria, translocated Tat substrates
are delivered to the periplasm, where they perform functions such
as redox catalysis, cofactor-dependent enzymatic reactions, cell envelope
biogenesis, and virulence-associated processes.
−

 The Tat pathway
was identified in the 1990s through genetic studies of protein export,
independent of the Sec system. Subsequent structural work using cryoelectron
microscopy, electron tomography, and biochemical reconstitution has
revealed that the system assembles into variable-sized oligomeric
complexes rather than a pre-formed, static channel.
−




The Tat system is driven exclusively by the
proton motive force
across the IM without direct coupling to ATP hydrolysis.
,,
 Substrate proteins bearing the
twin-arginine signal peptide are first recognized by the membrane
receptor complex, which triggers the recruitment and assembly of additional
membrane components into a transient, size-adaptive pore that is capable
of accommodating folded substrates of varying dimensions.
,,
 Current mechanistic models propose
that the proton gradient induces conformational changes and/or local
membrane perturbations that facilitate protein translocation, although
the precise coupling mechanism remains unresolved.
,
 A distinctive feature of the Tat pathway is its apparent ability
to preserve membrane integrity while translocating large, folded proteins,
suggesting that the translocation may be highly dynamic and involve
localized thinning or transient lipid-bilayer disruption.
−

 Despite significant progress in understanding the Tat translocation
mechanism, several key questions remain. These include how the system
discriminates between properly folded and misfolded substrates, how
pore size is regulated in response to substrate geometry, and how
proton motive force is mechanistically coupled to large-scale membrane
remodeling.
,−


Quality
control of the IM is vital in both monoderm and diderm
bacteria. The AAA+ protease FtsH recognizes and degrades misfolded
IMPs, preventing the accumulation of non-functional proteins. The
chaperone DnaK/DnaJ system also plays a role in maintaining IMP integrity,
particularly for proteins with large periplasmic or cytoplasmic domains. In addition to chaperones, phospholipids influence
the IMP folding and stability. Anionic lipids such as CL and PG cluster
around certain IMPs, stabilizing functional oligomers.
,
 The asymmetric distribution of lipids between the inner and outer
leaflets appears to differ between monoderm and diderm bacteria, suggesting
distinct lipid microdomains that may influence the protein topology
and function.
2.2.3IM Lipoprotein BiogenesisIn both
monoderm and diderm bacteria, lipoproteins are matured through a conserved
post-translational processing pathway (Figure
) that consists of a series of enzymes localized
to the IM. The biogenesis of all lipoproteins
(IM or OM) starts with the recognition of the N-terminal signal sequence
by either the Sec or the Tat translocation system, which directs the
nascent lipoprotein to the IM. Once at the membrane, diacylglyceryl
transferase (Lgt) cleaves a DAG moiety from an endogenous PG lipid
and covalently attaches it to a conserved cysteine residue within
the lipoprotein motif. Following this
diacylation, lipoprotein signal peptidase II (LspA) cleaves the N-terminal
signal peptide. Finally, a variable third
acyl modification occurs, which is dependent on the bacterial species
or a specific lipoprotein. This modification may involve N-acylation
by lipoprotein N-acyltransferase (Lnt) or transacylation by intramolecular
lipoprotein transferase (Lit). Upon completion
of this pathway, the mature lipoproteins are competent to perform
their cellular functions. Lgt and LspA are essential in diderm bacteria,
,
 and Lnt and Lit are not essential in certain bacteria. However,
for some bacteria, such as Helicobacter pylori
 and Streptococcus pneumoniae, Lnt is not essential for survival
but is essential for colonization.In monoderm bacteria, mature
lipoproteins either remain associated with the IM to perform their
functions or are secreted into the environment. In diderm bacteria,
many IM lipoproteins play roles in cell viability and overall maintenance,
including membrane homeostasis, envelope integrity, and stress-signaling
responses. Although these functions are
vital for cell survival, they are not directly involved in infection
pathways or pathogenesis. Lipoproteins in diderm bacteria can be transported
to the OM and is described in Section
.

Biogenesis of the IM

The assembly
of the IM requires coordinated biosynthesis and insertion of three
distinct molecular classes: lipids, IMPs, and lipoproteins. Peripheral
MPs are not covered in this Review due to the extensive scope. Lipids
are synthesized in the cytoplasm and incorporated into the bilayer,
establishing the physical foundations of the membrane. IMPs are inserted
co- or post-translationally through dedicated translocon machinery,
while lipoproteins undergo a conserved series of post-translational
modifications before being anchored to the IM (or trafficked to the
OM in diderm bacteria). Each of these processes is described in the
following subsections.

2.2.1Lipid BiosynthesisThe bacterial
type II fatty acid synthesis (FASII) pathway elongates alkyl chains
through repeated cycles of condensation, reduction, and dehydration.
This extensive and complex biosynthetic pathway has been discussed
at length in various review articles; as such, this section will not
cover it in detail. Beyond the canonical
bacterial phospholipids such as phosphoethanolamine (PE), phosphatidylglycerol
(PG), and cardiolipin (CL), bacteria have evolved a remarkable range
of structural variations to adapt to their specific environments.
Certain species produce unusual lipids, such as ornithine lipids (OLs),
hopanoids (HOPs), glycolipids (GLs), and diacylglycerols (DAGs). The
GLs are hypothesized to be glycerophospholipid substitutes under phosphorus-limiting
conditions. OLs, while rare, contribute
to greater bilayer order and contain a negatively charged carboxyl
group rather than a phosphate group in glycerophospholipids. HOPs are structurally analogous to eukaryotic
sterols and are thought to perform similar roles in modulating membrane
fluidity and organization, including the possible formation of lipid
nanodomains. Several lipid classes once
considered exclusive to eukaryotes, such as ceramides, have been identified
in bacteria through emerging biochemical and bioinformatics research.
Thus, the lipid composition of bacteria has been an active area of
research.

Lipid Biosynthesis

The bacterial
type II fatty acid synthesis (FASII) pathway elongates alkyl chains
through repeated cycles of condensation, reduction, and dehydration.
This extensive and complex biosynthetic pathway has been discussed
at length in various review articles; as such, this section will not
cover it in detail. Beyond the canonical
bacterial phospholipids such as phosphoethanolamine (PE), phosphatidylglycerol
(PG), and cardiolipin (CL), bacteria have evolved a remarkable range
of structural variations to adapt to their specific environments.
Certain species produce unusual lipids, such as ornithine lipids (OLs),
hopanoids (HOPs), glycolipids (GLs), and diacylglycerols (DAGs). The
GLs are hypothesized to be glycerophospholipid substitutes under phosphorus-limiting
conditions. OLs, while rare, contribute
to greater bilayer order and contain a negatively charged carboxyl
group rather than a phosphate group in glycerophospholipids. HOPs are structurally analogous to eukaryotic
sterols and are thought to perform similar roles in modulating membrane
fluidity and organization, including the possible formation of lipid
nanodomains. Several lipid classes once
considered exclusive to eukaryotes, such as ceramides, have been identified
in bacteria through emerging biochemical and bioinformatics research.
Thus, the lipid composition of bacteria has been an active area of
research.

2.2.2IM Protein Biogenesis and FoldingIM proteins (IMPs) are integrated co-translationally via the Sec
translocon or post-translationally via the YidC insertase. YidC is an insertase that can function independently
or in cooperation with Sec translocon (also referred to as SecYEG).
Some small IMPs with simple topologies insert via YidC alone, bypassing
the Sec machinery entirely. YidC also
assists with the folding of larger IMPs by stabilizing nascent chains
and facilitating membrane integration. The mechanism of YidC-mediated
insertion involves a hydrophilic groove that shields the hydrophobic
transmembrane segments during membrane crossing. However, the Sec pathway is the primary route for most
of the IMPs. The Sec system mediates the translocation and insertion
of polypeptides in and across the IM and is the most conserved and
universally distributed protein export pathway in eukaryotes, prokaryotes,
and archaea. SecYEG is a heterotrimeric
membrane-embedded channel that forms a protein-conducting pore with
a central constriction and a lateral gate opening into the lipid bilayer,
allowing integration of hydrophobic segments into the membrane.
,,
 In addition to IMPs, Sec translocon
substrates include periplasmic enzymes, prematurely unfolded OMPs,
secreted enzymes, cell envelope assembly factors such as BamA and
LptD, and numerous other proteins, which all have an N-terminal signal
peptide that is typically cleaved after translocation.
,
 The Sec pathway was identified through genetic and biochemical studies
in the 1970s and 1980s, and its structural basis was elucidated at
high resolution by X-ray crystallography and cryo-electron microscopy.
These structures revealed multiple conformational states corresponding
to closed, primed, and actively translocating conformations.
,,,

Translocation through the Sec channel is driven by the coordinated
action of ATP hydrolysis and the proton motive force across the IM.
,
 A cytoplasmic peripheral ATPase (SecA) associates with the Sec complex
and drives translocation of the unfolded protein through the Sec channel.
,
 Unusually long stretches of hydrophobic signal sequences or transmembrane
domains are recognized by the signal recognition particle (SRP) as
they emerge from the ribosome, targeting the ribosome-nascent chain
complex directly to the SecYEG translocon at the membrane. Cycles of ATP binding and hydrolysis induce
conformational changes in the cytosolic ATPase that promote directional
insertion of successive polypeptide segments into the channel.
,,
 At the same time, the electrochemical
gradient across the membrane contributes to directional transport,
likely by biasing diffusion of the polypeptide toward the periplasm
and suppressing backward movement.
,
 The channel
itself exhibits pronounced conformational flexibility: a plug domain
occludes the pore in the resting state, the central constriction formed
by hydrophobic residues expands during translocation, and the lateral
gate opens to permit partitioning of transmembrane helices into the
bilayer.
,,,
 Translocation can also occur post-translationally
through the chaperone SecB, which binds newly synthesized preproteins
and stabilizes them in an unfolded state, preventing aggregation. SecB then shuttles and transfers the bound,
unfolded protein across the cytoplasm to the membrane-bound SecA channel
for further translocation. Despite extensive
structural and biochemical characterization of the Sec translocon,
several fundamental questions about its mechanism remain unresolved.
These include how the ATPase conformational cycles are coupled to
substrate movement, what are the relative contributions of power-stroke
versus Brownian ratchet models of translocation, and how is substrate
flux regulated under conditions of high demand.
,−









The substrates of the Sec pathway are initially unfolded at
the
start of translocation; however, some proteins need to be properly
folded in the cytoplasm and translocated across the IM. For these
proteins, an alternative translocation system was used. The Tat (twin-arginine
translocation) system is an additional protein insertion and export
pathway that mediates the insertion or translocation of fully folded
proteins across the IM.
−

 In contrast to the Sec system,
which transports unfolded substrates, the Tat pathway accommodates
proteins that have already acquired tertiary structure, often including
tightly bound cofactors, such as metal centers, complex prosthetic
groups, or assembled multimeric domains. The core Tat architecture comprises a membrane-embedded receptor
complex that recognizes N-terminal signal peptides bearing a conserved
twin-arginine motif, together with a dynamic oligomeric pore-forming
assembly recruited upon substrate engagement.
−

 These components
are distributed within the IM and undergo substrate-induced reorganization
to form a transient translocation conduit.
,,
 In diderm bacteria, translocated Tat substrates
are delivered to the periplasm, where they perform functions such
as redox catalysis, cofactor-dependent enzymatic reactions, cell envelope
biogenesis, and virulence-associated processes.
−

 The Tat pathway
was identified in the 1990s through genetic studies of protein export,
independent of the Sec system. Subsequent structural work using cryoelectron
microscopy, electron tomography, and biochemical reconstitution has
revealed that the system assembles into variable-sized oligomeric
complexes rather than a pre-formed, static channel.
−




The Tat system is driven exclusively by the
proton motive force
across the IM without direct coupling to ATP hydrolysis.
,,
 Substrate proteins bearing the
twin-arginine signal peptide are first recognized by the membrane
receptor complex, which triggers the recruitment and assembly of additional
membrane components into a transient, size-adaptive pore that is capable
of accommodating folded substrates of varying dimensions.
,,
 Current mechanistic models propose
that the proton gradient induces conformational changes and/or local
membrane perturbations that facilitate protein translocation, although
the precise coupling mechanism remains unresolved.
,
 A distinctive feature of the Tat pathway is its apparent ability
to preserve membrane integrity while translocating large, folded proteins,
suggesting that the translocation may be highly dynamic and involve
localized thinning or transient lipid-bilayer disruption.
−

 Despite significant progress in understanding the Tat translocation
mechanism, several key questions remain. These include how the system
discriminates between properly folded and misfolded substrates, how
pore size is regulated in response to substrate geometry, and how
proton motive force is mechanistically coupled to large-scale membrane
remodeling.
,−


Quality
control of the IM is vital in both monoderm and diderm
bacteria. The AAA+ protease FtsH recognizes and degrades misfolded
IMPs, preventing the accumulation of non-functional proteins. The
chaperone DnaK/DnaJ system also plays a role in maintaining IMP integrity,
particularly for proteins with large periplasmic or cytoplasmic domains. In addition to chaperones, phospholipids influence
the IMP folding and stability. Anionic lipids such as CL and PG cluster
around certain IMPs, stabilizing functional oligomers.
,
 The asymmetric distribution of lipids between the inner and outer
leaflets appears to differ between monoderm and diderm bacteria, suggesting
distinct lipid microdomains that may influence the protein topology
and function.

diacylation, lipoprotein signal peptidase II (LspA) cleaves the N-terminal
