IM Lipoprotein Biogenesis

In both
monoderm and diderm bacteria, lipoproteins are matured through a conserved
post-translational processing pathway (Figure
) that consists of a series of enzymes localized
to the IM. The biogenesis of all lipoproteins
(IM or OM) starts with the recognition of the N-terminal signal sequence
by either the Sec or the Tat translocation system, which directs the
nascent lipoprotein to the IM. Once at the membrane, diacylglyceryl
transferase (Lgt) cleaves a DAG moiety from an endogenous PG lipid
and covalently attaches it to a conserved cysteine residue within
the lipoprotein motif. Following this
diacylation, lipoprotein signal peptidase II (LspA) cleaves the N-terminal
signal peptide. Finally, a variable third
acyl modification occurs, which is dependent on the bacterial species
or a specific lipoprotein. This modification may involve N-acylation
by lipoprotein N-acyltransferase (Lnt) or transacylation by intramolecular
lipoprotein transferase (Lit). Upon completion
of this pathway, the mature lipoproteins are competent to perform
their cellular functions. Lgt and LspA are essential in diderm bacteria,
,
 and Lnt and Lit are not essential in certain bacteria. However,
for some bacteria, such as Helicobacter pylori
 and Streptococcus pneumoniae, Lnt is not essential for survival
but is essential for colonization.

In monoderm bacteria, mature
lipoproteins either remain associated with the IM to perform their
functions or are secreted into the environment. In diderm bacteria,
many IM lipoproteins play roles in cell viability and overall maintenance,
including membrane homeostasis, envelope integrity, and stress-signaling
responses. Although these functions are
vital for cell survival, they are not directly involved in infection
pathways or pathogenesis. Lipoproteins in diderm bacteria can be transported
to the OM and is described in Section
.

2.3Role of IM in PathogenesisThe IM
interacts directly and indirectly with the host pathogens. In monoderm
bacteria, the IM components are more directly exposed to host immune
surveillance and can engage host receptors or modulate immune signaling.
In diderm bacteria, the IM is further shielded by the OM and its pathogenic
contributions are largely indirect. The IM provides the structural
foundation for translocation complexes, secretion systems, lipid trafficking,
and surface-display pathways that arm the bacterial exterior with
virulence factors. Across both architectures, the lipids, integral
proteins, and lipoproteins of the IM collectively shape the outcome
of infection through mechanisms ranging from direct immune activation
to support surface remodeling and toxin delivery.2.3.1IM Lipids in PathogenesisBeyond
proteins and lipoproteins, the lipids comprising the IM bilayer also
contribute significantly to pathogenesis. Bacteria use many lipid
moieties as carriers during the synthesis of surface-layer components
(e.g., PGs, teichoic acids, and polysaccharides). These surface layers
extend into the extracellular space and serve both as structural and
pathogenic functions. Pathogenic bacteria have evolved additional
mechanisms to adapt their membranes to host environments. For instance,
during infection, S. aureus acquires exogenous unsaturated
fatty acids from host cells and incorporates them into its membrane
lipids. These acquired fatty acids increase the production of branched-chain
fatty acids, which reduces lipoprotein-mediated toll-like receptors
(TLR)­2 activation. An example of membrane
reorganization in bacteria in response to antibiotic delivery after
infection is found in Enterococcus faecalis, which
utilizes exogenous oleic acid to increase resistance to the membrane-targeting
antibiotic daptomycin. Daptomycin, and
other membrane-disrupting antimicrobials such as amphotericin B or
polymyxin B, intercalate into microbial membranes, where the partitioning
of these molecules/peptides disrupts the stability of the cell membrane,
leading to cell death.
,
 Daptomycin is positively charged
and partitions into the membrane through interactions with negatively
charged phospholipid head groups, such as PG. Studies have shown that E. faecalis cells that are not readily resistant to daptomycin
scavenge oleic acid and incorporate it into their membranes, leading
to a downstream decrease in PG synthesis and conferring resistance
to the molecule. The obligate bacterium Chlamydia trachomatis relies on scavenging oleic acids from
host cells to form unsaturated fatty acids. Acyltransferase LpaT and acyl-ACP synthetase AasC enable the bacterium
to incorporate and synthesize phospholipids not endogenously produced
by the cell, thereby saving energy during infection for other biosynthetic
pathways.
2.3.2IM Proteins in PathogenesisThe
IM machinery is responsible for supporting PG synthesis, sorting,
and modifying lipoproteins and OMPs, supporting secretion systems,
assembling pili, and much more. Because of this central role, several
components are involved in the pathogenesis. Secretion systems (Section
), such as the
Type III secretion system (T3SS), span the entire cell envelope. A
portion of the complex is embedded in the IM and extends through the
periplasm and the OM to reach the extracellular space, where it secretes
proteins from the cell. Thus, the IM provides a supporting role in
infection by containing anchoring components of the secretion systems. Similarly, the IM supports host-cell interaction
by trafficking LPS to the OM, specifically via the LptFGC protein
complex, which accounts for half of the LPS trafficking complex. Although lipids and IMPs provide a more indirect
link to pathogenesis, some lipoproteins, particularly in monoderm
bacteria, play pathogenic roles.2.3.3IM Lipoproteins in PathogenesisMonoderm IM lipoproteins fulfill diverse roles in pathogenesis, including
the acquisition and transport of extracellular iron. Iron is essential for bacterial growth, yet its limited
bioavailability in the host necessitates specialized uptake systems
for a successful infection. In S. aureus, the number
of lipoprotein-mediated iron uptake systems correlates positively
with pathogenicity.
S. aureus encodes eight lipoproteins involved in iron acquisition and transport,
including the well-characterized proteins FhuD1 and FhuD2. These lipoproteins
bind iron­(III)-hydroxamate siderophores and transfer them to the adenosine
triphosphate binding cassette (ABC) transporter FhuCBG. Bacterial lipoproteins also bind to amino acids,
peptides, and lipids to assist in transport. Other bacterial lipoproteins
are critical to fitness and pathogenicity. For instance, PrsA is a
highly conserved surface lipoprotein found across many monoderm bacterial
species, including S. pneumoniae, B. subtilis, S. aureus, and others. PrsA, a parvulin-type peptidyl-prolyl cis/trans-isomerases (PPIase), catalyzes the structural rotation of proline
residues between cis and trans conformations
and is crucial for post-translational protein folding at the membrane. Although PrsA is highly conserved across monoderm
bacteria, the substrates differ across species. In group A Streptococcus, PrsA inactivation resulted in a significant
decrease in active exotoxin B (SpeB), as well decreased adherence
to host cells, biofilm formation, and virulence.
,
 Like PrsA, several other lipoproteins interact directly with host
proteins and cells.Some bacterial lipoproteins interact with
TLRs on the host cells and engage in cell invasion. Secreted lipoproteins
bind TLR2 through their lipid modification at the N-terminus and trigger
a cascading immune response from the host. A genomic cluster of lipoproteins in S. aureus contributes
to internalization of the bacterium into nonprofessional antigen-presenting
cells and is, thus, directly involved in bacterial virulence. Although there are numerous examples of lipoprotein
directly facilitating host interactions, there are still many lipoproteins
of unknown function that need to be investigated.2.3.4Outer-IM Vesicles in PathogenesisMercade et al. report in multiple publications that
outer-IM vesicles (O-IMVs) are secreted by various pathogenic diderm
bacteria such as Neisseria gonorrhoeae, Pseudomonas aeruginosa, and Acinetobacter baumanii. These O-IMVs contain periplasmic proteins, LPS, and other
OM lipids, cytoplasmic proteins, and other components such as DNA,
ATP, and IM proteins. Proteomic analysis
of O-IMVs from N. gonorrhoeae found that 13.7% of
the proteins were IM and 18% were cytoplasmic proteins. The thiol:disulfide interchange protein DsbD
and pyruvate dehydrogenase were found in the O-IMVs.
Shewanella vesiculosa M7T increases
in O-IMV secretion during the late exponential to early stationary
phase.
,
 In response to prophage-mediated explosive
cell lysis, single- and double-layer O-IMVs were observed.
,
 O-IMVs may facilitate the delivery and secretion of bacterial cytoplasmic
and IM components providing an additional mechanism of host interactions.

Role of IM in Pathogenesis

The IM
interacts directly and indirectly with the host pathogens. In monoderm
bacteria, the IM components are more directly exposed to host immune
surveillance and can engage host receptors or modulate immune signaling.
In diderm bacteria, the IM is further shielded by the OM and its pathogenic
contributions are largely indirect. The IM provides the structural
foundation for translocation complexes, secretion systems, lipid trafficking,
and surface-display pathways that arm the bacterial exterior with
virulence factors. Across both architectures, the lipids, integral
proteins, and lipoproteins of the IM collectively shape the outcome
of infection through mechanisms ranging from direct immune activation
to support surface remodeling and toxin delivery.

proteins and lipoproteins, the lipids comprising the IM bilayer also

moieties as carriers during the synthesis of surface-layer components

(TLR)­2 activation. An example of membrane
reorganization in bacteria in response to antibiotic delivery after
infection is found in Enterococcus faecalis, which
utilizes exogenous oleic acid to increase resistance to the membrane-targeting
antibiotic daptomycin. Daptomycin, and
other membrane-disrupting antimicrobials such as amphotericin B or
polymyxin B, intercalate into microbial membranes, where the partitioning
of these molecules/peptides disrupts the stability of the cell membrane,
leading to cell death.

,
 Daptomycin is positively charged
and partitions into the membrane through interactions with negatively
charged phospholipid head groups, such as PG. Studies have shown that E. faecalis cells that are not readily resistant to daptomycin
scavenge oleic acid and incorporate it into their membranes, leading
to a downstream decrease in PG synthesis and conferring resistance
to the molecule. The obligate bacterium Chlamydia trachomatis relies on scavenging oleic acids from
host cells to form unsaturated fatty acids. Acyltransferase LpaT and acyl-ACP synthetase AasC enable the bacterium
to incorporate and synthesize phospholipids not endogenously produced
by the cell, thereby saving energy during infection for other biosynthetic
pathways.

2.3.2IM Proteins in PathogenesisThe
IM machinery is responsible for supporting PG synthesis, sorting,
and modifying lipoproteins and OMPs, supporting secretion systems,
assembling pili, and much more. Because of this central role, several
components are involved in the pathogenesis. Secretion systems (Section
), such as the
Type III secretion system (T3SS), span the entire cell envelope. A
portion of the complex is embedded in the IM and extends through the
periplasm and the OM to reach the extracellular space, where it secretes
proteins from the cell. Thus, the IM provides a supporting role in
infection by containing anchoring components of the secretion systems. Similarly, the IM supports host-cell interaction
by trafficking LPS to the OM, specifically via the LptFGC protein
complex, which accounts for half of the LPS trafficking complex. Although lipids and IMPs provide a more indirect
link to pathogenesis, some lipoproteins, particularly in monoderm
bacteria, play pathogenic roles.

IM Proteins in Pathogenesis

The
IM machinery is responsible for supporting PG synthesis, sorting,
and modifying lipoproteins and OMPs, supporting secretion systems,
assembling pili, and much more. Because of this central role, several
components are involved in the pathogenesis. Secretion systems (Section
), such as the
Type III secretion system (T3SS), span the entire cell envelope. A
portion of the complex is embedded in the IM and extends through the
periplasm and the OM to reach the extracellular space, where it secretes
proteins from the cell. Thus, the IM provides a supporting role in
infection by containing anchoring components of the secretion systems. Similarly, the IM supports host-cell interaction
by trafficking LPS to the OM, specifically via the LptFGC protein
complex, which accounts for half of the LPS trafficking complex. Although lipids and IMPs provide a more indirect
link to pathogenesis, some lipoproteins, particularly in monoderm
bacteria, play pathogenic roles.

2.3.3IM Lipoproteins in PathogenesisMonoderm IM lipoproteins fulfill diverse roles in pathogenesis, including
the acquisition and transport of extracellular iron. Iron is essential for bacterial growth, yet its limited
bioavailability in the host necessitates specialized uptake systems
for a successful infection. In S. aureus, the number
of lipoprotein-mediated iron uptake systems correlates positively
with pathogenicity.
S. aureus encodes eight lipoproteins involved in iron acquisition and transport,
including the well-characterized proteins FhuD1 and FhuD2. These lipoproteins
bind iron­(III)-hydroxamate siderophores and transfer them to the adenosine
triphosphate binding cassette (ABC) transporter FhuCBG. Bacterial lipoproteins also bind to amino acids,
peptides, and lipids to assist in transport. Other bacterial lipoproteins
are critical to fitness and pathogenicity. For instance, PrsA is a
highly conserved surface lipoprotein found across many monoderm bacterial
species, including S. pneumoniae, B. subtilis, S. aureus, and others. PrsA, a parvulin-type peptidyl-prolyl cis/trans-isomerases (PPIase), catalyzes the structural rotation of proline
residues between cis and trans conformations
and is crucial for post-translational protein folding at the membrane. Although PrsA is highly conserved across monoderm
bacteria, the substrates differ across species. In group A Streptococcus, PrsA inactivation resulted in a significant
decrease in active exotoxin B (SpeB), as well decreased adherence
to host cells, biofilm formation, and virulence.
,
 Like PrsA, several other lipoproteins interact directly with host
proteins and cells.Some bacterial lipoproteins interact with
TLRs on the host cells and engage in cell invasion. Secreted lipoproteins
bind TLR2 through their lipid modification at the N-terminus and trigger
a cascading immune response from the host. A genomic cluster of lipoproteins in S. aureus contributes
to internalization of the bacterium into nonprofessional antigen-presenting
cells and is, thus, directly involved in bacterial virulence. Although there are numerous examples of lipoprotein
directly facilitating host interactions, there are still many lipoproteins
of unknown function that need to be investigated.

IM Lipoproteins in Pathogenesis

Monoderm IM lipoproteins fulfill diverse roles in pathogenesis, including
the acquisition and transport of extracellular iron. Iron is essential for bacterial growth, yet its limited
bioavailability in the host necessitates specialized uptake systems
for a successful infection. In S. aureus, the number
of lipoprotein-mediated iron uptake systems correlates positively
with pathogenicity.
S. aureus encodes eight lipoproteins involved in iron acquisition and transport,
including the well-characterized proteins FhuD1 and FhuD2. These lipoproteins
bind iron­(III)-hydroxamate siderophores and transfer them to the adenosine
triphosphate binding cassette (ABC) transporter FhuCBG. Bacterial lipoproteins also bind to amino acids,
peptides, and lipids to assist in transport. Other bacterial lipoproteins
are critical to fitness and pathogenicity. For instance, PrsA is a
highly conserved surface lipoprotein found across many monoderm bacterial
species, including S. pneumoniae, B. subtilis, S. aureus, and others. PrsA, a parvulin-type peptidyl-prolyl cis/trans-isomerases (PPIase), catalyzes the structural rotation of proline
residues between cis and trans conformations
and is crucial for post-translational protein folding at the membrane. Although PrsA is highly conserved across monoderm
bacteria, the substrates differ across species. In group A Streptococcus, PrsA inactivation resulted in a significant
decrease in active exotoxin B (SpeB), as well decreased adherence
to host cells, biofilm formation, and virulence.
,
 Like PrsA, several other lipoproteins interact directly with host
proteins and cells.

Some bacterial lipoproteins interact with
TLRs on the host cells and engage in cell invasion. Secreted lipoproteins
bind TLR2 through their lipid modification at the N-terminus and trigger
a cascading immune response from the host. A genomic cluster of lipoproteins in S. aureus contributes
to internalization of the bacterium into nonprofessional antigen-presenting
cells and is, thus, directly involved in bacterial virulence. Although there are numerous examples of lipoprotein
directly facilitating host interactions, there are still many lipoproteins
of unknown function that need to be investigated.

2.3.4Outer-IM Vesicles in PathogenesisMercade et al. report in multiple publications that
outer-IM vesicles (O-IMVs) are secreted by various pathogenic diderm
bacteria such as Neisseria gonorrhoeae, Pseudomonas aeruginosa, and Acinetobacter baumanii. These O-IMVs contain periplasmic proteins, LPS, and other
OM lipids, cytoplasmic proteins, and other components such as DNA,
ATP, and IM proteins. Proteomic analysis
of O-IMVs from N. gonorrhoeae found that 13.7% of
the proteins were IM and 18% were cytoplasmic proteins. The thiol:disulfide interchange protein DsbD
and pyruvate dehydrogenase were found in the O-IMVs.
Shewanella vesiculosa M7T increases
in O-IMV secretion during the late exponential to early stationary
phase.
,
 In response to prophage-mediated explosive
cell lysis, single- and double-layer O-IMVs were observed.
,
 O-IMVs may facilitate the delivery and secretion of bacterial cytoplasmic
and IM components providing an additional mechanism of host interactions.

the proteins were IM and 18% were cytoplasmic proteins. The thiol:disulfide interchange protein DsbD
and pyruvate dehydrogenase were found in the O-IMVs.

in O-IMV secretion during the late exponential to early stationary
phase.

2.4IM Therapeutic Targets2.4.1IM Antibiotic TargetsThe bacterial
IM houses several essential biosynthetic and assembly machineries
that are either absent from or sufficiently divergent in eukaryotic
cells to permit selective therapeutic intervention. Many IM components
and processes are thus promising antibiotic targets, with inhibition
often resulting in bactericidal activity due to downstream effects
on cell envelope integrity. The bacterial type II fatty acid synthesis
system, a set of discrete monofunctional enzymes distinct from the
mammalian fatty acid synthesis pathway, is an antibiotic target. FabI,
the enoyl-acyl carrier protein reductase, catalyzes the final reductive
step of the fatty acid elongation cycle and is the most advanced target
in this pathway. Triclosan and isoniazid established proof of concept
for FabI inhibition. Afabicin (Debio 1450), a prodrug targeting staphylococcal
FabI, completed a Phase 2 clinical trial for acute bacterial skin
and skin structure infections caused by staphylococci. CG400549, another FabI inhibitor,
is also in clinical development for staphylococcal infections. The mycobacterial FabI homolog, InhA, is inhibited
by isoniazid. However, isoniazid is a prodrug that requires activation
by a catalase-peroxidase. InhA inhibitors that bypass this activation
step are under development to address resistance arising from catalase-peroxidase
mutations. FabH, the β-ketoacyl-ACP
synthase III that initiates each elongation cycle, represents an underexploited
but promising target with diverse inhibitor scaffolds reported in
preclinical studies.
The lipoprotein
processing enzyme LspA (Figure
), is essential for survival in diderm bacteria and is inhibited
by the natural cyclic depsipeptides globomycin and myxovirescin. Crystal
structures of methicillin-resistant S. aureus (MRSA)
LspA bound to both compounds revealed provided a structural framework
for inhibitor design. Computationally
designed cyclic peptide analogues of globomycin with improved stability
exhibit activity against a group of highly virulent, antibiotic-resistant
bacteria responsible for most global hospital-acquired infections.
In mycobacteria, the IM MmpL3 exports
trehalose monomycolate across
the IM as an obligate step in mycolic acid cell wall biosynthesis
(see Section
) and
is the target of multiple structurally diverse compound classes. SQ109,
an ethambutol analog and MmpL3 inhibitor, has completed Phase 2b/3 clinical trials for MDR pulmonary tuberculosis and shows
synergistic activity with front-line agents including isoniazid, rifampicin,
and bedaquiline.
2.4.2IM Vaccine TargetsThe IM is the
most occluded bacterial surface from the extracellular space and does
not present antigens readily recognized by the host immune system,
making it a poor source of vaccine candidates compared with outer-surface
components. Despite this limitation, IM lipoproteins can achieve a
surface exposure. The iron-acquisition lipoprotein FhuD2 of S. aureus similarly achieves surface exposure despite IM
anchoring and has been developed as a vaccine antigen. FhuD2 is included
in the multicomponent 4C-Staph formulation, alongside the conserved
lipoprotein Csa1A and secreted virulence factors. This formulation
showed protection across multiple murine infection models. Beyond their roles as direct antigens, the lipid
modifications added to lipoproteins at the IM by Lgt confer adjuvant
activity that stimulates TLR2. This stimulatory property is exploited
to enhance the immunogenicity of recombinant vaccine antigens by engineering
the lipoprotein signal peptide and lipobox into heterologous proteins.

The IM is the
most occluded bacterial surface from the extracellular space and does
not present antigens readily recognized by the host immune system,
making it a poor source of vaccine candidates compared with outer-surface
components. Despite this limitation, IM lipoproteins can achieve a
surface exposure. The iron-acquisition lipoprotein FhuD2 of S. aureus similarly achieves surface exposure despite IM
anchoring and has been developed as a vaccine antigen. FhuD2 is included

Overview of the Components and Organization
of the PG Layer

PG is a complex, unique essential macromolecule
of bacterial cells
comprised of alternating N-acetylglucosamine (NAG)
and N-acetylmuramic acid (NAM), with short oligopeptides
attached to the NAM sugar (Figure
).
,
 The peptide chain can then be
crosslinked to other peptide chains, forming the full structure of
the PG layer. The PG layer is synthesized, cross-linked, and remodeled
by glycosyltransferases, transpeptidases, and hydrolases (Figure
).

PG can be covalently modified by proteins carrying
C-terminal sorting
sequences, such as LPXTG and NPQTN (Figure
). There are six families of sortase enzymes
(SrtA–F), each classified based on primary sequence. Sortase
A, the prototype of class A, catalyzes the covalent attachment of
LPXTG-bearing surface proteins to the PG (Figure
). Substrate proteins are first exported
across the cytoplasmic membrane by the Sec machinery, then folded
by an as-yet uncharacterized post-translocational mechanism and retained
at the membrane via their C-terminal cell wall sorting signal (CWSS).
Sortase A cleaves the LPXTG motif between threonine and glycine residues, forming a thioester-linked acyl-enzyme intermediate.
The substrate protein is then transferred to lipid II through a nucleophilic
attack from the amino group of the pentaglycine crossbridge on Lipid
II. Transpeptidases and glycosyltransferases
subsequently incorporate the Lipid II-linked protein into the mature
PG network. The role of sortase enzymes in pilus polymerization and
cell wall attachment during pili biogenesis is discussed in Section
.

3.1PG BiogenesisPG biosynthesis (Figure
) begins in the cytoplasm,
where the precursor UDP-N-acetylmuramic acid (UDP-MurNAc) is assembled
from UDP-N-acetylglucosamine (UDP-GlcNAc) through the sequential action
of MurA and MurB, which is then built into a pentapeptide stem by
MurC, MurD, MurE, and MurF.
,
 MraY and MurG convert
this product into lipid II, where flippases such as MurJ flip the
lipid II across the IM to the periplasmic face.
−

 Glycosyltransferases polymerize the glycan strands and penicillin
binding proteins (PBPs) crosslink the peptide stems to a form a mesh-like
network which is continuously remodeled throughout the bacterial cell
cycle to accommodate cell elongation, division, and shape maintenance. In diderms, LD-transpeptidases (LD-TPases)
form additional crosslinks and covalently attach the lipoprotein Lpp
to the OM in E. coli. Additionally, the PG can be
non-covalently modified with OMPs, such as OmpA, which further stabilizes
the cell wall.
While the core
biosynthetic machinery is broadly conserved across bacteria, the architecture
and composition of the final PG layer differ considerably between
monoderm and diderm bacteria. Monoderm bacteria, such as B.
subtilis and S. aureus, possess a thick,
multilayered PG wall that ranges from 30 to 100 nm in thickness and
is often heavily crosslinked.
,
 This robust barrier
is further decorated with accessory polymers such as wall teichoic
acids, which are covalently attached to PG and contribute to cell
wall rigidity, surface charge, and ion homeostasis.
,
 Teichoic acids are discussed in Section
. In diderm bacteria, the PG layer is significantly
thinner than in monoderm bacteria, measuring roughly 2 to 7 nm and
located within the periplasmic space.
,
 The periplasmic
space spans approximately 13–25 nm in width in E. coli and is not a fixed compartment; cryo-electron microscopy studies
show it behaves as a compressible gel whose width varies with osmotic
conditions. In diderm bacteria, the
PG layer must be physically anchored to the OM to maintain envelope
integrity, a function carried out by a subset of OM lipoproteins and
OMPs.
,
 The degree of peptide crosslinking is less
in diderm bacteria compared to their monoderm counterparts. The composition
of PG differs as well, with meso-Dap acid found in
most diderm species and l-lysine more common in monoderm
bacteria. These chemical and structural
differences have important implications for antibiotic targeting,
immune recognition, and the overall mechanical properties of the bacterial
cell envelope.

3.2PG in PathogenesisThe PG layer is
a biological stimulant in host–pathogen interactions, leading
to cytotoxicity and activation of macrophages. The innate immune system
recognizes muropeptides as PG fragments. Muropeptides are recognized
by host cell receptors nucleotide-binding oligomerization domains
(NOD)­1 and NOD2.
,
 NOD1 and NOD2 act as an immune
alarm system by triggering rapid inflammatory and antimicrobial responses.
NOD1 specifically recognizes a meso-Dap-containing
muropeptide found exclusively in diderm bacterial PG, while NOD2 recognizes
muramyl dipeptide (MDP), a fragment present in both monoderm and diderm
bacterial PGs.
Legionella pneumophila has adapted to this immune response by modifying its PG with PgdA,
a deacetylase that converts N-acetylglucosamine (NAG) in PG to glucosamine. This PG modification reduces cleavage of PG
by lysozymes, decreases murapeptide release, and thus evades NOD receptor
recognition.
Internalin, InlA,
in L. monocytogenes, is a PG-bound surface protein
that interacts with and binds host E-cadherin molecules, which contributes
to adhesion and internalization of the bacteria. Another family of cell wall PG-anchored virulence factors
is microbial surface components recognizing adhesive matrix Molecules
(MSCRAMM), SdrF, a S. epidermis MSCRAMM, participates in the initial adhesion to host cells by binding
type 1 collagen and keratins. Other
MSCRAMMs bind to fibronectin in host cells, promoting cell adhesion
and facilitating infection.

recognition.

MSCRAMMs bind to fibronectin in host cells, promoting cell adhesion
and facilitating infection.

3.3PG Therapeutic Targets3.3.1PG Antibiotic TargetsPG is the
most structurally and therapeutically exploited component of the bacterial
cell envelope. PG has the most abundant antibiotic targets identified
to date because of its role in maintaining cellular integrity across
virtually all bacteria, its absence from eukaryotic cells, and the
accessibility of its biosynthetic pathway at multiple chemically distinct
steps. Therapeutics targeting each biosynthetic
step have yielded clinically important antibiotics, and several are
under active development.
,

The extracellular
cross-linking step catalyzed by PBPs is one of the oldest known antibiotic
targets. β-Lactam antibiotics (e.g., penicillins, cephalosporins,
carbapenems, and monobactams) inactivate PBPs by covalently mimicking
the d-Ala-d-Ala terminus of the peptide stem, blocking
transpeptidation. Resistance driven
by the acquisition of PBP2a in MRSA is the paradigmatic clinical problem:
PBP2a has low β-lactam affinity and requires allosteric activation
by cell wall fragments at a site distal to the active site. Current
strategies focus on small-molecule adjuvants that bind this allosteric
site to restore β-lactam activity. Glycopeptide antibiotics target the lipid II pentapeptide and thereby
block its incorporation into PG. The lipoglycopeptides dalbavancin,
oritavancin, and telavancin extend this mechanism with additional
membrane-anchoring moieties that improve activity against vancomycin-resistant
strains. Lipid II itself has emerged
as a particularly attractive target because it is the single essential
convergence point of the PG biosynthetic pathway. Clovibactin, isolated
from an uncultured soil bacterium, kills drug-resistant Gram-positive
pathogens without detectable resistance by binding the pyrophosphate
moiety common to multiple lipid-anchored PG precursors (lipid II,
lipid III, and C55-PP). At the membrane-associated
biosynthetic steps, the phospho-MurNAc-pentapeptide translocase (MraY)
that generates lipid I has no commercially available inhibitor despite
being essential in all bacteria. Natural product nucleoside antibiotics
including caprazamycins, muraymycins, and riburamycins inhibit MraY
with broad-spectrum activity and low toxicity in preclinical studies;
however, incomplete mechanistic understanding of inhibitor binding
continues to hinder clinical translation. PG hydrolases, which remodel PG during growth and division, represent
a recently recognized target class. Small-molecule inhibitors of these
enzymes are in early development and show synergistic activity with
existing antibiotics. The broader preclinical
pipeline includes inhibitors of the cytoplasmic ligases as well as
the glucosamine-1-phosphate acetyltransferase/uridyltransferase that
synthesize the UDP-GlcNAc substrate required in PG and LPS biosynthesis.
3.3.2PG Vaccine TargetsPG is surface-accessible
and therefore immunologically visible, yet it has not yielded direct
vaccine antigens in a conventional sense. Unfortunately, the NOD and
murapeptide interactions are too non-specific to develop an adaptive
vaccine response, but MDP, the minimal NOD2-active PG fragment, has
a long history as a vaccine adjuvant. MDP stimulates cytokine production
and enhances adaptive immune responses to co-administered antigens. Clinical application of native MDP has been
limited by pyrogenicity, rapid elimination, and poor oral bioavailability;
extensive structure-activity relationship studies have produced synthetic
analogues with reduced pyrogenicity and improved pharmacokinetic profiles,
and several MDP-based adjuvants are under investigation for use with
bacterial and viral vaccine antigens.
,
 PG itself
is not a direct vaccine antigen because the PG backbone is highly
conserved across both pathogenic and commensal bacteria and because
the adaptive immune response to PG is predominantly innate and T-cell-independent.

PG Therapeutic Targets

3.3.1PG Antibiotic TargetsPG is the
most structurally and therapeutically exploited component of the bacterial
cell envelope. PG has the most abundant antibiotic targets identified
to date because of its role in maintaining cellular integrity across
virtually all bacteria, its absence from eukaryotic cells, and the
accessibility of its biosynthetic pathway at multiple chemically distinct
steps. Therapeutics targeting each biosynthetic
step have yielded clinically important antibiotics, and several are
under active development.
,

The extracellular
cross-linking step catalyzed by PBPs is one of the oldest known antibiotic
targets. β-Lactam antibiotics (e.g., penicillins, cephalosporins,
carbapenems, and monobactams) inactivate PBPs by covalently mimicking
the d-Ala-d-Ala terminus of the peptide stem, blocking
transpeptidation. Resistance driven
by the acquisition of PBP2a in MRSA is the paradigmatic clinical problem:
PBP2a has low β-lactam affinity and requires allosteric activation
by cell wall fragments at a site distal to the active site. Current
strategies focus on small-molecule adjuvants that bind this allosteric
site to restore β-lactam activity. Glycopeptide antibiotics target the lipid II pentapeptide and thereby
block its incorporation into PG. The lipoglycopeptides dalbavancin,
oritavancin, and telavancin extend this mechanism with additional
membrane-anchoring moieties that improve activity against vancomycin-resistant
strains. Lipid II itself has emerged
as a particularly attractive target because it is the single essential
convergence point of the PG biosynthetic pathway. Clovibactin, isolated
from an uncultured soil bacterium, kills drug-resistant Gram-positive
pathogens without detectable resistance by binding the pyrophosphate
moiety common to multiple lipid-anchored PG precursors (lipid II,
lipid III, and C55-PP). At the membrane-associated
biosynthetic steps, the phospho-MurNAc-pentapeptide translocase (MraY)
that generates lipid I has no commercially available inhibitor despite
being essential in all bacteria. Natural product nucleoside antibiotics
including caprazamycins, muraymycins, and riburamycins inhibit MraY
with broad-spectrum activity and low toxicity in preclinical studies;
however, incomplete mechanistic understanding of inhibitor binding
continues to hinder clinical translation. PG hydrolases, which remodel PG during growth and division, represent
a recently recognized target class. Small-molecule inhibitors of these
enzymes are in early development and show synergistic activity with
existing antibiotics. The broader preclinical
pipeline includes inhibitors of the cytoplasmic ligases as well as
the glucosamine-1-phosphate acetyltransferase/uridyltransferase that
synthesize the UDP-GlcNAc substrate required in PG and LPS biosynthesis.

PG Antibiotic Targets

PG is the
most structurally and therapeutically exploited component of the bacterial
cell envelope. PG has the most abundant antibiotic targets identified
to date because of its role in maintaining cellular integrity across
virtually all bacteria, its absence from eukaryotic cells, and the
accessibility of its biosynthetic pathway at multiple chemically distinct
steps. Therapeutics targeting each biosynthetic
step have yielded clinically important antibiotics, and several are
under active development.
,

The extracellular
cross-linking step catalyzed by PBPs is one of the oldest known antibiotic
targets. β-Lactam antibiotics (e.g., penicillins, cephalosporins,
carbapenems, and monobactams) inactivate PBPs by covalently mimicking
the d-Ala-d-Ala terminus of the peptide stem, blocking
transpeptidation. Resistance driven
by the acquisition of PBP2a in MRSA is the paradigmatic clinical problem:
PBP2a has low β-lactam affinity and requires allosteric activation
by cell wall fragments at a site distal to the active site. Current
strategies focus on small-molecule adjuvants that bind this allosteric
site to restore β-lactam activity. Glycopeptide antibiotics target the lipid II pentapeptide and thereby
block its incorporation into PG. The lipoglycopeptides dalbavancin,

strains. Lipid II itself has emerged
as a particularly attractive target because it is the single essential
convergence point of the PG biosynthetic pathway. Clovibactin, isolated
from an uncultured soil bacterium, kills drug-resistant Gram-positive
pathogens without detectable resistance by binding the pyrophosphate
moiety common to multiple lipid-anchored PG precursors (lipid II,

biosynthetic steps, the phospho-MurNAc-pentapeptide translocase (MraY)
that generates lipid I has no commercially available inhibitor despite

including caprazamycins, muraymycins, and riburamycins inhibit MraY

however, incomplete mechanistic understanding of inhibitor binding

a recently recognized target class. Small-molecule inhibitors of these
enzymes are in early development and show synergistic activity with

pipeline includes inhibitors of the cytoplasmic ligases as well as

3.3.2PG Vaccine TargetsPG is surface-accessible
and therefore immunologically visible, yet it has not yielded direct
vaccine antigens in a conventional sense. Unfortunately, the NOD and
murapeptide interactions are too non-specific to develop an adaptive
vaccine response, but MDP, the minimal NOD2-active PG fragment, has
a long history as a vaccine adjuvant. MDP stimulates cytokine production
and enhances adaptive immune responses to co-administered antigens. Clinical application of native MDP has been
limited by pyrogenicity, rapid elimination, and poor oral bioavailability;
extensive structure-activity relationship studies have produced synthetic
analogues with reduced pyrogenicity and improved pharmacokinetic profiles,
and several MDP-based adjuvants are under investigation for use with
bacterial and viral vaccine antigens.
,
 PG itself
is not a direct vaccine antigen because the PG backbone is highly
conserved across both pathogenic and commensal bacteria and because
the adaptive immune response to PG is predominantly innate and T-cell-independent.

PG Vaccine Targets

PG is surface-accessible
and therefore immunologically visible, yet it has not yielded direct
vaccine antigens in a conventional sense. Unfortunately, the NOD and
murapeptide interactions are too non-specific to develop an adaptive
vaccine response, but MDP, the minimal NOD2-active PG fragment, has
a long history as a vaccine adjuvant. MDP stimulates cytokine production
and enhances adaptive immune responses to co-administered antigens. Clinical application of native MDP has been
limited by pyrogenicity, rapid elimination, and poor oral bioavailability;
extensive structure-activity relationship studies have produced synthetic
analogues with reduced pyrogenicity and improved pharmacokinetic profiles,
and several MDP-based adjuvants are under investigation for use with
bacterial and viral vaccine antigens.
,
 PG itself
is not a direct vaccine antigen because the PG backbone is highly
conserved across both pathogenic and commensal bacteria and because
the adaptive immune response to PG is predominantly innate and T-cell-independent.

4Overview of the Structure and Organization of
Teichoic AcidsMonoderm PG layers can be modified with glycolipid
polymers known
as teichoic acids (TAs; Figure
) that extend outward from the cell. TAs can be anchored to
the cell membrane as lipoteichoic acids (LTA) or covalently linked
to the PG as wall teichoic acids (WTA) (Figure
). Both LTAs and WTAs are unique to monoderm
bacteria and have roles in biofilm formation and bacterial adhesion
as well as controlling cell shape and cell division. LTA has polymerized glycerol phosphate repeat units and
is covalently attached to PG. WTAs have a disaccharide base, covalently
attached to PG, and a long polymer of sugar phosphate units (e.g.,
glycerol phosphate or ribitol phosphate) linked through a phosphodiester
bond. WTAs are anchored to the outer leaflet of the IM through a covalent
linkage to a glycolipid. TA chemical modifications identified include
acylation (e.g., d-alanine), esterification (of the d-alanine modifications), choline, and various sugar moieties, such
as GlcNAc.
,
 TA protein modification occurs
when choline-binding proteins (CBPs) bind to TAs, decorating the bacterium’s
surface. CBPs are a diverse protein family that non-covalently bind
to phosphorylcholine moieties via a choline binding domain found on
both lipoteichoic and wall teichoic acids.
5
Biosynthesis pathways of wall teichoic acid (WTA) and lipoteichoic
acid (LTA) in
S. aureus
. WTA
biosynthesis (left) is initiated in the cytoplasm by TarO, which transfers
N-acetylglucosamine-1-phosphate from UDP-GlcNAc onto the lipid carrier
undecaprenyl phosphate, releasing UMP. TarA adds N-acetylmannosamine
(ManNAc) from UDP-ManNAc, and TarB transfers the first CDP-glycerol
unit. TarF extends the linkage unit, and TarL polymerizes the ribitol-phosphate
backbone from CDP-ribitol. The nascent WTA polymer is modified by
TarM (α-GlcNAc glycosylation) and TarS (β-GlcNAc glycosylation)
from UDP-GlcNAc in the cytoplasm, before the ABC transporter complex
TarGH flips the completed polymer across the IM. TarTUV translocate
the mature WTA to the extracellular face of the PG layer for covalent
attachment to MurNAc residues. LTA biosynthesis (right) occurs at
the outer leaflet of the IM. YpfP synthesizes the diglucosyl-diacylglycerol
(Glc2-DAG) glycolipid anchor from UDP-glucose. LtaA flips
the glycolipid anchor to the outer leaflet. LtaS enzymes then polymerize
glycerophosphate (GroP) units derived from phosphatidylglycerol onto
the glycolipid anchor, extending the LTA chain at the membrane surface.
Both WTA and LTA are modified by d-alanylation through the
Dlt pathway: transferring d-alanine to the TA backbone to
reduce net negative surface charge and modulate interactions with
cationic antimicrobial peptides. Molecular components are color-coded
as indicated in the legend. Created in https://BioRender.com.TAs are critical to the basic cellular architecture
and division.
In S. aureus, TAs direct the positioning of the cell
wall synthesis machinery. LTAs in S. aureus help regulate autolytic enzyme activity, preventing
excessive self-digestion of the cell wall. In S. aureus and L. monocytogenes, WTAs contribute to cell morphology, and their loss results in severe
defects in cell shape and division. In B. subtilis, CBPs modulate the activity of autolysins, the
enzymes responsible for controlled cell wall turnover.
4.1Biosynthesis of TAsThe biosynthesis
of teichoic acids (Figure
) in monoderm bacteria involves two distinct pathways for
WTAs and LTAs, each requiring a dedicated set of enzymatic machinery.
The synthesis of WTAs in S. aureus proceeds through
two major stages that take place on opposite sides of the cytoplasmic
membrane. The process begins in the cytoplasm, where a N-acetylglucosamine-1-phosphate
transferase (TarO in S. aureus) transfers N-acetylglucosamine-1-phosphate
onto the lipid carrier undecaprenyl phosphate, forming a membrane-anchored
intermediate from which the polymer is subsequently assembled. TarA and TarB add sugar and ribitol-phosphate
units to build the polymer, and the completed chain is exported by
the TarGH ABC transporter.
,
 Once on the exterior
face of the membrane, the WTA polymer is covalently attached to the
PG layer through the action of LytR-CpsA-Psr (LCP) (TarTUV), which
transfers the completed polymer onto muramic acid residues within
the cell wall.
The biosynthesis
of LTAs occurs on the outer leaflet of the cytoplasmic membrane. In S. aureus and B. subtilis, the enzyme LtaS
polymerizes glycerol-phosphate units derived from PG directly onto
a glycolipid anchor. In order for LTA
polymers to be modified with GlcNAc (or galactose in some species),
GlcNAc must be transported to the periplasm. A set of three proteins
(a cytoplasmic glycotransferase, a flippase, and a periplasmic glycotransferase)
is required.
,
 The cytoplasmic glycotransferase
utilizes UDP-galactose to form a C55–P-galactose,
which is transported across the membrane by the flippase. Once in
the periplasm, the periplasmic glycotransferase transfers GlcNac to
the LTA backbone. This extracellular glycosylation mechanism may also
modify the WTA.
Glycosyltransferases
add further modifications to the teichoic
acid backbone, such as the incorporation of d-alanine through
the Dlt pathway, esterification, and the attachment of glycosyl groups.
The Dlt pathway, which is widespread among pathogens including S. aureus, L. monocytogenes, and Streptococcus pyogenes, is well characterized.
,
 DltA adenylates free d-alanine and loads it onto DltC as
a thioester, DltB then transfers it to a tyrosine on DltX, and DltD
finally transfers the d-alanine onto the LTA.
−



4.2TAs in PathogenesisIn S.
aureus, wall teichoic acids facilitate nasal colonization
by interacting with host ligands present on nasal epithelial cells,
and animal models in which WTA biosynthesis has been genetically disrupted
show dramatically reduced colonization efficiency. WTAs interact with the scavenger receptor SREC-I expressed
on keratinocytes and contributes to staphylococcal skin colonization.
Given that nasal and skin carriage of S. aureus is
a well-established risk factor for invasive infection, this adhesion
function carries considerable clinical significance. The glycotransferase GtcA in L. monocytogenes adds galactose residues to WTAs. Strains lacking this modification
exhibit reduced virulence in animal models, indicating that the glycosylation
modification contributes to pathogenicity.
,
 In Staphylococcus epidermidis, LTAs contribute
to resistance against human β-defensins produced by skin epithelial
cells and increase bacterial survival. In Enterococcus faecium and E. faecalis, modifications to LTA structure influence the degree to which these
molecules stimulate TLR2-mediated inflammatory signaling, with more
heavily modified LTAs sometimes evading robust immune detection and
allowing the bacteria to persist in host tissues with reduced inflammatory
clearance.
,
 Teichoic acids also play important
roles in modulating complement activation, which is a central pillar
of the innate immune response against bacterial pathogens. CBPs (PspA
and PspC) in S. pneuomoniae lyse human red blood
cells during infection. Another CBP
in S. pneuomoniae, CbpC, plays a pivotal role in
dampening host autophagy during infection, promoting bacterial cell
survival. Finally, CbpG in S.
pneuomoniae is an adherence factor that also cleaves the
host extracellular matrix after secretion.
In response to antimicrobial peptides, bacteria modifiy TAs. d-Alanylation reduces the overall negative charge of the bacterial
surface, thereby diminishing the electrostatic attraction between
the cell wall and cationic antimicrobial peptides such as defensins
and cathelicidins. Thus, d-alanylation provides the bacterium
with resistance to a front-line component of the host innate immune
system.
,
 This antimicrobial resistance can be seen
in group A Streptococcus, where the loss of the d-alanylation-responsible enzyme DltA results in increased net
negative charge at the cell surface and higher susceptibility to antimicrobial
peptides. Finally, d-alanylation
of TAs plays a supplementary role in lipoprotein binding to TLR2 during
host–pathogen interaction, where the loss of these d-alanylated teichoic acids contributes to a loss in lipoprotein binding.
4.3TA Therapeutic Targets4.3.1TA Antibiotic TargetsTeichoic
acids and their associated CBPs represent therapeutically attractive
targets because of their surface accessibility, essential structural
and pathogenic roles, and the absence in mammalian cells. The WTA
biosynthetic pathway is particularly rich in potential drug targets
because it is dispensable under standard laboratory conditions, yet
essential during infection. This distinction implies that WTA pathway
inhibitors cause minimal on-target toxicity to commensal bacteria
while targeting MRSA and other antibiotic-resistant pathogens.
The WTA biosynthetic pathway has generated
sustained interest as an antibiotic adjuvant target, particularly
against MRSA. Genetic or pharmacological inactivation of TarO does
not kill bacteria outright but abolishes WTA production. Loss of WTA
prevents the proper localization of PBP4, the enzyme responsible for
highly cross-linked PG. The reduction in crosslinks increases the
permeability of antibiotics and resensitizes MRSA to β-lactam
antibiotics that would otherwise be ineffective. Multiple small-molecule TarO inhibitors have been identified,
including ticlopidine and the more potent compound tunicamycin. Natural
products such as berberine inhibit TarO and the glycosyltransferases
TarS and TarM, thereby disrupting WTA glycosylation. These inhibitors
restore oxacillin and methicillin susceptibility in both wild-type
and mutant MRSA strains. The ABC transporter
TarGH is another validated target, and an inhibitor, Targocil, has
been developed. A cryo-EM structure of the TarGH homolog bound to
Targocil identified the inhibitor-binding site and a substrate translocation
mechanism, providing a framework for structure-guided drug design. WTA has additionally been exploited as a targeting
handle for antibody-antibiotic conjugates. DSTA4637S (Genentech) is
a THIOMAB antibody conjugate in which an anti-WTA IgG1 monoclonal
antibody is linked via a protease-cleavable linker to the rifamycin-class
antibiotic dmDNA31. Phagocytic cells ingest DSTA4637S-opsonized S. aureus, and intracellular cathepsins release dmDNA31,
which in turn kills the intracellular reservoir of bacteria that conventional
antibiotics cannot reach. A Phase 1a study of DSTA4637S in healthy
volunteers resulted with a favorable safety and pharmacokinetic profile.
However, a subsequent Phase 1b study in patients with complicated S. aureus bacteremia revealed infusion-related reactions
that led to the discontinuation of the trial.
,

4.3.2TA Vaccine TargetsThe surface
exposure of WTA and LTA, combined with their structural roles in bacterial–host
recognition, makes them good candidates for vaccine-based strategies.
However, their high conservation across commensal and pathogenic species
has historically complicated their use as direct antigens. The CBP
PspA of S. pneumoniae has received the most sustained
attention as a vaccine antigen. PspA is present in virtually all clinically
relevant S. pneumoniae strains and interferes with
complement activation by blocking C3b deposition on the bacterial
surface and binding lactoferrin to inhibit the alternative pathway. Recombinant PspA fragments, fusion constructs
combining PspA with other CBPs, and nanoparticle-based formulations
have all demonstrated strong immunogenicity and protection across
multiple animal models, and early phase clinical trials have confirmed
safety and immunogenicity in humans. The primary challenge remains
antigenic variability across the two major PspA clades and their further
subdivisions, which has driven efforts to identify broadly cross-reactive
fragments. LTA itself has been tested
as a vaccine antigen in S. aureus but has not yielded
successful clinical candidates, in part because the adaptive immune
response to LTA is T-cell-independent and does not generate durable
immunological memory and in part because anti-LTA antibodies have
not demonstrated consistent opsonophagocytic killing in preclinical
models.

Overview of the Structure and Organization of
Teichoic Acids

Monoderm PG layers can be modified with glycolipid
polymers known
as teichoic acids (TAs; Figure
) that extend outward from the cell. TAs can be anchored to
the cell membrane as lipoteichoic acids (LTA) or covalently linked
to the PG as wall teichoic acids (WTA) (Figure
). Both LTAs and WTAs are unique to monoderm
bacteria and have roles in biofilm formation and bacterial adhesion
as well as controlling cell shape and cell division. LTA has polymerized glycerol phosphate repeat units and
is covalently attached to PG. WTAs have a disaccharide base, covalently
attached to PG, and a long polymer of sugar phosphate units (e.g.,
glycerol phosphate or ribitol phosphate) linked through a phosphodiester
bond. WTAs are anchored to the outer leaflet of the IM through a covalent
linkage to a glycolipid. TA chemical modifications identified include
acylation (e.g., d-alanine), esterification (of the d-alanine modifications), choline, and various sugar moieties, such
as GlcNAc.
,
 TA protein modification occurs
when choline-binding proteins (CBPs) bind to TAs, decorating the bacterium’s
surface. CBPs are a diverse protein family that non-covalently bind
to phosphorylcholine moieties via a choline binding domain found on
both lipoteichoic and wall teichoic acids.

backbone from CDP-ribitol. The nascent WTA polymer is modified by
TarM (α-GlcNAc glycosylation) and TarS (β-GlcNAc glycosylation)
from UDP-GlcNAc in the cytoplasm, before the ABC transporter complex

TAs are critical to the basic cellular architecture
and division.
In S. aureus, TAs direct the positioning of the cell
wall synthesis machinery. LTAs in S. aureus help regulate autolytic enzyme activity, preventing
excessive self-digestion of the cell wall. In S. aureus and L. monocytogenes, WTAs contribute to cell morphology, and their loss results in severe
defects in cell shape and division. In B. subtilis, CBPs modulate the activity of autolysins, the
enzymes responsible for controlled cell wall turnover.

4.1Biosynthesis of TAsThe biosynthesis
of teichoic acids (Figure
) in monoderm bacteria involves two distinct pathways for
WTAs and LTAs, each requiring a dedicated set of enzymatic machinery.
The synthesis of WTAs in S. aureus proceeds through
two major stages that take place on opposite sides of the cytoplasmic
membrane. The process begins in the cytoplasm, where a N-acetylglucosamine-1-phosphate
transferase (TarO in S. aureus) transfers N-acetylglucosamine-1-phosphate
onto the lipid carrier undecaprenyl phosphate, forming a membrane-anchored
intermediate from which the polymer is subsequently assembled. TarA and TarB add sugar and ribitol-phosphate
units to build the polymer, and the completed chain is exported by
the TarGH ABC transporter.
,
 Once on the exterior
face of the membrane, the WTA polymer is covalently attached to the
PG layer through the action of LytR-CpsA-Psr (LCP) (TarTUV), which
transfers the completed polymer onto muramic acid residues within
the cell wall.
The biosynthesis
of LTAs occurs on the outer leaflet of the cytoplasmic membrane. In S. aureus and B. subtilis, the enzyme LtaS
polymerizes glycerol-phosphate units derived from PG directly onto
a glycolipid anchor. In order for LTA
polymers to be modified with GlcNAc (or galactose in some species),
GlcNAc must be transported to the periplasm. A set of three proteins
(a cytoplasmic glycotransferase, a flippase, and a periplasmic glycotransferase)
is required.
,
 The cytoplasmic glycotransferase
utilizes UDP-galactose to form a C55–P-galactose,
which is transported across the membrane by the flippase. Once in
the periplasm, the periplasmic glycotransferase transfers GlcNac to
the LTA backbone. This extracellular glycosylation mechanism may also
modify the WTA.
Glycosyltransferases
add further modifications to the teichoic
acid backbone, such as the incorporation of d-alanine through
the Dlt pathway, esterification, and the attachment of glycosyl groups.
The Dlt pathway, which is widespread among pathogens including S. aureus, L. monocytogenes, and Streptococcus pyogenes, is well characterized.
,
 DltA adenylates free d-alanine and loads it onto DltC as
a thioester, DltB then transfers it to a tyrosine on DltX, and DltD
finally transfers the d-alanine onto the LTA.

4.2TAs in PathogenesisIn S.
aureus, wall teichoic acids facilitate nasal colonization
by interacting with host ligands present on nasal epithelial cells,
and animal models in which WTA biosynthesis has been genetically disrupted
show dramatically reduced colonization efficiency. WTAs interact with the scavenger receptor SREC-I expressed
on keratinocytes and contributes to staphylococcal skin colonization.
Given that nasal and skin carriage of S. aureus is
a well-established risk factor for invasive infection, this adhesion
function carries considerable clinical significance. The glycotransferase GtcA in L. monocytogenes adds galactose residues to WTAs. Strains lacking this modification
exhibit reduced virulence in animal models, indicating that the glycosylation
modification contributes to pathogenicity.
,

to resistance against human β-defensins produced by skin epithelial
cells and increase bacterial survival. In Enterococcus faecium and E. faecalis, modifications to LTA structure influence the degree to which these
molecules stimulate TLR2-mediated inflammatory signaling, with more
heavily modified LTAs sometimes evading robust immune detection and
allowing the bacteria to persist in host tissues with reduced inflammatory
clearance.
,

roles in modulating complement activation, which is a central pillar
of the innate immune response against bacterial pathogens. CBPs (PspA
and PspC) in S. pneuomoniae lyse human red blood
cells during infection. Another CBP
in S. pneuomoniae, CbpC, plays a pivotal role in
dampening host autophagy during infection, promoting bacterial cell
survival. Finally, CbpG in S.
pneuomoniae is an adherence factor that also cleaves the
host extracellular matrix after secretion.

surface, thereby diminishing the electrostatic attraction between
the cell wall and cationic antimicrobial peptides such as defensins
and cathelicidins. Thus, d-alanylation provides the bacterium
with resistance to a front-line component of the host innate immune
system.
,

in group A Streptococcus, where the loss of the d-alanylation-responsible enzyme DltA results in increased net
negative charge at the cell surface and higher susceptibility to antimicrobial
peptides. Finally, d-alanylation
of TAs plays a supplementary role in lipoprotein binding to TLR2 during
host–pathogen interaction, where the loss of these d-alanylated teichoic acids contributes to a loss in lipoprotein binding.

In S.
aureus, wall teichoic acids facilitate nasal colonization
by interacting with host ligands present on nasal epithelial cells,
and animal models in which WTA biosynthesis has been genetically disrupted

function carries considerable clinical significance. The glycotransferase GtcA in L. monocytogenes adds galactose residues to WTAs. Strains lacking this modification
exhibit reduced virulence in animal models, indicating that the glycosylation
modification contributes to pathogenicity.
,

to resistance against human β-defensins produced by skin epithelial
cells and increase bacterial survival. In Enterococcus faecium and E. faecalis, modifications to LTA structure influence the degree to which these
molecules stimulate TLR2-mediated inflammatory signaling, with more

allowing the bacteria to persist in host tissues with reduced inflammatory

roles in modulating complement activation, which is a central pillar

and PspC) in S. pneuomoniae lyse human red blood
cells during infection. Another CBP
in S. pneuomoniae, CbpC, plays a pivotal role in
dampening host autophagy during infection, promoting bacterial cell
survival. Finally, CbpG in S.
pneuomoniae is an adherence factor that also cleaves the
host extracellular matrix after secretion.

In response to antimicrobial peptides, bacteria modifiy TAs. d-Alanylation reduces the overall negative charge of the bacterial
surface, thereby diminishing the electrostatic attraction between
the cell wall and cationic antimicrobial peptides such as defensins
and cathelicidins. Thus, d-alanylation provides the bacterium
with resistance to a front-line component of the host innate immune
system.
,
 This antimicrobial resistance can be seen
in group A Streptococcus, where the loss of the d-alanylation-responsible enzyme DltA results in increased net
negative charge at the cell surface and higher susceptibility to antimicrobial
peptides. Finally, d-alanylation
of TAs plays a supplementary role in lipoprotein binding to TLR2 during
host–pathogen interaction, where the loss of these d-alanylated teichoic acids contributes to a loss in lipoprotein binding.

4.3TA Therapeutic Targets4.3.1TA Antibiotic TargetsTeichoic
acids and their associated CBPs represent therapeutically attractive
targets because of their surface accessibility, essential structural
and pathogenic roles, and the absence in mammalian cells. The WTA
biosynthetic pathway is particularly rich in potential drug targets
because it is dispensable under standard laboratory conditions, yet
essential during infection. This distinction implies that WTA pathway
inhibitors cause minimal on-target toxicity to commensal bacteria
while targeting MRSA and other antibiotic-resistant pathogens.
The WTA biosynthetic pathway has generated
sustained interest as an antibiotic adjuvant target, particularly
against MRSA. Genetic or pharmacological inactivation of TarO does
not kill bacteria outright but abolishes WTA production. Loss of WTA
prevents the proper localization of PBP4, the enzyme responsible for
highly cross-linked PG. The reduction in crosslinks increases the
permeability of antibiotics and resensitizes MRSA to β-lactam
antibiotics that would otherwise be ineffective. Multiple small-molecule TarO inhibitors have been identified,
including ticlopidine and the more potent compound tunicamycin. Natural
products such as berberine inhibit TarO and the glycosyltransferases
TarS and TarM, thereby disrupting WTA glycosylation. These inhibitors
restore oxacillin and methicillin susceptibility in both wild-type
and mutant MRSA strains. The ABC transporter
TarGH is another validated target, and an inhibitor, Targocil, has
been developed. A cryo-EM structure of the TarGH homolog bound to
Targocil identified the inhibitor-binding site and a substrate translocation
mechanism, providing a framework for structure-guided drug design. WTA has additionally been exploited as a targeting
handle for antibody-antibiotic conjugates. DSTA4637S (Genentech) is
a THIOMAB antibody conjugate in which an anti-WTA IgG1 monoclonal
antibody is linked via a protease-cleavable linker to the rifamycin-class
antibiotic dmDNA31. Phagocytic cells ingest DSTA4637S-opsonized S. aureus, and intracellular cathepsins release dmDNA31,
which in turn kills the intracellular reservoir of bacteria that conventional
antibiotics cannot reach. A Phase 1a study of DSTA4637S in healthy
volunteers resulted with a favorable safety and pharmacokinetic profile.
However, a subsequent Phase 1b study in patients with complicated S. aureus bacteremia revealed infusion-related reactions
that led to the discontinuation of the trial.
,

4.3.2TA Vaccine TargetsThe surface
exposure of WTA and LTA, combined with their structural roles in bacterial–host
recognition, makes them good candidates for vaccine-based strategies.
However, their high conservation across commensal and pathogenic species
has historically complicated their use as direct antigens. The CBP
PspA of S. pneumoniae has received the most sustained
attention as a vaccine antigen. PspA is present in virtually all clinically
relevant S. pneumoniae strains and interferes with
complement activation by blocking C3b deposition on the bacterial
surface and binding lactoferrin to inhibit the alternative pathway. Recombinant PspA fragments, fusion constructs
combining PspA with other CBPs, and nanoparticle-based formulations
have all demonstrated strong immunogenicity and protection across
multiple animal models, and early phase clinical trials have confirmed
safety and immunogenicity in humans. The primary challenge remains
antigenic variability across the two major PspA clades and their further
subdivisions, which has driven efforts to identify broadly cross-reactive
fragments. LTA itself has been tested
as a vaccine antigen in S. aureus but has not yielded
successful clinical candidates, in part because the adaptive immune
response to LTA is T-cell-independent and does not generate durable
immunological memory and in part because anti-LTA antibodies have
not demonstrated consistent opsonophagocytic killing in preclinical
models.

Overview of the Structure and Organization of
the Surface-Layer

Surface (S) layers are crystalline arrays
of surface-layer proteins
(SLPs) that are typically on the outside of both monoderm and diderm
bacteria. SLPs interact with phages, host cells, small molecules,
and each other to form biofilms. SLPs self-assemble into two-dimensional
crystalline arrays with remarkable diversity of symmetry patterns.
SLPs exhibit minimal sequence conservation, even among closely related
species, and glycosylation patterns vary considerably across organisms.
Some SLPs are nonglycosylated, while others are heavily glycosylated.
Many organisms have S-layers that are composed of a mix of nonglycosylated
SLPs and glycosylated SLPs with varying glycosylation patterns.

Despite their low sequence conservation, SLPs share recurring structural
features at the domain level. Most SLPs are modular proteins composed
of two functionally distinct regions: a conserved N-terminal anchoring
domain that mediates attachment to the cell surface, and a C-terminal
self-assembly domain that drives lattice formation.
,
 The self-assembly domain typically adopts an immunoglobulin-like
or β-sheet-rich fold, and the stacking of these domains across
adjacent monomers generates the regular repeating geometry of the
S-layer lattice.
−

 The lattices themselves display oblique
(p1, p2), square (p4), or hexagonal (p3, p6) symmetry depending on
the species, with unit cell dimensions typically ranging from 4 to
35 nm.
,
 SLPs range considerably in molecular weight,
generally falling between 40 and 200 kDa, and in many organisms the
S-layer is composed of a single abundant SLP species, though some
bacteria, such as Bacillus anthracis, produce heterogeneous
arrays containing subunits of distinct sizes and functions.
,
 In organisms with heavily glycosylated SLPs, the glycans are attached
to serine, threonine, or tyrosine residues and can constitute a substantial
fraction of the total molecular mass, influencing both the biophysical
properties of the lattice and its interactions with the external environment.
−

SLPs interact non-covalently or attach covalently to a structurally
and functionally diverse class of anionic glycopolymers, collectively
termed “non-classical” secondary cell wall polysaccharides
(SCWPs). Teichoic acids (wall teichoic acids, WTAs, and lipoteichoic
acids, LTAs) are considered “classical” SCWPs and are
discussed in Section
. The best-characterized SCWPs are the pyruvylated polysaccharides
that serve as anchoring ligands for S-layer homology (SLH)-domain
proteins, but the SCWP family also encompasses
a variety of species-specific capsular and quasi-capsular polysaccharides
in both monoderm bacteria and diderm bacteria.

SCWPs are heteropolysaccharides
covalently attached to the PG layer
via muramic acid residues of the PG backbone.
,
 Unlike wall teichoic acids, which are largely built from polyol–phosphate
repeat units (Section
), SCWPs are composed of neutral and charged sugar residues
including hexoses, hexosamines, and uronic acids, arranged in species-specific
repeat units of two to six sugars. The
anchoring interaction between SCWPs and SLPs is mediated by dedicated
protein domains whose identity varies between organisms, and this
variation reflects fundamental differences in cell wall anchoring
strategy across bacterial lineages.

In B. anthracis, the SCWP is a pyruvylated trisaccharide
repeat of ManNAc, GlcNAc, and GalNAc; the ketal pyruvate modification
at the 4,6-position of ManNAc is the critical chemical feature recognized
by the SLH domains of S-layer proteins Sap and EA1, providing the
molecular basis for S-layer anchoring to the cell wall.
,
 This SLH-domain/pyruvylated-SCWP interaction is considered an ancestral
mechanism for SLP anchoring and is conserved across many SLH-domain-carrying
bacteria, including Geobacillus stearothermophilus and Paenibacillus alvei.

In Clostridioides difficile, the anchoring
polymer
and recognition domain are both distinct from those of the B. anthracis system. The predominant anchoring polysaccharide
is polysaccharide II (PSII), a hexaglycosyl phosphate repeat, which
interacts with the cell wall binding domain 2 (CWB2). CWB2 is a structurally
unrelated SLP. PSII attachment to PG
is mediated by LCP enzymes LcpA and LcpB, and this attachment is essential
for bacterial viability. The S-protein
organization and anchoring diversity extend further to deep-branching
extremophiles such as Thermus thermophilus and Deinococcus radiodurans, which possess S-layers positioned
outside an atypical OM that lacks lipopolysaccharides and interact
with both the OM and PG through distinct mechanisms.

S-Layer Biogenesis

S-Layer biogenesis
requires the coordinated execution of two distinct but interdependent
biosynthetic programs: the production and surface display of the S-layer
protein itself and the synthesis and cell wall attachment of the secondary
cell wall polysaccharide (SCWP) that anchors it. In organisms that
use SLH domain-mediated anchoring, an SLP secreted in the absence
of its cognate pyruvylated SCWP fails to associate stably with the
cell surface, and conversely, a properly assembled SCWP without its
SLP partner provides no protective lattice. The pathways differ substantially
between monoderm and diderm bacteria, reflecting the structural differences
in their cell envelopes, but in both cases, the result is the same:
a self-assembled crystalline protein array precisely positioned at
the outermost surface of the cell. The following subsections describe
the biogenesis of SCWPs and S-layer proteins in turn with particular
emphasis on the molecular mechanisms of secretion, modification, anchoring,
and lattice assembly.

SCWP Biogenesis

SCWP biosynthesis
(Figure
) in B. anthracis initiates with TagO, a UDP-N-acetylglucosamine:undecaprenyl-phosphate transferase that generates
the lipid-linked murein linkage unit onto which the SCWP trisaccharide
repeat is built.
,
 The trisaccharide repeat unit
[→4)-β-ManNAc-(1→4)-β-GlcNAc-(1→6)-α-GlcNAc-(1→]
requires dedicated glycosyltransferases for its assembly, including
the products of wpaA and wpaB, which
are proposed to polymerize repeat units or transfer the completed
polymer to the linkage unit prior to PG attachment. CsaB, a ketal pyruvyltransferase encoded within the conserved
S-layer gene cluster, modifies the SCWP with a pyruvate group; this
modification is the critical chemical feature recognized by the SLH
domains of Sap and EA1, and loss of CsaB abolishes S-layer anchoring
entirely. Following assembly and modification,
mature SCWP chains are transferred from the lipid carrier to the C6-hydroxyl
of MurNAc residues in PG by LCP family enzymes, the same enzymes that
attach wall teichoic acids to PG (Section
) and capsular polysaccharides (CPS) in
monoderm bacteria (Section
). B. anthracis encodes six LCP paralogs
with partially overlapping but distinct roles in SCWP deposition,
vegetative growth, and sporulation.
,
 Additional
modifications including galactosylation of the trisaccharide backbone
contribute to proper cell wall envelope assembly and are required
for full virulence.

The overall logic of the B. anthracis pathway
(lipid-carrier-mediated repeat unit assembly, pyruvylation, LCP-dependent
PG ligation, and SLH-domain recognition) is conserved as a general
strategy among SLH-domain-carrying bacteria, although the specific
polysaccharide structures and modifying enzymes vary considerably
between species. In G. stearothermophilus and related
thermophilic bacilli, the SCWP is similarly pyruvylated and the CsaB
orthologue is required for pyruvylation and S-layer anchoring. In P. alvei, the SLH domains
of the glycosylated S-layer protein SpaA bind to pyruvylated SCWP
and the PG directly, which provides additional anchoring not observed
in B. anthracis.

5.1.2S-Protein BiogenesisIn monoderm
species such as B. anthracis and C. difficile, S-layer precursors are secreted via accessory Sec systems involving
SecA2 and SecY2, with signal peptides cleaved upon membrane translocation
(Figure
).
−

 Diderm bacteria use secretion systems; however, different processes
are utilized depending on the species.
−

 Following secretion,
proteins anchor to the cell wall through distinct mechanisms: monoderm
SLPs have SLH-domains that bind secondary cell wall polymers such
as pyruvylated polysaccharides, while diderm SLPs associate with LPS
via protein–carbohydrate interactions.
−

 Recent cryotomography studies reveal that S-layer assembly is spatially
coordinated with PG synthesis and cell division, with new proteins
incorporating at poles and division sites where lattice discontinuities
facilitate insertion. Some species such
as C. difficile require proteolytic processing by
cysteine proteases (Cwp84) for proper S-layer maturation and assembly. Some bacteria change the S-layer in response
to environmental conditions or to evade the host immune system. For
example, B. anthracis expresses switchable SLPs (Sap
and EA1), with Sap produced during exponential growth and EA1 during
the stationary phase. Campylobacter
fetus employs antigenic variation of S-layer proteins to evade adaptive
immunity during chronic infections.

S-Protein Biogenesis

SecA2 and SecY2, with signal peptides cleaved upon membrane translocation
(Figure
).
−

are utilized depending on the species.
−

proteins anchor to the cell wall through distinct mechanisms: monoderm

5.2S-Layer in PathogenesisSLPs are
critical virulence factors mediating pathogen–host recognition
and immune modulation through multiple mechanisms.
,
 In C. difficile, the predominant S-layer protein
SlpA contains high and low molecular weight subunits. The high molecular
weight subunit mediates adhesion to intestinal epithelial cells and
extracellular matrix components and, thus contributes to colonization.
,
 The intact S-layer induces both pro-inflammatory cytokines (IL-1β,
TNF-α) and regulatory cytokines (IL-10) through interactions
with pattern recognition receptors including TLR2 and dendritic cell-specific
ICAM-3-grabbing non-integrin (DC-SIGN).
,,
 In Tannerella forsythia, an oral pathogen, S-layer deficiency abolishes adherence to gingival
epithelial cells, demonstrating the essential role in colonization. Conversely, probiotic Lactobacillus species utilize S-layers for beneficial host interactions, with Lactobacilus acidophilus S-layer proteins blocking pathogen
adhesion by occupying DC-SIGN binding sites phenotypes.
Additionally, the cell envelope of C. difficile features a prominent S-layer composed of SLPs
that mediate host cell adhesion and immune evasion, contributing to
its pathogenicity.
,,
 This proteinaceous outer layer is essential for colonization and
toxin delivery, which underscores the diverse strategies bacteria
employ to establish infection.
,

,

5.3S-Layer Therapeutic Targets5.3.1S-Layer Antibiotic TargetsS-Layers
are the outermost bacterial surface in many Gram-positive pathogens
and are accordingly exposed to host immune surveillance and potential
therapeutic agents. Their lineage-specific composition, high abundance,
and essential roles in adhesion, immune evasion, and cell envelope
integrity make them attractive targets for both antibiotic adjuvant
strategies and vaccine development. The
SLH-SCWP anchoring interface represents a discrete, chemically accessible
point of vulnerability. Disruption of this interaction would collapse
the S-layer assembly without requiring intracellular drug penetration.
Despite the structural rationale, no
commercially available antibiotics that directly target S-layer proteins
(SLPs) or SCWPs are currently available. The SLH–SCWP interface
has been proposed as a drug target based on crystallographic evidence
that the terminal pyruvylated ManNAc moiety of the SCWP serves as
the nearly exclusive anchoring point for the SLH domain trimer of P. alvei SpaA. The co-crystal structures define two distinct
binding sites and show that engagement of a single pyruvylated monosaccharide
is sufficient to trigger receptor rearrangement, suggesting that small
molecules or synthetic SCWP analogs designed to block or mimic this
interaction could prevent S-layer assembly. The broader appeal of the S-layer as a drug target is further supported
by work in C. difficile, where SlpA mediates epithelial
adhesion and tight junction disruption, functions essential to colonization.
Thus, pharmacological interference with S-layer assembly or surface
presentation could reduce virulence independently of toxin production. However, antibiotic development targeting the
S-layer remains at the structural biology and target validation stage;
no inhibitor series targeting this interface has yet been reported
in clinical or late preclinical literature.5.3.2S-Layer Vaccine TargetsS-Layer
proteins have a more developed translational record as vaccine antigens
than as drug targets, primarily in the context of C. difficile and B. anthracis. In C. difficile, the predominant S-layer protein SlpA has been proposed as a surface
antigen component to complement toxoid-based vaccine formulations. The C. difficile vaccine landscape
has been shaped by the Phase 3 CLOVER trial (NCT03090191), which tested
Pfizer’s detoxified toxin A/B vaccine PF-06425090 in 17 535
adults aged 50 years or older at increased risk of CDI. The primary
efficacy end point was not met, although the vaccine significantly
reduced CDI symptom duration, the need for medical attention, and
antibiotic use among those who developed CDI. This failure of a toxin-focused approach has intensified
interest in surface protein antigens, including SlpA, as components
of next-generation multicomponent C. difficile vaccines. In B. anthracis, both EA1
and the SCWP have been investigated as supplementary antigens in recombinant
anthrax vaccine formulations to broaden immune coverage beyond the
current protective antigen (PA)-based approach, although no S-layer-based
anthrax vaccine has advanced to clinical trials. Across both pathogens, the central challenge for S-layer-based
vaccines is antigenic variation. C. difficile undergoes
phase-variable S-layer switching between different SLP variants, and B. anthracis switches between Sap and EA1 depending on the
growth phase, both of which could reduce the breadth of S-layer-directed
immune responses.
