OMP Structure

OMPs are antiparallel
β-sheet that closes into a barrel, with the sequence of each
strand alternating hydrophobic and hydrophilic residues. This alternating
pattern, with hydrophobic residues facing the lipid environment and
hydrophilic residues lining the barrel interior or forming interstrand
hydrogen bonds, is essential for membrane insertion and stability.
−

 Aromatic residues form prominent ’aromatic belts’
at the membrane interfaces. These aromatic
residues preferentially partition at the membrane-water interface,
anchoring the barrel at the correct depth in the membrane and providing
thermodynamic stability. The positioning
of aromatic belts is remarkably conserved across diverse OMPs, and
the distance between the extracellular and periplasmic belts correlates
with the hydrophobic thickness of the OM (∼25-27 Å, thinner
than the IM). The number of β-strands
in bacterial OMPs typically ranges from 8 to 36, with the barrel diameter
determined by strand and shear number. Strand tilt relative to the barrel axis averages 40-50, optimizing
hydrogen bonding between strands and the overall barrel stability.
Many OMPs have a ’β-signal’ at the C-terminus,
typically ending with an aromatic residue and a polar or charged residue
in the penultimate position, while the -3 position contains an aromatic
residue, which is important for recognition by the BAM complex. That
mediates the insertion of OMPs into the OM.

6.2OM BiogenesisBuilding and maintaining
the OM therefore requires the coordinated activity of at least four
distinct systems: the LPS synthesis and transport machinery that establishes
outer leaflet asymmetry, the Sec and BAM machineries that translocate,
fold and insert β-barrel OMPs, the LPS-OMP interaction network
that organizes the mature membrane into protective structure, the
Lol pathway that traffics lipoproteins to their final destinations,
and the transport of glycerophospholipids to the inner leaflet of
the OM to maintain asymmetry that helps prevent influx of toxins.
The following subsections describe each of these processes.6.2.1LPS BiogenesisLPS biosynthesis
is a multi-stage process that proceeds from the cytoplasm to the OM
outer leaflet and can be divided into three sequential phases: synthesis
of lipid A and its glycosylation with Kdo, assembly and attachment
of the core oligosaccharide and O-antigen, and transport across the
cell envelope via the Lpt machinery.Lipid A biosynthesis proceeds
through a conserved nine-enzyme pathway at the cytoplasmic face of
the IM, known as the Raetz pathway.
,
 The first
committed step is the deacetylation of UDP-3-O-(R-3-hydroxymyristoyl)-N-acetylglucosamine
by LpxC, which is tightly post-translationally controlled and serves
as the primary regulatory checkpoint for LPS synthesis. Sequential reactions catalyzed by LpxD, LpxH,
LpxB, and LpxK generate the lipid IVA intermediate, to
which two 3-deoxy-D-manno-oct-2-ulosonic acid (Kdo)
residues are added by the bifunctional enzyme KdtA. Addition of the
secondary acyl chains then produces the mature hexa-acylated Kdo2-lipid A endotoxin moiety embedded in the inner leaflet of
the IM.
,
 Core oligosaccharide assembly is completed
on the cytoplasmic face by sequential glycosyltransferases, generating
the lipid A core (rough LPS, R-LPS).
,

In
smooth LPS (S-LPS)-producing bacteria, the O-antigen polysaccharide
is synthesized separately. In the widely distributed Wzx/Wzy-dependent
pathway, repeat units are assembled on an undecaprenyl phosphate (Und-P)
lipid carrier at the cytoplasmic face of the IM, flipped to the periplasmic
face by the Wzx flippase, polymerized by Wzy, and then ligated to
the completed lipid A core by the WaaL O-antigen ligase.
,
 In the ABC transporter-dependent pathway, used by organisms such
as Brucella abortus, the O-antigen polymer is assembled
on Und-PP and translocated by the Wzm/Wzt ABC transporter complex.
,

Regardless of the O-antigen assembly route, the completed
R-LPS
or S-LPS must be flipped from the inner to the outer leaflet of the
IM by the essential ABC transporter MsbA before it can be exported. Cryo-EM structures of MsbA in multiple functional
states show that LPS binds deep within the transporter cavity, establishing
extensive hydrophilic and hydrophobic contacts, and that ATP-driven
transitions from inward- to outward-facing conformations catalyze
translocation. In unipolarly growing
bacteria such as B. abortus, MsbA localizes preferentially
to the old cell pole, while the downstream Lpt complex concentrates
at the new pole, implying that LPS undergoes lateral diffusion in
the IM before reaching the export machinery.
Once in the periplasmic leaflet of the IM, LPS is extracted
and
transported to the OM by the seven-component Lpt system, which forms
a continuous transenvelope bridge. The
IM ABC transporter LptB∼2∼FGC uses ATP hydrolysis to
extract LPS from the membrane and powers its movement through the
periplasmic carrier LptA, which relays it to the OM translocon formed
by LptD and LptE. LptD is a 26-stranded
β-barrel through whose lateral gate LPS is inserted into the
outer leaflet of the OM; crystal structures of LptD/LptE complexes
with bound LPS have directly visualized this insertion step and the
role of the lateral gate. The unidirectional,
energy-dependent nature of LPS biogenesis maintains the characteristic
lipid asymmetry of the OM that is essential for the impermeability
barrier.6.2.2OMP Biogenesis and FoldingOMP
biogenesis starts with translocation across the IM via the Sec pathway,
with signal peptides (SPs) directing them to SecYEG (Figure
). The N-terminal SP sequence facilitates their translocation across
the IM. Once the SP is recognized and
processed, periplasmic chaperones SurA, Skp, FkpA, and DegP prevent
aggregation and premature folding of OMPs in the aqueous periplasm
(Figure
).
−

 SurA is the primary periplasmic chaperone, recognizing unfolded
OMPs through the conserved hydrophobic motif (ϕXϕϕPro)
via its peptidylprolyl isomerase (PPIase) domains which maintain them
in a folding-competent state and facilitate their folding. SurA escorts OMPs, while Skp forms a trimer
that encapsulates OMPs to promote the early stages of OMP assembly.
,−

 Skp also acts as a quality control chaperone,
directing the removal of stalled OMPs that have undergone incomplete
folding for degradation by DegP.
,
 FkpA responds
to heat-shock stress and, unlike other periplasmic chaperones, increases
the folded yield but decreases the folding rate of OMPs. DegP provides both chaperone and protease activities
depending on temperature and cellular stress.
,
 The absence of SurA results in slower translocation of the protein
to the BAM complex. The absence of Skp facilitates direct binding
of SurA to the protein.
−

 The conserved ‘β-signal’
motif (GXXϕXϕ) located at the C-terminus of OMPs is an
important recognition determinant for the BAM complex, including the
essential accessory lipoprotein BamD.
,−

 BamA, the core component of the BAM complex, contains N-terminal
periplasmic polypeptide transport-associated (POTRA) domains that
interact with unfolded OMPs delivered by periplasmic chaperones and
coordinate their assembly.
,,
 The accessory lipoproteins BamBCDE contribute to the stability,
organization, and regulation of the BAM complex, modulating BamA conformational
dynamics and substrate assembly rather than directly mediating the
transfer of unfolded OMPs into the BamA β-barrel lumen.
−

 Together, these components stabilize the complex and facilitate
efficient transfer and folding of unfolded OMPs into the lumen of
the C-terminal β-barrel BamA.
,,,
 OMPs that lack the
β-signal may exhibit impaired BAM recognition and consequently
remain in the periplasm or undergo degradation. Differences in the β-signal sequence appear to be
species- and clade-specific, suggesting an adaptation between the
BAM complex and nascent OMP substrates.
,,
 These chaperones work synergistically,
with partially redundant functions ensuring robust OMP delivery.
The final step of OMP biogenesis occurs
at the BAM complex (Figure
). In E. coli, BamA, a 16-stranded β-barrel
with five N-terminal periplasmic polypeptide transport-associated
(POTRA) domains interact with unfolded substrates from periplasmic
chaperones.
,,
 Several models have been proposed to explain how the BAM complex
coordinates OMP assembly into the OM, and these models may represent
complementary mechanisms rather than mutually exclusive pathways.
The “budding” or “threading” model proposes
that BamA transiently accommodates unfolded OMPs within its lumen,
where the substrate progressively folds into β-hairpins that
are subsequently released or “bud” laterally through
the BamA barrel to assemble into the mature β-barrel.
,
 In contrast, the “BAM-assisted“ model proposes that
the complex functions primarily as a chaperone-like catalyst that
locally destabilizes the membrane environment, thereby lowering the
energetic barrier for spontaneous OMP folding and insertion.
,
 Both models emphasize the dynamic nature of BamA and the importance
of the surrounding lipid environment in facilitating OMP assembly.
,,

Structural and biochemical
studies have proposed additional models
that describe specific stages of this process. The “swing”
model proposes that OMP engagement begins through an interaction between
the substrate’s C-terminal β-signal and the first β-strand
of BamA, forming an inter-β-barrel seam. Subsequent conformational
changes in BamA promote a swinging motion of the partially assembled
OMP away from the BamA barrel and into the surrounding membrane, where
β-strand augmentation and progressive folding can occur. Related “β-signal exchange”
and “β-barrel switching” models emphasize the
transient formation of a hybrid β-barrel between BamA and the
incoming OMP. In these models, β-strand interactions between
the substrate β-signal and BamA initiate assembly, followed
by progressive β-strand exchange that allows the substrate to
displace BamA and form an independent β-barrel. Rather than representing entirely distinct
mechanisms, these models provide complementary frameworks for understanding
how β-signal recognition, BamA conformational dynamics, OMP
folding, and membrane insertion may be coordinated during OMP assembly.Experimental evidence further supports an important role for β-signal
recognition during the early stages of BAM-mediated assembly. In E. coli, the small molecule antibiotic darobactin inhibits
binding of the autotransporter EspP β-signal to BamA and prevents
subsequent OMP assembly, whereas other BAM-targeting compounds, including
Polyphor peptide 7 and MRL-494, interfere with substrate binding and
subsequent stages of assembly.
−

 These findings support a model
in which β-signal engagement with BamA initiates membrane insertion
and BAM-mediated assembly proceeds through multiple mechanistically
distinct stages. Collectively, current structural and biochemical
evidence supports a dynamic model in which BamA transiently engages
nascent OMPs through their β-signal, undergoes conformational
rearrangements that facilitate substrate insertion, and coordinates
progressive β-barrel folding with release into the OM. The relative
contributions of threading, membrane-assisted, swing, and strand-exchange
mechanisms may vary among OMP substrates and their respective folding
pathways, rather than reflecting a single universal assembly mechanism
for all OMPs.
,−


Atypical OMs, such as those in T. maritima, other Thermotogae, Spirochaetes, and Bacteroidetes have unusual and unknown biogenesis
pathways.
In T. maritima and other Thermotogae, the detached toga membrane requires specialized trafficking mechanisms
that involve vesicular transport and distinct lipid compositions.
,
 In Treponema pallidum, Tp92, also known as Tp0326
(BamA), is evolutionarily distinct from that found in other diderm
bacteria, which suggests the OMPs may also be atypical.
,
 The T. pallidum BAM complex is larger than E. coli (∼300 to 400 kDa versus 232 kDa) and lacks
BamBCDE accessory lipoprotein subunits. Moreover, T. pallidum BamA has a positively charged
lumen and an unusual extracellular loop containing polyserine-rich
sequences found only in pathogenic treponemes. Unlike typical diderm bacteria, the OM of T. pallidum is thought to be fragile and deficient in OMPs and is, thus, more
susceptible to disruption by detergents, even at relatively low concentrations. Similarly, the Flavobacterium johnsoniae BAM complex structure is different than the canonical E.
coli BAM complex.
,

F. johnsoniae BAM complex contains an extracellular region (composed of BamG and
H) that extends over the substrate folding site, as well as an additional
subunit that inserts into the BamA pore.
,

Importantly, atypical OMP architecture is not restricted to
variations
in BAM composition or biogenesis pathways. Although most characterized
OMPs form individual β-barrels, structural and bioinformatic
analyses have identified OMPs containing multiple β-barrel domains
within a single polypeptide, expanding the known architectural diversity
of OM β-barrels. Other OMPs form
higher order oligomeric assemblies in which multiple small β-barrel
subunits collectively generate a larger membrane-spanning pore. For
example, the E. coli curli secretion protein CsgG
comprises nine approximately four-stranded protomers that assemble
into a 36-stranded β-barrel secretion channel.
−

 More recently, the pentameric PopA from the predatory bacterium Bdellovibrio bacteriovorous was shown to form an unusual
porin-like oligomeric architecture associated with lipid trapping,
further illustrating the structural diversity of β-barrel OMP
assemblies. Conversely, some OMPs depart
entirely from the canonical β-barrel fold. The E. coli polysaccharide export protein Wza, for example, forms a large α-helical
transmembrane barrel with a central cavity that accommodates the passage
of assembled capsular polysaccharide. Collectively, these examples demonstrate that OMPs encompass a broader
range of architectures than the canonical single β-barrel paradigm,
including multibarrel proteins, oligomeric β-barrel assemblies,
and entirely α-helical transmembrane structures. This structural
diversity likely necessitates corresponding adaptations in the pathways
responsible for OMP targeting, assembly, and function across diverse
diderm bacterial lineages.6.2.3LPS-OMP Interactions and OrganizationDirect interactions between OMPs and LPS have been identified in
several systems. In E. coli, interactions between
the OmpC porin and LPS have been proposed to facilitate OmpC insertion
into the OM, suggesting that LPS contributes to both OMP biogenesis
and OM organization.
−

 The ferrichrome receptor FhuA (a 22-stranded
β-barrel) co-crystallized with a bound LPS molecule. The FhuA-LPS contacts occur primarily through
electrostatic interactions between the phosphate residues of lipid
A and Kdo I and II sugar moieties and basic, positively charged amino
acids of FhuA. This FhuA structure revealed
a conserved LPS-binding motif consisting of four basic amino acids,
which was identified in other prokaryotic and eukaryotic LPS-binding
proteins. Solution NMR spectroscopy
demonstrated that P. aeruginosa OprH directly binds
LPS through predominantly electrostatic interactions localized to
extracellular loops 2 and 3.
,
 The OM protease OmpT
requires LPS as an essential cofactor for catalytic activity; the
crystal structure identifies a putative LPS-binding site containing
conserved basic residues (Arg138, Arg175, Lys226).
,

LPS forms a tightly integrated supramolecular network with
OMPs. Molecular dynamics simulations reveal that each OMP has a unique
pattern of interactions with surrounding LPS molecules, determined
by the protein’s composition, extracellular domain architecture,
and positioning of residues at the membrane interface (referred to
as “LPS fingerprint”).
,,
 Both OMPs and LPS molecules are proposed to have
limited lateral mobility once inserted into the OM. Furthermore, the
OMPs and LPS segregate into clusters, forming an ordered protein–lipid
lattice that provides structural integrity and mechanical strength.
,
 OmpA plays a critical role in organizing this lattice by remaining
immobile through anchoring to the PG and strongly interacting with
surrounding OMPs via LPS bridges.
6.2.4OM Lipoprotein BiogenesisOM lipoproteins
have diverse cellular functions, and many are virulence factors.
,
 Lipoproteins are matured through a post-translational lipoprotein-processing
pathway in the IM as described in Section
 (Figure
). Following translocation across the IM, lipidation,
and signal sequence cleavage, lipoproteins that lack IM retention
signals (an Asp residue in the 2 position) are selectively transported
to the OM through the localization of lipoproteins (Lol) pathway (Figure
). This process is initiated by the ABC transporter complex
LolCDE, embedded in the IM. Powered by ATP hydrolysis, LolCDE extracts
mature lipoproteins from the IM and transfers them to the periplasmic
chaperone LolA. LolA shields the hydrophobic
lipid moieties of lipoproteins and transports them across the aqueous
periplasm.
,
 The LolA-lipoprotein complex
subsequently interacts with LolB, an OM receptor that facilitates
lipoprotein insertion into the inner leaflet of the OM. LolB exhibits
a higher affinity for lipoproteins than LolA, promoting an efficient
transfer and release of the substrate.
,
 Although
LolA and LolB share structurally similar hydrophobic cavities, differences
in amino acid composition contribute to their differential binding
affinities and functional roles.
,

In
addition to lipoprotein trafficking, the OM must maintain a highly
asymmetric lipid composition in which glycerophospholipids are predominantly
restricted to the inner leaflet.
,
 The maintenance
of this asymmetry is mediated in part by the maintenance of lipid
asymmetry (Mla) pathway, which spans the IM, periplasm, and OM. The Mla system comprises the IM ABC transporter
MlaFEDB, the periplasmic lipid-binding protein MlaC, and the OM lipoprotein
MlaA, which associates with OmpC or OmpF.
−

 Current evidence supports a primary role for Mla in retrograde glycerophospholipid
transport, whereby mislocalized glycerophospholipids are extracted
from the OM by MlaA, transferred to MlaC, and shuttled across the
periplasm to MlaFEDB for reinsertion into the IM.
,,
 Structural and biochemical studies
have provided mechanistic insight into this process, demonstrating
that MlaFEDB can bind and translocate phospholipids through its central
cavity.
,
 Although Mla-mediated anterograde glycerophospholipid
transport from the IM toward the OM has also been proposed, the physiological
directionality and contribution of this activity remain debated. Thus,
Mla is best characterized as a lipid trafficking pathway that helps
maintain OM lipid asymmetry by removing and recycling aberrantly localized
glycerophospholipids.
,

Following insertion into
the inner leaflet of the OM by LolB, a
subset of lipoproteins are translocated to the outer leaflet or cell
surface through dedicated secretion pathways, including the surface
lipoprotein assembly modulator (Slam), type II secretion system (T2SS),
and the type V secretion system (T5SS) (see Section
).
−

 For example, the pullulanase
lipoprotein from Klebsiella oxytoca is a substrate
of T2SS, whereas the lipidated autotransporter NalP from N.
meningitidis is secreted through the T5SS.
,
 Together, these pathways illustrate the diverse mechanisms by which
diderm bacteria traffic lipoproteins from their site of synthesis
at the IM to distinct destinations within or beyond the OM.

OM Biogenesis

Building and maintaining
the OM therefore requires the coordinated activity of at least four
distinct systems: the LPS synthesis and transport machinery that establishes
outer leaflet asymmetry, the Sec and BAM machineries that translocate,
fold and insert β-barrel OMPs, the LPS-OMP interaction network
that organizes the mature membrane into protective structure, the
Lol pathway that traffics lipoproteins to their final destinations,
and the transport of glycerophospholipids to the inner leaflet of
the OM to maintain asymmetry that helps prevent influx of toxins.
The following subsections describe each of these processes.

6.2.1LPS BiogenesisLPS biosynthesis
is a multi-stage process that proceeds from the cytoplasm to the OM
outer leaflet and can be divided into three sequential phases: synthesis
of lipid A and its glycosylation with Kdo, assembly and attachment
of the core oligosaccharide and O-antigen, and transport across the
cell envelope via the Lpt machinery.Lipid A biosynthesis proceeds
through a conserved nine-enzyme pathway at the cytoplasmic face of
the IM, known as the Raetz pathway.
,
 The first
committed step is the deacetylation of UDP-3-O-(R-3-hydroxymyristoyl)-N-acetylglucosamine
by LpxC, which is tightly post-translationally controlled and serves
as the primary regulatory checkpoint for LPS synthesis. Sequential reactions catalyzed by LpxD, LpxH,
LpxB, and LpxK generate the lipid IVA intermediate, to
which two 3-deoxy-D-manno-oct-2-ulosonic acid (Kdo)
residues are added by the bifunctional enzyme KdtA. Addition of the
secondary acyl chains then produces the mature hexa-acylated Kdo2-lipid A endotoxin moiety embedded in the inner leaflet of
the IM.
,
 Core oligosaccharide assembly is completed
on the cytoplasmic face by sequential glycosyltransferases, generating
the lipid A core (rough LPS, R-LPS).
,

In
smooth LPS (S-LPS)-producing bacteria, the O-antigen polysaccharide
is synthesized separately. In the widely distributed Wzx/Wzy-dependent
pathway, repeat units are assembled on an undecaprenyl phosphate (Und-P)
lipid carrier at the cytoplasmic face of the IM, flipped to the periplasmic
face by the Wzx flippase, polymerized by Wzy, and then ligated to
the completed lipid A core by the WaaL O-antigen ligase.
,
 In the ABC transporter-dependent pathway, used by organisms such
as Brucella abortus, the O-antigen polymer is assembled
on Und-PP and translocated by the Wzm/Wzt ABC transporter complex.
,

Regardless of the O-antigen assembly route, the completed
R-LPS
or S-LPS must be flipped from the inner to the outer leaflet of the
IM by the essential ABC transporter MsbA before it can be exported. Cryo-EM structures of MsbA in multiple functional
states show that LPS binds deep within the transporter cavity, establishing
extensive hydrophilic and hydrophobic contacts, and that ATP-driven
transitions from inward- to outward-facing conformations catalyze
translocation. In unipolarly growing
bacteria such as B. abortus, MsbA localizes preferentially
to the old cell pole, while the downstream Lpt complex concentrates
at the new pole, implying that LPS undergoes lateral diffusion in
the IM before reaching the export machinery.
Once in the periplasmic leaflet of the IM, LPS is extracted
and
transported to the OM by the seven-component Lpt system, which forms
a continuous transenvelope bridge. The
IM ABC transporter LptB∼2∼FGC uses ATP hydrolysis to
extract LPS from the membrane and powers its movement through the
periplasmic carrier LptA, which relays it to the OM translocon formed
by LptD and LptE. LptD is a 26-stranded
β-barrel through whose lateral gate LPS is inserted into the
outer leaflet of the OM; crystal structures of LptD/LptE complexes
with bound LPS have directly visualized this insertion step and the
role of the lateral gate. The unidirectional,
energy-dependent nature of LPS biogenesis maintains the characteristic
lipid asymmetry of the OM that is essential for the impermeability
barrier.

LPS Biogenesis

LPS biosynthesis
is a multi-stage process that proceeds from the cytoplasm to the OM
outer leaflet and can be divided into three sequential phases: synthesis
of lipid A and its glycosylation with Kdo, assembly and attachment
of the core oligosaccharide and O-antigen, and transport across the
cell envelope via the Lpt machinery.

Lipid A biosynthesis proceeds
through a conserved nine-enzyme pathway at the cytoplasmic face of
the IM, known as the Raetz pathway.
,
 The first
committed step is the deacetylation of UDP-3-O-(R-3-hydroxymyristoyl)-N-acetylglucosamine
by LpxC, which is tightly post-translationally controlled and serves
as the primary regulatory checkpoint for LPS synthesis. Sequential reactions catalyzed by LpxD, LpxH,
LpxB, and LpxK generate the lipid IVA intermediate, to
which two 3-deoxy-D-manno-oct-2-ulosonic acid (Kdo)
residues are added by the bifunctional enzyme KdtA. Addition of the
secondary acyl chains then produces the mature hexa-acylated Kdo2-lipid A endotoxin moiety embedded in the inner leaflet of
the IM.
,
 Core oligosaccharide assembly is completed
on the cytoplasmic face by sequential glycosyltransferases, generating
the lipid A core (rough LPS, R-LPS).
,

In
smooth LPS (S-LPS)-producing bacteria, the O-antigen polysaccharide
is synthesized separately. In the widely distributed Wzx/Wzy-dependent
pathway, repeat units are assembled on an undecaprenyl phosphate (Und-P)
lipid carrier at the cytoplasmic face of the IM, flipped to the periplasmic
face by the Wzx flippase, polymerized by Wzy, and then ligated to
the completed lipid A core by the WaaL O-antigen ligase.
,
 In the ABC transporter-dependent pathway, used by organisms such
as Brucella abortus, the O-antigen polymer is assembled
on Und-PP and translocated by the Wzm/Wzt ABC transporter complex.
,

Regardless of the O-antigen assembly route, the completed
R-LPS
or S-LPS must be flipped from the inner to the outer leaflet of the
IM by the essential ABC transporter MsbA before it can be exported. Cryo-EM structures of MsbA in multiple functional
states show that LPS binds deep within the transporter cavity, establishing
extensive hydrophilic and hydrophobic contacts, and that ATP-driven
transitions from inward- to outward-facing conformations catalyze
translocation. In unipolarly growing
bacteria such as B. abortus, MsbA localizes preferentially
to the old cell pole, while the downstream Lpt complex concentrates
at the new pole, implying that LPS undergoes lateral diffusion in
the IM before reaching the export machinery.

Once in the periplasmic leaflet of the IM, LPS is extracted
and
transported to the OM by the seven-component Lpt system, which forms
a continuous transenvelope bridge. The
IM ABC transporter LptB∼2∼FGC uses ATP hydrolysis to
extract LPS from the membrane and powers its movement through the
periplasmic carrier LptA, which relays it to the OM translocon formed
by LptD and LptE. LptD is a 26-stranded
β-barrel through whose lateral gate LPS is inserted into the
outer leaflet of the OM; crystal structures of LptD/LptE complexes
with bound LPS have directly visualized this insertion step and the
role of the lateral gate. The unidirectional,
energy-dependent nature of LPS biogenesis maintains the characteristic
lipid asymmetry of the OM that is essential for the impermeability
barrier.

6.2.2OMP Biogenesis and FoldingOMP
biogenesis starts with translocation across the IM via the Sec pathway,
with signal peptides (SPs) directing them to SecYEG (Figure
). The N-terminal SP sequence facilitates their translocation across
the IM. Once the SP is recognized and
processed, periplasmic chaperones SurA, Skp, FkpA, and DegP prevent
aggregation and premature folding of OMPs in the aqueous periplasm
(Figure
).
−

 SurA is the primary periplasmic chaperone, recognizing unfolded
OMPs through the conserved hydrophobic motif (ϕXϕϕPro)
via its peptidylprolyl isomerase (PPIase) domains which maintain them
in a folding-competent state and facilitate their folding. SurA escorts OMPs, while Skp forms a trimer
that encapsulates OMPs to promote the early stages of OMP assembly.
,−

 Skp also acts as a quality control chaperone,
directing the removal of stalled OMPs that have undergone incomplete
folding for degradation by DegP.
,
 FkpA responds
to heat-shock stress and, unlike other periplasmic chaperones, increases
the folded yield but decreases the folding rate of OMPs. DegP provides both chaperone and protease activities
depending on temperature and cellular stress.
,
 The absence of SurA results in slower translocation of the protein
to the BAM complex. The absence of Skp facilitates direct binding
of SurA to the protein.
−

 The conserved ‘β-signal’
motif (GXXϕXϕ) located at the C-terminus of OMPs is an
important recognition determinant for the BAM complex, including the
essential accessory lipoprotein BamD.
,−

 BamA, the core component of the BAM complex, contains N-terminal
periplasmic polypeptide transport-associated (POTRA) domains that
interact with unfolded OMPs delivered by periplasmic chaperones and
coordinate their assembly.
,,
 The accessory lipoproteins BamBCDE contribute to the stability,
organization, and regulation of the BAM complex, modulating BamA conformational
dynamics and substrate assembly rather than directly mediating the
transfer of unfolded OMPs into the BamA β-barrel lumen.
−

 Together, these components stabilize the complex and facilitate
efficient transfer and folding of unfolded OMPs into the lumen of
the C-terminal β-barrel BamA.
,,,
 OMPs that lack the
β-signal may exhibit impaired BAM recognition and consequently
remain in the periplasm or undergo degradation. Differences in the β-signal sequence appear to be
species- and clade-specific, suggesting an adaptation between the
BAM complex and nascent OMP substrates.
,,
 These chaperones work synergistically,
with partially redundant functions ensuring robust OMP delivery.
The final step of OMP biogenesis occurs
at the BAM complex (Figure
). In E. coli, BamA, a 16-stranded β-barrel
with five N-terminal periplasmic polypeptide transport-associated
(POTRA) domains interact with unfolded substrates from periplasmic
chaperones.
,,
 Several models have been proposed to explain how the BAM complex
coordinates OMP assembly into the OM, and these models may represent
complementary mechanisms rather than mutually exclusive pathways.
The “budding” or “threading” model proposes
that BamA transiently accommodates unfolded OMPs within its lumen,
where the substrate progressively folds into β-hairpins that
are subsequently released or “bud” laterally through
the BamA barrel to assemble into the mature β-barrel.
,
 In contrast, the “BAM-assisted“ model proposes that
the complex functions primarily as a chaperone-like catalyst that
locally destabilizes the membrane environment, thereby lowering the
energetic barrier for spontaneous OMP folding and insertion.
,
 Both models emphasize the dynamic nature of BamA and the importance
of the surrounding lipid environment in facilitating OMP assembly.
,,

Structural and biochemical
studies have proposed additional models
that describe specific stages of this process. The “swing”
model proposes that OMP engagement begins through an interaction between
the substrate’s C-terminal β-signal and the first β-strand
of BamA, forming an inter-β-barrel seam. Subsequent conformational
changes in BamA promote a swinging motion of the partially assembled
OMP away from the BamA barrel and into the surrounding membrane, where
β-strand augmentation and progressive folding can occur. Related “β-signal exchange”
and “β-barrel switching” models emphasize the
transient formation of a hybrid β-barrel between BamA and the
incoming OMP. In these models, β-strand interactions between
the substrate β-signal and BamA initiate assembly, followed
by progressive β-strand exchange that allows the substrate to
displace BamA and form an independent β-barrel. Rather than representing entirely distinct
mechanisms, these models provide complementary frameworks for understanding
how β-signal recognition, BamA conformational dynamics, OMP
folding, and membrane insertion may be coordinated during OMP assembly.Experimental evidence further supports an important role for β-signal
recognition during the early stages of BAM-mediated assembly. In E. coli, the small molecule antibiotic darobactin inhibits
binding of the autotransporter EspP β-signal to BamA and prevents
subsequent OMP assembly, whereas other BAM-targeting compounds, including
Polyphor peptide 7 and MRL-494, interfere with substrate binding and
subsequent stages of assembly.
−

 These findings support a model
in which β-signal engagement with BamA initiates membrane insertion
and BAM-mediated assembly proceeds through multiple mechanistically
distinct stages. Collectively, current structural and biochemical
evidence supports a dynamic model in which BamA transiently engages
nascent OMPs through their β-signal, undergoes conformational
rearrangements that facilitate substrate insertion, and coordinates
progressive β-barrel folding with release into the OM. The relative
contributions of threading, membrane-assisted, swing, and strand-exchange
mechanisms may vary among OMP substrates and their respective folding
pathways, rather than reflecting a single universal assembly mechanism
for all OMPs.
,−


Atypical OMs, such as those in T. maritima, other Thermotogae, Spirochaetes, and Bacteroidetes have unusual and unknown biogenesis
pathways.
In T. maritima and other Thermotogae, the detached toga membrane requires specialized trafficking mechanisms
that involve vesicular transport and distinct lipid compositions.
,
 In Treponema pallidum, Tp92, also known as Tp0326
(BamA), is evolutionarily distinct from that found in other diderm
bacteria, which suggests the OMPs may also be atypical.
,
 The T. pallidum BAM complex is larger than E. coli (∼300 to 400 kDa versus 232 kDa) and lacks
BamBCDE accessory lipoprotein subunits. Moreover, T. pallidum BamA has a positively charged
lumen and an unusual extracellular loop containing polyserine-rich
sequences found only in pathogenic treponemes. Unlike typical diderm bacteria, the OM of T. pallidum is thought to be fragile and deficient in OMPs and is, thus, more
susceptible to disruption by detergents, even at relatively low concentrations. Similarly, the Flavobacterium johnsoniae BAM complex structure is different than the canonical E.
coli BAM complex.
,

F. johnsoniae BAM complex contains an extracellular region (composed of BamG and
H) that extends over the substrate folding site, as well as an additional
subunit that inserts into the BamA pore.
,

Importantly, atypical OMP architecture is not restricted to
variations
in BAM composition or biogenesis pathways. Although most characterized
OMPs form individual β-barrels, structural and bioinformatic
analyses have identified OMPs containing multiple β-barrel domains
within a single polypeptide, expanding the known architectural diversity
of OM β-barrels. Other OMPs form
higher order oligomeric assemblies in which multiple small β-barrel
subunits collectively generate a larger membrane-spanning pore. For
example, the E. coli curli secretion protein CsgG
comprises nine approximately four-stranded protomers that assemble
into a 36-stranded β-barrel secretion channel.
−

 More recently, the pentameric PopA from the predatory bacterium Bdellovibrio bacteriovorous was shown to form an unusual
porin-like oligomeric architecture associated with lipid trapping,
further illustrating the structural diversity of β-barrel OMP
assemblies. Conversely, some OMPs depart
entirely from the canonical β-barrel fold. The E. coli polysaccharide export protein Wza, for example, forms a large α-helical
transmembrane barrel with a central cavity that accommodates the passage
of assembled capsular polysaccharide. Collectively, these examples demonstrate that OMPs encompass a broader
range of architectures than the canonical single β-barrel paradigm,
including multibarrel proteins, oligomeric β-barrel assemblies,
and entirely α-helical transmembrane structures. This structural
diversity likely necessitates corresponding adaptations in the pathways
responsible for OMP targeting, assembly, and function across diverse
diderm bacterial lineages.

OMP Biogenesis and Folding

OMP
biogenesis starts with translocation across the IM via the Sec pathway,
with signal peptides (SPs) directing them to SecYEG (Figure
). The N-terminal SP sequence facilitates their translocation across
the IM. Once the SP is recognized and
processed, periplasmic chaperones SurA, Skp, FkpA, and DegP prevent
aggregation and premature folding of OMPs in the aqueous periplasm
(Figure
).
−

 SurA is the primary periplasmic chaperone, recognizing unfolded
OMPs through the conserved hydrophobic motif (ϕXϕϕPro)
via its peptidylprolyl isomerase (PPIase) domains which maintain them
in a folding-competent state and facilitate their folding. SurA escorts OMPs, while Skp forms a trimer
that encapsulates OMPs to promote the early stages of OMP assembly.
,−

 Skp also acts as a quality control chaperone,
directing the removal of stalled OMPs that have undergone incomplete
folding for degradation by DegP.
,
 FkpA responds
to heat-shock stress and, unlike other periplasmic chaperones, increases
the folded yield but decreases the folding rate of OMPs. DegP provides both chaperone and protease activities
depending on temperature and cellular stress.
,
 The absence of SurA results in slower translocation of the protein
to the BAM complex. The absence of Skp facilitates direct binding
of SurA to the protein.
−

 The conserved ‘β-signal’
motif (GXXϕXϕ) located at the C-terminus of OMPs is an
important recognition determinant for the BAM complex, including the
essential accessory lipoprotein BamD.
,−

 BamA, the core component of the BAM complex, contains N-terminal
periplasmic polypeptide transport-associated (POTRA) domains that
interact with unfolded OMPs delivered by periplasmic chaperones and
coordinate their assembly.
,,
 The accessory lipoproteins BamBCDE contribute to the stability,
organization, and regulation of the BAM complex, modulating BamA conformational
dynamics and substrate assembly rather than directly mediating the
transfer of unfolded OMPs into the BamA β-barrel lumen.
−

 Together, these components stabilize the complex and facilitate
efficient transfer and folding of unfolded OMPs into the lumen of
the C-terminal β-barrel BamA.
,,,
 OMPs that lack the
β-signal may exhibit impaired BAM recognition and consequently
remain in the periplasm or undergo degradation. Differences in the β-signal sequence appear to be
species- and clade-specific, suggesting an adaptation between the
BAM complex and nascent OMP substrates.
,,
 These chaperones work synergistically,
with partially redundant functions ensuring robust OMP delivery.

The final step of OMP biogenesis occurs
at the BAM complex (Figure
). In E. coli, BamA, a 16-stranded β-barrel
with five N-terminal periplasmic polypeptide transport-associated
(POTRA) domains interact with unfolded substrates from periplasmic
chaperones.
,,
 Several models have been proposed to explain how the BAM complex
coordinates OMP assembly into the OM, and these models may represent
complementary mechanisms rather than mutually exclusive pathways.
The “budding” or “threading” model proposes
that BamA transiently accommodates unfolded OMPs within its lumen,
where the substrate progressively folds into β-hairpins that
are subsequently released or “bud” laterally through
the BamA barrel to assemble into the mature β-barrel.
,
 In contrast, the “BAM-assisted“ model proposes that
the complex functions primarily as a chaperone-like catalyst that
locally destabilizes the membrane environment, thereby lowering the
energetic barrier for spontaneous OMP folding and insertion.
,
 Both models emphasize the dynamic nature of BamA and the importance
of the surrounding lipid environment in facilitating OMP assembly.
,,

Structural and biochemical
studies have proposed additional models
that describe specific stages of this process. The “swing”
model proposes that OMP engagement begins through an interaction between
the substrate’s C-terminal β-signal and the first β-strand
of BamA, forming an inter-β-barrel seam. Subsequent conformational
changes in BamA promote a swinging motion of the partially assembled
OMP away from the BamA barrel and into the surrounding membrane, where
β-strand augmentation and progressive folding can occur. Related “β-signal exchange”
and “β-barrel switching” models emphasize the
transient formation of a hybrid β-barrel between BamA and the
incoming OMP. In these models, β-strand interactions between
the substrate β-signal and BamA initiate assembly, followed
by progressive β-strand exchange that allows the substrate to
displace BamA and form an independent β-barrel. Rather than representing entirely distinct
mechanisms, these models provide complementary frameworks for understanding
how β-signal recognition, BamA conformational dynamics, OMP
folding, and membrane insertion may be coordinated during OMP assembly.

Experimental evidence further supports an important role for β-signal
recognition during the early stages of BAM-mediated assembly. In E. coli, the small molecule antibiotic darobactin inhibits
binding of the autotransporter EspP β-signal to BamA and prevents
subsequent OMP assembly, whereas other BAM-targeting compounds, including
Polyphor peptide 7 and MRL-494, interfere with substrate binding and
subsequent stages of assembly.
−

 These findings support a model
in which β-signal engagement with BamA initiates membrane insertion
and BAM-mediated assembly proceeds through multiple mechanistically
distinct stages. Collectively, current structural and biochemical
evidence supports a dynamic model in which BamA transiently engages
nascent OMPs through their β-signal, undergoes conformational
rearrangements that facilitate substrate insertion, and coordinates
progressive β-barrel folding with release into the OM. The relative
contributions of threading, membrane-assisted, swing, and strand-exchange
mechanisms may vary among OMP substrates and their respective folding
pathways, rather than reflecting a single universal assembly mechanism
for all OMPs.
,−

Atypical OMs, such as those in T. maritima, other Thermotogae, Spirochaetes, and Bacteroidetes have unusual and unknown biogenesis
pathways.
In T. maritima and other Thermotogae, the detached toga membrane requires specialized trafficking mechanisms
that involve vesicular transport and distinct lipid compositions.
,
 In Treponema pallidum, Tp92, also known as Tp0326
(BamA), is evolutionarily distinct from that found in other diderm
bacteria, which suggests the OMPs may also be atypical.
,
 The T. pallidum BAM complex is larger than E. coli (∼300 to 400 kDa versus 232 kDa) and lacks
BamBCDE accessory lipoprotein subunits. Moreover, T. pallidum BamA has a positively charged
lumen and an unusual extracellular loop containing polyserine-rich
sequences found only in pathogenic treponemes. Unlike typical diderm bacteria, the OM of T. pallidum is thought to be fragile and deficient in OMPs and is, thus, more
susceptible to disruption by detergents, even at relatively low concentrations. Similarly, the Flavobacterium johnsoniae BAM complex structure is different than the canonical E.
coli BAM complex.
,

F. johnsoniae BAM complex contains an extracellular region (composed of BamG and
H) that extends over the substrate folding site, as well as an additional
subunit that inserts into the BamA pore.
,

Importantly, atypical OMP architecture is not restricted to
variations
in BAM composition or biogenesis pathways. Although most characterized
OMPs form individual β-barrels, structural and bioinformatic
analyses have identified OMPs containing multiple β-barrel domains
within a single polypeptide, expanding the known architectural diversity
of OM β-barrels. Other OMPs form
higher order oligomeric assemblies in which multiple small β-barrel
subunits collectively generate a larger membrane-spanning pore. For
example, the E. coli curli secretion protein CsgG
comprises nine approximately four-stranded protomers that assemble
into a 36-stranded β-barrel secretion channel.
−

 More recently, the pentameric PopA from the predatory bacterium Bdellovibrio bacteriovorous was shown to form an unusual
porin-like oligomeric architecture associated with lipid trapping,
further illustrating the structural diversity of β-barrel OMP
assemblies. Conversely, some OMPs depart
entirely from the canonical β-barrel fold. The E. coli polysaccharide export protein Wza, for example, forms a large α-helical
transmembrane barrel with a central cavity that accommodates the passage
of assembled capsular polysaccharide. Collectively, these examples demonstrate that OMPs encompass a broader
range of architectures than the canonical single β-barrel paradigm,
including multibarrel proteins, oligomeric β-barrel assemblies,
and entirely α-helical transmembrane structures. This structural
diversity likely necessitates corresponding adaptations in the pathways
responsible for OMP targeting, assembly, and function across diverse
diderm bacterial lineages.

6.2.3LPS-OMP Interactions and OrganizationDirect interactions between OMPs and LPS have been identified in
several systems. In E. coli, interactions between
the OmpC porin and LPS have been proposed to facilitate OmpC insertion
into the OM, suggesting that LPS contributes to both OMP biogenesis
and OM organization.
−

 The ferrichrome receptor FhuA (a 22-stranded
β-barrel) co-crystallized with a bound LPS molecule. The FhuA-LPS contacts occur primarily through
electrostatic interactions between the phosphate residues of lipid
A and Kdo I and II sugar moieties and basic, positively charged amino
acids of FhuA. This FhuA structure revealed
a conserved LPS-binding motif consisting of four basic amino acids,
which was identified in other prokaryotic and eukaryotic LPS-binding
proteins. Solution NMR spectroscopy
demonstrated that P. aeruginosa OprH directly binds
LPS through predominantly electrostatic interactions localized to
extracellular loops 2 and 3.
,
 The OM protease OmpT
requires LPS as an essential cofactor for catalytic activity; the
crystal structure identifies a putative LPS-binding site containing
conserved basic residues (Arg138, Arg175, Lys226).
,

LPS forms a tightly integrated supramolecular network with
OMPs. Molecular dynamics simulations reveal that each OMP has a unique
pattern of interactions with surrounding LPS molecules, determined
by the protein’s composition, extracellular domain architecture,
and positioning of residues at the membrane interface (referred to
as “LPS fingerprint”).
,,
 Both OMPs and LPS molecules are proposed to have
limited lateral mobility once inserted into the OM. Furthermore, the
OMPs and LPS segregate into clusters, forming an ordered protein–lipid
lattice that provides structural integrity and mechanical strength.
,
 OmpA plays a critical role in organizing this lattice by remaining
immobile through anchoring to the PG and strongly interacting with
surrounding OMPs via LPS bridges.

LPS-OMP Interactions and Organization

Direct interactions between OMPs and LPS have been identified in
several systems. In E. coli, interactions between
the OmpC porin and LPS have been proposed to facilitate OmpC insertion
into the OM, suggesting that LPS contributes to both OMP biogenesis
and OM organization.
−

 The ferrichrome receptor FhuA (a 22-stranded
β-barrel) co-crystallized with a bound LPS molecule. The FhuA-LPS contacts occur primarily through
electrostatic interactions between the phosphate residues of lipid
A and Kdo I and II sugar moieties and basic, positively charged amino
acids of FhuA. This FhuA structure revealed
a conserved LPS-binding motif consisting of four basic amino acids,
which was identified in other prokaryotic and eukaryotic LPS-binding
proteins. Solution NMR spectroscopy
demonstrated that P. aeruginosa OprH directly binds
LPS through predominantly electrostatic interactions localized to
extracellular loops 2 and 3.
,
 The OM protease OmpT
requires LPS as an essential cofactor for catalytic activity; the
crystal structure identifies a putative LPS-binding site containing
conserved basic residues (Arg138, Arg175, Lys226).
,

LPS forms a tightly integrated supramolecular network with
OMPs. Molecular dynamics simulations reveal that each OMP has a unique
pattern of interactions with surrounding LPS molecules, determined
by the protein’s composition, extracellular domain architecture,
and positioning of residues at the membrane interface (referred to
as “LPS fingerprint”).
,,
 Both OMPs and LPS molecules are proposed to have
limited lateral mobility once inserted into the OM. Furthermore, the
OMPs and LPS segregate into clusters, forming an ordered protein–lipid
lattice that provides structural integrity and mechanical strength.
,
 OmpA plays a critical role in organizing this lattice by remaining
immobile through anchoring to the PG and strongly interacting with
surrounding OMPs via LPS bridges.

6.2.4OM Lipoprotein BiogenesisOM lipoproteins
have diverse cellular functions, and many are virulence factors.
,
 Lipoproteins are matured through a post-translational lipoprotein-processing
pathway in the IM as described in Section
 (Figure
). Following translocation across the IM, lipidation,
and signal sequence cleavage, lipoproteins that lack IM retention
signals (an Asp residue in the 2 position) are selectively transported
to the OM through the localization of lipoproteins (Lol) pathway (Figure
). This process is initiated by the ABC transporter complex
LolCDE, embedded in the IM. Powered by ATP hydrolysis, LolCDE extracts
mature lipoproteins from the IM and transfers them to the periplasmic
chaperone LolA. LolA shields the hydrophobic
lipid moieties of lipoproteins and transports them across the aqueous
periplasm.
,
 The LolA-lipoprotein complex
subsequently interacts with LolB, an OM receptor that facilitates
lipoprotein insertion into the inner leaflet of the OM. LolB exhibits
a higher affinity for lipoproteins than LolA, promoting an efficient
transfer and release of the substrate.
,
 Although
LolA and LolB share structurally similar hydrophobic cavities, differences
in amino acid composition contribute to their differential binding
affinities and functional roles.
,

In
addition to lipoprotein trafficking, the OM must maintain a highly
asymmetric lipid composition in which glycerophospholipids are predominantly
restricted to the inner leaflet.
,
 The maintenance
of this asymmetry is mediated in part by the maintenance of lipid
asymmetry (Mla) pathway, which spans the IM, periplasm, and OM. The Mla system comprises the IM ABC transporter
MlaFEDB, the periplasmic lipid-binding protein MlaC, and the OM lipoprotein
MlaA, which associates with OmpC or OmpF.
−

 Current evidence supports a primary role for Mla in retrograde glycerophospholipid
transport, whereby mislocalized glycerophospholipids are extracted
from the OM by MlaA, transferred to MlaC, and shuttled across the
periplasm to MlaFEDB for reinsertion into the IM.
,,
 Structural and biochemical studies
have provided mechanistic insight into this process, demonstrating
that MlaFEDB can bind and translocate phospholipids through its central
cavity.
,
 Although Mla-mediated anterograde glycerophospholipid
transport from the IM toward the OM has also been proposed, the physiological
directionality and contribution of this activity remain debated. Thus,
Mla is best characterized as a lipid trafficking pathway that helps
maintain OM lipid asymmetry by removing and recycling aberrantly localized
glycerophospholipids.
,

Following insertion into
the inner leaflet of the OM by LolB, a
subset of lipoproteins are translocated to the outer leaflet or cell
surface through dedicated secretion pathways, including the surface
lipoprotein assembly modulator (Slam), type II secretion system (T2SS),
and the type V secretion system (T5SS) (see Section
).
−

 For example, the pullulanase
lipoprotein from Klebsiella oxytoca is a substrate
of T2SS, whereas the lipidated autotransporter NalP from N.
meningitidis is secreted through the T5SS.
,
 Together, these pathways illustrate the diverse mechanisms by which
diderm bacteria traffic lipoproteins from their site of synthesis
at the IM to distinct destinations within or beyond the OM.

OM Lipoprotein Biogenesis

OM lipoproteins
have diverse cellular functions, and many are virulence factors.
,
 Lipoproteins are matured through a post-translational lipoprotein-processing
pathway in the IM as described in Section
 (Figure
). Following translocation across the IM, lipidation,
and signal sequence cleavage, lipoproteins that lack IM retention
signals (an Asp residue in the 2 position) are selectively transported
to the OM through the localization of lipoproteins (Lol) pathway (Figure
). This process is initiated by the ABC transporter complex
LolCDE, embedded in the IM. Powered by ATP hydrolysis, LolCDE extracts
mature lipoproteins from the IM and transfers them to the periplasmic
chaperone LolA. LolA shields the hydrophobic
lipid moieties of lipoproteins and transports them across the aqueous
periplasm.
,
 The LolA-lipoprotein complex
subsequently interacts with LolB, an OM receptor that facilitates
lipoprotein insertion into the inner leaflet of the OM. LolB exhibits
a higher affinity for lipoproteins than LolA, promoting an efficient
transfer and release of the substrate.
,
 Although
LolA and LolB share structurally similar hydrophobic cavities, differences
in amino acid composition contribute to their differential binding
affinities and functional roles.
,

In
addition to lipoprotein trafficking, the OM must maintain a highly
asymmetric lipid composition in which glycerophospholipids are predominantly
restricted to the inner leaflet.
,
 The maintenance
of this asymmetry is mediated in part by the maintenance of lipid
asymmetry (Mla) pathway, which spans the IM, periplasm, and OM. The Mla system comprises the IM ABC transporter
MlaFEDB, the periplasmic lipid-binding protein MlaC, and the OM lipoprotein
MlaA, which associates with OmpC or OmpF.
−

 Current evidence supports a primary role for Mla in retrograde glycerophospholipid
transport, whereby mislocalized glycerophospholipids are extracted
from the OM by MlaA, transferred to MlaC, and shuttled across the
periplasm to MlaFEDB for reinsertion into the IM.
,,
 Structural and biochemical studies
have provided mechanistic insight into this process, demonstrating
that MlaFEDB can bind and translocate phospholipids through its central
cavity.
,
 Although Mla-mediated anterograde glycerophospholipid
transport from the IM toward the OM has also been proposed, the physiological
directionality and contribution of this activity remain debated. Thus,
Mla is best characterized as a lipid trafficking pathway that helps
maintain OM lipid asymmetry by removing and recycling aberrantly localized
glycerophospholipids.
,

Following insertion into
the inner leaflet of the OM by LolB, a
subset of lipoproteins are translocated to the outer leaflet or cell
surface through dedicated secretion pathways, including the surface
lipoprotein assembly modulator (Slam), type II secretion system (T2SS),
and the type V secretion system (T5SS) (see Section
).
−

 For example, the pullulanase
lipoprotein from Klebsiella oxytoca is a substrate
of T2SS, whereas the lipidated autotransporter NalP from N.
meningitidis is secreted through the T5SS.
,
 Together, these pathways illustrate the diverse mechanisms by which
diderm bacteria traffic lipoproteins from their site of synthesis
at the IM to distinct destinations within or beyond the OM.

6.3OM in Pathogenesis6.3.1OMPs in PathogenesisThe OM of
diderm bacteria is a crucial permeability barrier that protects against
components of the innate immune response, antibiotics, and other antimicrobials.
,
 β-Barrel OMPs are key for the membrane’s integrity
and selective permeability. They perform several essential tasks,
including membrane biogenesis, cell signaling, protein import and
export, and the transport of toxins and metabolites. In addition,
they act as virulence factors in pathogenic bacterial strains. OMPs in diderm bacteria are crucial for survival
and pathogenicity. The OmpA family has
several different functions and provides structural stability, connecting
the OM to the PG layer via interactions with Braun’s lipoprotein.
,
 OmpA has an unusual domain organization in which an N-terminal eight-stranded
β-barrel spans the OM and a C-terminal periplasmic domain binds
PG, making it one of the few OMPs with a large functional domain on
the periplasmic face. Without OmpA,
the OM loses mechanical cohesion, making the bacteria more susceptible
to environmental stress and potentially affecting their ability to
cause infections.
,
 OmpA also contributes to host–pathogen
interactions through both direct and indirect mechanisms. E. coli K1 OmpA mediates invasion of brain microvascular
endothelial cells to facilitate meningitis,
Klebsiella pneumoniae OmpA attenuates immune evasion in vitro and in vivo; however, these effects
appear to involve downstream host signaling and bacterial defense
mechanisms rather than a single defined direct receptor-mediated interaction. Enterohemorrhagic E. coli O157:H7
OmpA induces dendritic cell cytokine production, although the contribution
of other bacterial surface components to this response cannot be excluded. Collectively, these findings indicate that
OmpA influences pathogenesis through a combination of direct interactions
with host cells and indirect effects arising from its structural and
regulatory roles in the OM.Beyond the OmpA family, other OMPs
mediate host interactions. The multivalent adhesion molecule (MAM)
binds fibronectin and phosphatidic acid on host membranes, thereby
stabilizing attachment. The phase-variable
opacity-associated (Opa) proteins of N. gonorrhoeae and N. meningitidis are 8-stranded β-barrel
proteins that bind to carcinoembryonic antigen-related cell adhesion
molecules (CEACAMs) and heparan sulfate proteoglycans (HSPGs) on epithelial
cells, neutrophils, and lymphocytes. Opa proteins engage multiple
CEACAM receptors (CEACAM1, 3, 5, and 6), facilitating bacterial adhesion
and invasion and modulating both innate and adaptive immune responses.
−


 Opa binding to CEACAM1 on epithelial cells promotes colonization
and prevents epithelial shedding, while interaction with CEACAM3 on
neutrophils enhances phagocytosis and bacterial killing.

H. pylori express an
exceptionally large family
of OMPs, with approximately 30 hop (Helicobacter
OMP) genes encoding adhesins that bind to diverse host glycan and
protein receptors. Hop proteins are
8-stranded OMPs with a large extracellular domain (varying between
15-108 kDa) inserted in the first extracellular loop. Three examples of Hop proteins are the blood
group antigen-binding adhesin (BabA), sialic acid-binding adhesin
(SabA), and Helicobacter OMP Q (HopQ). BabA binds Lewis b and related
ABO blood group antigens on gastric epithelial cells.
,
 SabA binds to sialyl-dimeric-Lewis x glycosphingolipid, which facilitates
persistent colonization during chronic infection. HopQ binds to CEACAMs on gastric epithelial cells and promotes
adherence.
Trimeric autotransporter
adhesins (TAAs; also known as type Vc
secretion systems) represent another functionally diverse OMP family,
with the prototype YadA from Yersinia enterocolitica. YadA forms a homotrimeric and facilitates bacterial adhesion and
confers autoagglutination, serum resistance, and protection from phagocytosis.
−

 The closely related N. meningitidis adhesin NadA
binds β1 integrins to mediate epithelial cell adhesion and invasion.
,
 NadA is one of the four protein components of the Bexsero®
vaccine. TAAs vary significantly in
the size of their extracellular domains. For example, the TAA AtaA,
from Acinetobacter, is 3630 amino acid and forms
a fibrous structure that is more than 1 MDa and 260 nm long.
Intimin and invasin are both inverse
autotransporters (Type Ve
secretion system), comprising an N-terminal β-barrel translocation
unit embedded in the OM and a C-terminal extracellular passenger domain
that mediates host adhesion (see Section
). Intimin,
expressed by enteropathogenic and enterohemorrhagic E. coli, presents its passenger domain to engage the translocated intimin
receptor (Tir), which the bacterium delivers into the host cell membrane
via the T3SS, as well as host nucleolin and β1 integrins in Tir-independent interactions. Invasin, expressed by Yersinia species, similarly projects its C-terminal passenger
domain to bind multiple β1 integrin family members (α3β1,
α4β1, α5β1, α6β1, αvβ1)
with picomolar affinity, triggering bacterial internalization through
a zipper-like mechanism
,
 and proinflammatory
cytokine production.
,

6.3.2LPS in PathogenesisLPS is recognized
by the host innate immune system primarily by the TLR4-myeloid differentiation
factor (MD-2) signaling complex.
,
 LPS has substantial
antigenic diversity through modifications such as altered acylation
and phosphorylation patterns, unusual glycosidic linkages, and the
incorporation of rare monosaccharides.
,
 This structural
heterogeneity directly affects host immune recognition and susceptibility
to immune-mediated clearance. Chemical variations within the O-antigen
and core oligosaccharide regions can alter the bacterial sensitivity
to complement-mediated killing and antibody recognition. However,
chemical variations in lipid A enable pathogens to evade innate immune
detection. In particular, modifications
to lipid A, such as reduced acylation or altered phosphate group decoration,
can attenuate TLR4 activation and thereby diminish host inflammatory
responses and promote bacterial persistence.
,

Beyond immunological functions, LPS also serves as a critical
structural component of the OM by contributing to the membrane integrity
and permeability control. The densely packed and highly negatively
charged O layer shields the bacterium from environmental stressors,
including detergents, bile salts, and antimicrobial compounds. Hydrophobic antibiotics such as macrolides,
rifamycin, novobiocin, and fusidic acid preferentially partition into
the lipid A domain of LPS but are severely impeded by the tight packing
and low membrane fluidity imposed by its saturated acyl chains. The acylation state of lipid A and sugar composition
of the O-antigen influence the overall permeability of the OM and
antibiotic susceptibility.
The
essential role of LPS in bacterial viability and its strong
immunogenic properties have made it a target for vaccine development.
Certain modified forms of LPS have been used in the design of live-attenuated
and glycoconjugate vaccines due to their ability to stimulate protective
antibody responses. However, these development
efforts have been complicated by the extensive structural variability
of LPS among bacterial serotypes, which has hindered the achievement
of broad spectrum protection.
,

6.3.3OM Lipoprotein in PathogenesisOM lipoproteins in diderm bacteria play direct roles in host–pathogen
interactions. Surface lipoproteins of pathogenic bacteria have a wide
variety of functions, ranging from metal scavenging and acquisition
to hijacking of host cellular machinery. A few examples are described
to illustrate the different roles that lipoproteins play in bacterial
pathogenesis. TbpB is a surface lipoprotein that is involved in iron
acquisition in many pathogenic diderm bacteria such as N.
meningitidis, Moraxella bovis, and Actinobacillus suis. TbpB binds to host iron transport proteins,
such as transferrin. OspE in B. burgdorferi binds to an inhibitor of the alternative
complement pathway of the immune system, Factor H (FH). OspE binds and recruits FH to the bacterial
cell surface, thereby disrupting the complement cascade and evading
complement-mediated cell lysis.
6.3.4OM Vesicles in PathogenesisOMVs
are spherical nanostructures that originate from blebbing of the OM
and play a significant role in bacterial pathogenicity. These vesicles contain proteins, lipids, and
LPS that can modulate host responses far away from the bacteria of
origin. OMVs facilitate the dissemination
of virulence factors, enabling bacteria to deliver toxins, proteases,
and phospholipases to host cells without requiring direct bacterial–host
interaction.
,
 For example, P. aeruginosa OMVs contain enzymes such as protease, phospholipase C, alkaline
phosphatase, and proelastase, all of which contribute to host tissue
damage and the establishment of infection. OMVs also function as “immune decoys” that absorb
and neutralize antimicrobial peptides, thereby reducing the efficacy
of antimicrobial treatments against pathogenic bacterial populations.
,
 Some pathogens use OMVs to modulate host immune responses by selectively
up- or downregulating inflammation depending on OMV cargo and the
infection stage.

6.3.4OM Vesicles in PathogenesisOMVs
are spherical nanostructures that originate from blebbing of the OM

LPS that can modulate host responses far away from the bacteria of
origin. OMVs facilitate the dissemination
of virulence factors, enabling bacteria to deliver toxins, proteases,
and phospholipases to host cells without requiring direct bacterial–host
interaction.

phosphatase, and proelastase, all of which contribute to host tissue
damage and the establishment of infection. OMVs also function as “immune decoys” that absorb
and neutralize antimicrobial peptides, thereby reducing the efficacy
of antimicrobial treatments against pathogenic bacterial populations.

 Some pathogens use OMVs to modulate host immune responses by selectively
up- or downregulating inflammation depending on OMV cargo and the
infection stage.

6.4OM Therapeutic Targets6.4.1OM Antibiotic TargetsThe OM of
diderm bacteria has many therapeutic targets across the three components:
LPS, OMPs, and surface-exposed lipoproteins. Essential components
and enzymes critical to their synthesis are viable antibiotic targets.
LPS, surface OMPs, and OMV-packaged antigens are directly accessible
to host immune surveillance, making them central to successful vaccine
adjuvant platforms.
LPS biosynthesis
involves multiple enzymatic steps, none of which have mammalian counterparts.
Enzymes that synthesize lipid A are essential for the survival of
most diderm bacteria. LpxC is the most intensely pursued target. Multiple
inhibitors have demonstrated potent antibacterial activity in preclinical
models. LpxH is also a promising target.
Inhibitors with in vivo activity against wild-type Enterobacterales are reported to suggest that LpxH is a
viable next-generation target for multidrug-resistant diderm infections. The Lpt transenvelope complex (LptABCDEFG)
is structurally well-characterized, and competitive inhibitors of
LptA-LptC and LptD-LptE interactions have been identified. Although
preliminary, these inhibitors provide proof of concept for blocking
LPS insertion as a bactericidal strategy. A clinical candidate, zosurabalpin (RG6006), derived from tethered
macrocyclic peptide antibiotics with potent antibacterial activity
against carbapenem-resistant A. baumannii that (in
phase 3 trials, NCT05614895) inhibits the LptB2FGC complex
and blocks the transport of bacterial lipopolysaccharide from the
inner membrane to the outer membrane.
,
 The BAM complex
represents another OM target with several bactericidal inhibitors
identified.
−


 However, a potential resistance to BamA-targeting inhibitors can
arise through the upregulation of BamK, a BamA paralog in some bacteria.
,,

6.4.2OM Vaccine TargetsThe OM has many
surface components already used in vaccines. Lipid A derivatives as
adjuvants, OM proteins as antigens, and OM vesicles as multicomponent
antigen-delivery platforms used effectively to generate vaccines.
To use lipid A as an adjuvant, isolated lipid A is chemically detoxified
through a two-step process of acid and base hydrolysis, which remove
specific phosphate and acyl groups to produce 3-O-desacyl-4’-monophosphoryl
lipid A (3D-MPLA). 3D-MPLA powerfully
activates TLR4 and avoids the inflammatory toxicity seen with native
LPS. 3D-MPLA is the active immunostimulatory component of the AS01
adjuvant system, which is incorporated in Shingrix (recombinant zoster
vaccine), RTS, S/AS01E (malaria vaccine), and an approved RSV vaccine.
AS01 triggers a rapid innate immune response that in turn shapes durable,
antigen-specific T-cell and antibody responses, and this effect holds
regardless of a recipient’s pre-existing immune status. The four components of the meningococcal serogroup
B vaccine 4CMenB (Bexsero, GSK) consist entirely of OMPs, lipoproteins,
and OMVs: the lipoproteins factor H binding protein (fHbp) and Neisseria
heparin-binding antigen (NHBA), the OMP NadA (a TAA OMP), and an OMV
incorporating the OMP PorA. The 4CMenB
vaccine effectiveness against invasive MenB disease is 71–95%,
with cross-protection observed against non-B serogroups and moderate
cross-protection against N. gonorrhoeae in adolescents. The extracellular domains of TAAs are antigens
in several other vaccines. For example, pertactin and filamentous
hemagglutinin (from Bordetella pertussis) are TAA
antigens for acellular pertussis (aP) vaccines including DTaP. More broadly, OMVs are an increasingly utilized
vaccine platform: naturally produced or engineered OMVs can carry
LPS, PG fragments, lipoproteins, and OMPs and provide intrinsic adjuvant
activity. The ability to genetically
engineer OMV cargo and to detoxify or modify the LPS has expanded
their application across diverse bacterial pathogens in preclinical
and early clinical development.

OM Therapeutic Targets

6.4.1OM Antibiotic TargetsThe OM of
diderm bacteria has many therapeutic targets across the three components:
LPS, OMPs, and surface-exposed lipoproteins. Essential components
and enzymes critical to their synthesis are viable antibiotic targets.
LPS, surface OMPs, and OMV-packaged antigens are directly accessible
to host immune surveillance, making them central to successful vaccine
adjuvant platforms.
LPS biosynthesis
involves multiple enzymatic steps, none of which have mammalian counterparts.
Enzymes that synthesize lipid A are essential for the survival of
most diderm bacteria. LpxC is the most intensely pursued target. Multiple
inhibitors have demonstrated potent antibacterial activity in preclinical
models. LpxH is also a promising target.
Inhibitors with in vivo activity against wild-type Enterobacterales are reported to suggest that LpxH is a
viable next-generation target for multidrug-resistant diderm infections. The Lpt transenvelope complex (LptABCDEFG)
is structurally well-characterized, and competitive inhibitors of
LptA-LptC and LptD-LptE interactions have been identified. Although
preliminary, these inhibitors provide proof of concept for blocking
LPS insertion as a bactericidal strategy. A clinical candidate, zosurabalpin (RG6006), derived from tethered
macrocyclic peptide antibiotics with potent antibacterial activity
against carbapenem-resistant A. baumannii that (in
phase 3 trials, NCT05614895) inhibits the LptB2FGC complex
and blocks the transport of bacterial lipopolysaccharide from the
inner membrane to the outer membrane.
,
 The BAM complex
represents another OM target with several bactericidal inhibitors
identified.
−


 However, a potential resistance to BamA-targeting inhibitors can
arise through the upregulation of BamK, a BamA paralog in some bacteria.
,,

B vaccine 4CMenB (Bexsero, GSK) consist entirely of OMPs, lipoproteins,
