7Overview of the Components and Organization
of Mycobacterial Cell EnvelopeMycobacteria, belonging to
the Terrabacteria clade
within the Actinobacteria phylum, possess one of the most distinctive
and pathogenically relevant cell envelopes among bacteria.
,
 The mycobacterial OM (MOM) (Figure
) is different from that of diderm bacteria.
,
 Mycolic acids (long-chain fatty acids consisting of approximately
70-90 carbons) coat the inner leaflet, which constitutes approximately
30% of the cell envelope dry weight.
,
 The mycolic
acid leaflet is covalently linked to arabinogalactan, which is in
turn covalently attached to PG, forming the mycolyl-arabinogalactan-PG
(mAGP) complex (Figure
).
,
 This covalent integration, which is unique
among bacteria, creates an impenetrable barrier. The mAGP complex
confers antibiotic resistance and is crucial to viability; mutations
reduce mycolic acid content, alter colony morphology, decrease persistence
in host cells, or create unstable OMs with altered drug permeability. Complete loss of mycolic acids is lethal to Mycobacterium tuberculosis but not lethal to the closely
related Corynebacterium species, the specialized
adaptations mycobacteria have undergone. The outer leaflet contains loosely-packed phthiocerol dimycocerosate
(PDIM), trehalose dimycolate (TDM, or cord factor), trehalose monomycolate
(TMM), and other complex glycolipids (Figure
).
8
Mycomembrane
biogenesis. The mycobacterial cell envelope
is organized in concentric layers: the IM, the arabinogalactan layer,
and an outer mycomembrane composed of trehalose dimycolate (TDM) and
mycolic acids, topped by an outermost protein layer. Mycolic acid
biosynthesis is initiated in the cytoplasm by Pks13/CmrA, which condenses
mycolic acid (MA) precursors to produce trehalose monomycolate (TMM).
TMM is transported across the IM by the MmpL3 transporter and serves
as the mycolic acid donor for transfer reactions in the arabinogalactan
layer, generating TDM and arabinogalactan mycolates (AGM). The Ag85ABC
complex facilitates additional mycolic acid transfer steps within
the arabinogalactan layer. The LpqY-SugABC system imports trehalose
(Tre) from the mycomembrane back to the cytoplasm to sustain TMM synthesis.
PE (proline-glutamate) and PPE (proline–proline–glutamate)
surface proteins are secreted via the Type VII secretion system (T7SS),
whose machinery includes the membrane-spanning EccB subunit and the
surface-associated protease MycP. The cytoplasmic chaperone EspG binds
a conserved hydrophobic motif in the N-terminal domain of the PPE
protein and directs the PE/PPE heterodimeric complex to the cognate
ESX translocon for translocation across the IM as a heterodimer. Created
in https://BioRender.com.Porins in the mycomembrane facilitate the diffusion
of hydrophilic
nutrients across the mycobacterial OM, which is otherwise highly impermeable
because of its mycolic acid content. MspA, the major porin of Mycobacterium smegmatis, is the first structurally characterized
mycobacterial OMP and is homooctameric with a single central channel
approximately 9.6 nm in length that facilitates the passive diffusion
of small, hydrophilic solutes.
,
 OmpATb specifically
facilitates permeability to small water-soluble nutrients such as
serine and plays a role in enabling M. tuberculosis to respond to acidic pH conditions.
Several other types of proteins associate with the mycobacterium
that are not β-barrel OMPs. In Corynebacterium glutamicum, PorA and PorH form hetero-oligomeric channels essential for nutrient
uptake.
,
 PorA requires mycoloylation, a post-translational
modification unique to Corynebacteriales, for proper
channel function, and both proteins are co-transcribed and must associate
to form functional pores in the mycomembrane. Mycobacterial species have a set of proteins (Proline-glutamate
(PE) and proline–proline glutamate (PPE) proteins) that non-covalently
associate with the outer leaflet of the mycomembrane and form non-specific
channels that allow small molecule nutrients to permeate the mycomembrane.7.1Mycomembrane Biogenesis7.1.1Mycolic Acid Biosynthesis, Transport, and
Mycomembrane AssemblyMycolic acid biosynthesis proceeds
through a sequential two-stage fatty acid synthesis pathway unique
to mycobacteria. The multifunctional eukaryotic-type FAS-I enzyme
first synthesizes medium-chain fatty acids (C16–C26) from acetyl-CoA. These products are then elongated by the discrete,
ACP-dependent FAS-II system, composed of monofunctional enzymes including
the condensing enzymes KasA and KasB, the dehydratases HadAB and HadBC,
the ketoreductase MabA, and the enoyl-ACP reductase InhA. The final
elongated products are meromycolic acids of C40–C60 length.
,
 Unlike FAS-II systems in other bacteria, the mycobacterial FAS-II
cannot initiate de novo synthesis and is dedicated solely to elongation
of FAS-I products. The meromycolic acids
are subsequently modified by S-adenosylmethionine-dependent methyltransferases
that introduce cyclopropane rings, methyl branches, and oxygenated
functionalities defining the three major mycolic acid classes: alpha-,
keto-, and methoxymycolates.
,
 Final condensation
of the meromycolate chain with a C24–C26 fatty acid derived
from FAS-I is catalyzed by the polyketide synthase Pks13, which performs
a Claisen-type condensation and thioesterase-mediated release to yield
the mature mycolic acid.
,

Following synthesis
at the cytoplasmic membrane, mature mycolic acids must be transported
across the IM and arabinogalactan layer to assemble the mycomembrane’s
outer leaflet. This flipping step is mediated primarily by MmpL3,
an IM transporter, which exports mycolic acids in the form of TMM. Cryo-EM and X-ray structures of MmpL3 have
revealed a proton motive force-driven transport mechanism, with a
flexible linker between the two transmembrane subdomains implicated
in the translocation process. Once TMM
reaches the periplasm and arabinogalactan layer, the mycolyltransferases
Ag85A, Ag85B, and Ag85C (serine esterases of the carboxylesterase
family) covalently attach mycolic acids from TMM to arabinogalactan
termini to form arabinogalactan mycolates (AGMs) or to a second TMM
molecule to generate trehalose dimycolate (TDM).
,
 The spatial coordination of this process is regulated in part by
the periplasmic protein PgfA, which interacts with MmpL3 and influences
the polar localization of TMM transport, thereby linking mycomembrane
assembly to the asymmetric growth program of mycobacteria.
7.1.2Mycomembrane-Associated Protein BiogenesisProteins associated with or inserted into the mycomembrane face
unique biogenesis requirements as they must navigate through the IM,
periplasm, and mAGP complex to reach the mycomembrane. With respect
to OMP biogenesis, it remains unknown how mycomembrane OMPs are inserted
into the mycomembrane; a BAM homologue has not been identified.Proline–glutamate (PE) and proline–proline glutamate
(PPE) proteins are transported across the mycobacterial IM via Type
VII (ESX) secretion systems and associate to the outer surface of
the mycomembrane (see Section
). PE/PPE proteins form heterodimeric
complexes (Figure
) that are recognized by system-specific cytoplasmic chaperones called
EspG.
,
 The EspG chaperones bind to a conserved
hydrophobic hh motif in the PPE protein’s N-terminal domain,
preventing aggregation of the PE/PPE complex and directing it to the
cognate ESX translocon (ESX-1, ESX-3, or ESX-5) for translocation
across the IM.
,
 The EspG-binding domain on PPE
proteins serves as the primary determinant of the system specificity.
Swapping the EspG1-binding domain for the EspG5-binding domain reroutes
substrates from ESX-1 to ESX-5. Following
translocation, PE/PPE surface proteins localize to and become anchored
to the mycomembrane OM, where they remain surface-associated.
−

 While the precise molecular mechanism of membrane anchoring remains
unknown, once localized to the mycomembrane, PE/PPE proteins form
small-molecule channels that facilitate the uptake of essential small-molecule
substrates across the hydrophobic mycobacterial OM barrier.
,,

7.2Mycomembrane in PathogenesisSurface-exposed
PE/PPE proteins represent a large and functionally diverse protein
family that plays critical roles in nutrient acquisition and host–pathogen
interactions in mycobacteria. PPE51 (Rv3136) functions as a gateway
for trehalose uptake across the mycomembrane. Deletion of the ppe51 gene prevents bacterial growth on trehalose, maltose,
and lactose as sole carbon sources, but maintains bacterial growth
on glucose and glycerol.
,
 In M. smegmatis, the PE9/PE10 heterodimer is a unique example of PE/PE protein interaction,
which deviates from the typical PE/PPE interaction more commonly found. This surface-localized complex interacts with
TLR4 on macrophage and triggers the TRIF pathway and enhances interferon-β
secretion. PPE36 and PPE62 are involved
in heme-iron acquisition. PPE36 modulates
host inflammatory responses by promoting ubiquitination and degradation
of a signal transducer for the immune system. PPE38 is required for the secretion of all PE and PPE proteins
via the ESX-5 system (Figure
 and  and Section
), with loss-of-function mutations in ppe38 preventing the secretion of these protein families.
Mycobacterial glycolipids are critical
virulence factors with dedicated immune evasion functions.
,
 Trehalose dimycolate, the most abundant glycolipid in M.
tuberculosis, directly interacts with C-type lectin receptors
on macrophages. This interaction impedes phagosome acidification and
promotes granuloma formation, both of which facilitate bacterial persistence. Phthiocerol dimycocerosate facilitates escape
from phagolysosomes and contributes to virulence; its absence in bacterial
strains reduces the pathogenicity. The hydrophobic mycolic acid layer
facilitates the release of immunomodulatory lipids and also a high
degree of impermeability.
7.3Mycomembrane Therapeutic Targets7.3.1Mycomembrane Antibiotic TargetsThe mycobacterial cell envelope is a clinically relevant bacterial
surface for the development of antibiotics and vaccines. The global
burden of tuberculosis motivates therapeutic development, and the
unique structural properties of the mycomembrane provide therapeutic
targets specific to M. tuberculosis. The biosynthetic
enzymes and transporters that assemble this barrier are critical for M. tuberculosis viability and provide multiple therapeutic
targets.
,

Ethambutol targets the
arabinosyltransferases (EmbB and EmbC), which catalyze arabinosylation
of arabinogalactan, yet resistance is emerging. The cryo-EM structure of M. smegmatis EmbB
confirmed that resistance mutations cluster around the putative active
site, consistent with direct drug-protein interaction. The antigen 85 complex (Ag85A, Ag85B, Ag85C),
is another validated cell wall target. Cyclipostins and cyclophostin
analogues bind covalently to the catalytic serine of Ag85C and inhibit
both mycolyltransferase and triacylglycerol synthase activities, and
reduce trehalose dimycolate synthesis and arabinogalactan mycolylation
in culture. The FAD-dependent oxidase
(DprE1) that contributes to the synthesis of arabinogalactan was identified
as the target of benzothiazinones (BTZs) through a genetic and biochemical
screen. BTZ043 kills M. tuberculosis
in vitro, ex vivo, and in murine tuberculosis
(TB) models and was the first compound to establish DprE1 as a druggable
target. Subsequent optimization produced
PBTZ169 (macozinone) and a broad range of non-covalent inhibitor scaffolds. The recently approved BPaLM regimen is a cocktail
of bedaquiline, pretomanid, linezolid, and moxifloxacin. Pretomanid
targets cell wall synthesis indirectly by inhibiting cytochrome bd
oxidase of the electron-transport chain. Overall, specific inhibitors of mycobacterial cell envelope synthesis
are underdeveloped despite the promise of these inhibitors to increase
the accessibility of antibiotics across the mycomembrane.
7.3.2Mycomembrane Vaccine TargetsTB
vaccine development is an active area of bacterial vaccine research
globally, with 16 candidates currently in clinical evaluation and
four in active Phase 3 efficacy trials. The currently used Bacillus
Calmette-Guérin (BCG) vaccine provides inconsistent and incomplete
protection against adult pulmonary disease. The most advanced vaccine candidate is M72/AS01E, a recombinant
fusion protein derived from two M. tuberculosis antigens
(Mtb32A and Mtb39A, both involved in cell wall metabolism and persistence)
formulated with the AS01E adjuvant system containing 3D-MPLA (mentioned
in Section
) and QS-21 (a triterpene saponin that activates the immune system’s
antigen-presenting cells). In GSK’s Phase 2b trial, M72/AS01E
demonstrated approximately 50% protection against progression to active
pulmonary TB over three years without safety concerns, establishing
proof of concept for a protein subunit TB vaccine. A Phase 3 efficacy trial initiated in March 2024 and results
are anticipated within approximately five years. Surface-exposed PE/PPE proteins are a functionally diverse
antigen family with established immunogenicity in natural infection.
PPE18 is both a virulence factor and a potential vaccine antigen. Proteins secreted by the ESX-1 type VII secretion
system (specifically ESAT-6 and CFP-10) are among the most immunodominant M. tuberculosis T cell antigens and are included in multiple
subunit vaccine formulations. The inclusion of these proteins in next-generation
vaccines is expected to improve on BCG’s limited efficacy against
adult pulmonary disease.
,

Ag85A, Ag85B, and Ag85C (serine esterases of the carboxylesterase
family) covalently attach mycolic acids from TMM to arabinogalactan
termini to form arabinogalactan mycolates (AGMs) or to a second TMM
molecule to generate trehalose dimycolate (TDM).
,
 The spatial coordination of this process is regulated in part by
the periplasmic protein PgfA, which interacts with MmpL3 and influences
the polar localization of TMM transport, thereby linking mycomembrane
assembly to the asymmetric growth program of mycobacteria.
7.1.2Mycomembrane-Associated Protein BiogenesisProteins associated with or inserted into the mycomembrane face
unique biogenesis requirements as they must navigate through the IM,
periplasm, and mAGP complex to reach the mycomembrane. With respect
to OMP biogenesis, it remains unknown how mycomembrane OMPs are inserted
into the mycomembrane; a BAM homologue has not been identified.Proline–glutamate (PE) and proline–proline glutamate
(PPE) proteins are transported across the mycobacterial IM via Type
VII (ESX) secretion systems and associate to the outer surface of
the mycomembrane (see Section
). PE/PPE proteins form heterodimeric
complexes (Figure
) that are recognized by system-specific cytoplasmic chaperones called
EspG.
,
 The EspG chaperones bind to a conserved
hydrophobic hh motif in the PPE protein’s N-terminal domain,
preventing aggregation of the PE/PPE complex and directing it to the
cognate ESX translocon (ESX-1, ESX-3, or ESX-5) for translocation
across the IM.
,
 The EspG-binding domain on PPE
proteins serves as the primary determinant of the system specificity.
Swapping the EspG1-binding domain for the EspG5-binding domain reroutes
substrates from ESX-1 to ESX-5. Following
translocation, PE/PPE surface proteins localize to and become anchored
to the mycomembrane OM, where they remain surface-associated.
−

 While the precise molecular mechanism of membrane anchoring remains
unknown, once localized to the mycomembrane, PE/PPE proteins form
small-molecule channels that facilitate the uptake of essential small-molecule
substrates across the hydrophobic mycobacterial OM barrier.
,,

Mycomembrane Biogenesis

7.1.1Mycolic Acid Biosynthesis, Transport, and
Mycomembrane AssemblyMycolic acid biosynthesis proceeds
through a sequential two-stage fatty acid synthesis pathway unique
to mycobacteria. The multifunctional eukaryotic-type FAS-I enzyme
first synthesizes medium-chain fatty acids (C16–C26) from acetyl-CoA. These products are then elongated by the discrete,
ACP-dependent FAS-II system, composed of monofunctional enzymes including
the condensing enzymes KasA and KasB, the dehydratases HadAB and HadBC,
the ketoreductase MabA, and the enoyl-ACP reductase InhA. The final
elongated products are meromycolic acids of C40–C60 length.
,
 Unlike FAS-II systems in other bacteria, the mycobacterial FAS-II
cannot initiate de novo synthesis and is dedicated solely to elongation
of FAS-I products. The meromycolic acids
are subsequently modified by S-adenosylmethionine-dependent methyltransferases
that introduce cyclopropane rings, methyl branches, and oxygenated
functionalities defining the three major mycolic acid classes: alpha-,
keto-, and methoxymycolates.
,
 Final condensation
of the meromycolate chain with a C24–C26 fatty acid derived
from FAS-I is catalyzed by the polyketide synthase Pks13, which performs
a Claisen-type condensation and thioesterase-mediated release to yield
the mature mycolic acid.
,

Following synthesis
at the cytoplasmic membrane, mature mycolic acids must be transported
across the IM and arabinogalactan layer to assemble the mycomembrane’s
outer leaflet. This flipping step is mediated primarily by MmpL3,
an IM transporter, which exports mycolic acids in the form of TMM. Cryo-EM and X-ray structures of MmpL3 have
revealed a proton motive force-driven transport mechanism, with a
flexible linker between the two transmembrane subdomains implicated
in the translocation process. Once TMM
reaches the periplasm and arabinogalactan layer, the mycolyltransferases
Ag85A, Ag85B, and Ag85C (serine esterases of the carboxylesterase
family) covalently attach mycolic acids from TMM to arabinogalactan
termini to form arabinogalactan mycolates (AGMs) or to a second TMM
molecule to generate trehalose dimycolate (TDM).
,
 The spatial coordination of this process is regulated in part by
the periplasmic protein PgfA, which interacts with MmpL3 and influences
the polar localization of TMM transport, thereby linking mycomembrane
assembly to the asymmetric growth program of mycobacteria.

7.2Mycomembrane in PathogenesisSurface-exposed
PE/PPE proteins represent a large and functionally diverse protein
family that plays critical roles in nutrient acquisition and host–pathogen
interactions in mycobacteria. PPE51 (Rv3136) functions as a gateway
for trehalose uptake across the mycomembrane. Deletion of the ppe51 gene prevents bacterial growth on trehalose, maltose,
and lactose as sole carbon sources, but maintains bacterial growth
on glucose and glycerol.
,
 In M. smegmatis, the PE9/PE10 heterodimer is a unique example of PE/PE protein interaction,
which deviates from the typical PE/PPE interaction more commonly found. This surface-localized complex interacts with
TLR4 on macrophage and triggers the TRIF pathway and enhances interferon-β
secretion. PPE36 and PPE62 are involved
in heme-iron acquisition. PPE36 modulates
host inflammatory responses by promoting ubiquitination and degradation
of a signal transducer for the immune system. PPE38 is required for the secretion of all PE and PPE proteins
via the ESX-5 system (Figure
 and  and Section
), with loss-of-function mutations in ppe38 preventing the secretion of these protein families.
Mycobacterial glycolipids are critical
virulence factors with dedicated immune evasion functions.
,
 Trehalose dimycolate, the most abundant glycolipid in M.
tuberculosis, directly interacts with C-type lectin receptors
on macrophages. This interaction impedes phagosome acidification and
promotes granuloma formation, both of which facilitate bacterial persistence. Phthiocerol dimycocerosate facilitates escape
from phagolysosomes and contributes to virulence; its absence in bacterial
strains reduces the pathogenicity. The hydrophobic mycolic acid layer
facilitates the release of immunomodulatory lipids and also a high
degree of impermeability.

PE/PPE proteins represent a large and functionally diverse protein
family that plays critical roles in nutrient acquisition and host–pathogen
interactions in mycobacteria. PPE51 (Rv3136) functions as a gateway
for trehalose uptake across the mycomembrane. Deletion of the ppe51 gene prevents bacterial growth on trehalose, maltose,
and lactose as sole carbon sources, but maintains bacterial growth
on glucose and glycerol.
,

which deviates from the typical PE/PPE interaction more commonly found. This surface-localized complex interacts with
TLR4 on macrophage and triggers the TRIF pathway and enhances interferon-β
secretion. PPE36 and PPE62 are involved
in heme-iron acquisition. PPE36 modulates
host inflammatory responses by promoting ubiquitination and degradation
of a signal transducer for the immune system. PPE38 is required for the secretion of all PE and PPE proteins
via the ESX-5 system (Figure
 and  and Section
), with loss-of-function mutations in ppe38 preventing the secretion of these protein families.

Mycobacterial glycolipids are critical
virulence factors with dedicated immune evasion functions.
,

tuberculosis, directly interacts with C-type lectin receptors
on macrophages. This interaction impedes phagosome acidification and
promotes granuloma formation, both of which facilitate bacterial persistence. Phthiocerol dimycocerosate facilitates escape
from phagolysosomes and contributes to virulence; its absence in bacterial
strains reduces the pathogenicity. The hydrophobic mycolic acid layer

7.3Mycomembrane Therapeutic Targets7.3.1Mycomembrane Antibiotic TargetsThe mycobacterial cell envelope is a clinically relevant bacterial
surface for the development of antibiotics and vaccines. The global
burden of tuberculosis motivates therapeutic development, and the
unique structural properties of the mycomembrane provide therapeutic
targets specific to M. tuberculosis. The biosynthetic
enzymes and transporters that assemble this barrier are critical for M. tuberculosis viability and provide multiple therapeutic
targets.
,

Ethambutol targets the
arabinosyltransferases (EmbB and EmbC), which catalyze arabinosylation
of arabinogalactan, yet resistance is emerging. The cryo-EM structure of M. smegmatis EmbB
confirmed that resistance mutations cluster around the putative active
site, consistent with direct drug-protein interaction. The antigen 85 complex (Ag85A, Ag85B, Ag85C),
is another validated cell wall target. Cyclipostins and cyclophostin
analogues bind covalently to the catalytic serine of Ag85C and inhibit
both mycolyltransferase and triacylglycerol synthase activities, and
reduce trehalose dimycolate synthesis and arabinogalactan mycolylation
in culture. The FAD-dependent oxidase
(DprE1) that contributes to the synthesis of arabinogalactan was identified
as the target of benzothiazinones (BTZs) through a genetic and biochemical
screen. BTZ043 kills M. tuberculosis
in vitro, ex vivo, and in murine tuberculosis
(TB) models and was the first compound to establish DprE1 as a druggable
target. Subsequent optimization produced
PBTZ169 (macozinone) and a broad range of non-covalent inhibitor scaffolds. The recently approved BPaLM regimen is a cocktail
of bedaquiline, pretomanid, linezolid, and moxifloxacin. Pretomanid
targets cell wall synthesis indirectly by inhibiting cytochrome bd
oxidase of the electron-transport chain. Overall, specific inhibitors of mycobacterial cell envelope synthesis
are underdeveloped despite the promise of these inhibitors to increase
the accessibility of antibiotics across the mycomembrane.
7.3.2Mycomembrane Vaccine TargetsTB
vaccine development is an active area of bacterial vaccine research
globally, with 16 candidates currently in clinical evaluation and
four in active Phase 3 efficacy trials. The currently used Bacillus
Calmette-Guérin (BCG) vaccine provides inconsistent and incomplete
protection against adult pulmonary disease. The most advanced vaccine candidate is M72/AS01E, a recombinant
fusion protein derived from two M. tuberculosis antigens
(Mtb32A and Mtb39A, both involved in cell wall metabolism and persistence)
formulated with the AS01E adjuvant system containing 3D-MPLA (mentioned
in Section
) and QS-21 (a triterpene saponin that activates the immune system’s
antigen-presenting cells). In GSK’s Phase 2b trial, M72/AS01E
demonstrated approximately 50% protection against progression to active
pulmonary TB over three years without safety concerns, establishing
proof of concept for a protein subunit TB vaccine. A Phase 3 efficacy trial initiated in March 2024 and results
are anticipated within approximately five years. Surface-exposed PE/PPE proteins are a functionally diverse
antigen family with established immunogenicity in natural infection.
PPE18 is both a virulence factor and a potential vaccine antigen. Proteins secreted by the ESX-1 type VII secretion
system (specifically ESAT-6 and CFP-10) are among the most immunodominant M. tuberculosis T cell antigens and are included in multiple
subunit vaccine formulations. The inclusion of these proteins in next-generation
vaccines is expected to improve on BCG’s limited efficacy against
adult pulmonary disease.
,

vaccine development is an active area of bacterial vaccine research

) and QS-21 (a triterpene saponin that activates the immune system’s

Overview of the Components and Organization
of Bacterial Capsules

Bacterial capsules are surface-associated
polymers that envelop
the cell exterior from the cell wall. By forming a thick, hydrated
barrier outside the cell wall or OM, capsules protect bacteria from
desiccation, bacteriophage predation, and host immune defenses, including
phagocytosis, complement-mediated killing, and antimicrobial peptides. The specific contribution of a given capsule
to virulence depends on its chemical composition, charge, and structural
relationship to host molecules, and can include resistance to phagocytosis,
evasion of complement-mediated killing, and molecular mimicry of host
tissue, discussed in Section
. Because the capsule is often the most exposed and
immunogenic bacterial surface structure, it is subject to strong diversifying
selection by host adaptive immunity: serotype-specific antibodies
favor bacteria that switch to antigenically distinct capsule types,
driving the extensive serotype diversity seen within single pathogens. In E. coli, this diversity
is further amplified by recombination and plasmid-mediated horizontal
transfer of capsule gene clusters between species. Capsules are anchored to either the PG layer in monoderm
bacteria or the OM in diderm bacteria, and they are classified primarily
by their chemical composition.
,
 The most common capsules
are polysaccharide-based, but polypeptide- and hybrid-based capsules
also exist. Within the polysaccharide category, capsules are further
divided into heteropolysaccharide types, which are composed of two
or more distinct monosaccharides in their repeat units, and homopolysaccharide
types, which are built from a single sugar or disaccharide. The diversity
of polysaccharide capsule structures arises from variation in monosaccharide
identity, glycosidic linkage position and configuration (L or D),
chain branching, and chemical modifications such as O-acetylation,
pyruvylation, and phosphorylation.

8.1Polysaccharide CapsulesHeteropolysaccharide
capsules are the most prevalent capsule type and are distributed broadly
across both monoderm and diderm pathogens. Their repeat units typically
contain two to six monosaccharides, including common sugars such as
glucose (Glc), galactose (Gal), mannose (Man), rhamnose (Rha), fucose
(Fuc), N-acetylglucosamine (GlcNAc), N-acetylgalactosamine (GalNAc),
and N-acetylmannosamine (ManNAc). Uronic acids, including glucuronic
acid (GlcA) and galacturonic acid (GalA), are frequently incorporated
and confer negative charge to the capsule. Despite common components,
there is wide diversity in capsule composition and within serotypes
of the same bacteria. S. pneumoniae produces at least
93 antigenically distinct capsule serotypes, most of which are built
on trisaccharide repeating units, although hexasaccharide (serotype
12F) and tetrasaccharide (serotypes 4 and 6A) repeat units also occur.
,

K. pneumoniae produces at least 77 serotypes with
main-chain monosaccharides that include mannose, glucose, galactose,
fucose, and rhamnose.
E. coli produces approximately 80 antigenically distinct capsule types.
N. meningitidis is divided
into serogroups A, B, C, W, X, Y, and Z based on capsule polysaccharide
structure. Serogroups B and C carry polysialic acid capsules and are
described below. Serogroup A capsule is composed of α-1,6-linked
N-acetylmannosamine-1-phosphate. Serogroup W carries a capsule of
alternating sialic acid and galactose residues, and serogroup Y carries
alternating sialic acid and glucose residues.
Haemophilus influenzae produces six capsule
types designated a through f. Serotype b (Hib) carries a polyribose
ribitol phosphate capsule in which ribose and ribitol units are joined
by phosphodiester bonds.
,
 Serotype a capsule
consists of a polyribose ribitol phosphate polymer similar to but
antigenically distinct from the Hib capsule.
S. aureus produces at least 11 capsule types and Streptococcus agalactiae (group B Streptococcus, GBS) produces
nine serotypes (Ia, Ib, and II through VIII), which contain terminal
sialic acid residues on their side chains, and the serotype differences
arise from variation in the underlying backbone sugars. These are just a few examples; many more bacteria
and serotypes have been identified.Homopolysaccharide capsules
are composed of a single monosaccharide or disaccharide unit polymerized
into long linear chains. A defining feature of this group is structural
identity or close resemblance to mammalian glycosaminoglycans or cell-surface
glycans, which confer immunological advantages discussed further in
the pathogenicity section. Hyaluronic acid (HA) capsules are composed
of alternating β-1,3-linked GlcNAc and β-1,4-linked GlcA
residues, an exact chemical replica of the vertebrate extracellular
matrix component hyaluronic acid. HA
is a non-sulfated glycosaminoglycan compared to sulfated glycosaminoglycans
such as heparin, heparan sulfate, chondroitin sulfate, dermatan sulfate
and keratan sulfate. HA capsules are produced by S. pyogenes (Group A Streptococcus),
Streptococcus equi,
Pasteurella multocida serogroup A, and many others. Polysialic acid (PSA) capsules
are composed of polymerized N-acetylneuraminic acid (Neu5Ac, sialic
acid) residues. E. coli K1 and N. meningitidis serogroup B have α-2,8-linked
PSA.
N. meningitidis serogroup C carries α-2,9-linked PSA.
Mannheimia haemolytica serotype A2, a
bovine respiratory pathogen, also produces a PSA capsule. Heparosan capsules are composed of alternating
α-1,4-linked GlcNAc and β-1,4-linked GlcA residues, structurally
resembling the mammalian glycosaminoglycan heparin before its sulfation. E. coli K5 is the canonical heparosan-producing strain and
serves as the industrial source for biosynthetic heparin production. Chondroitin-like capsules are composed of alternating
GlcNAc and GlcA residues in a β-1,3/β-1,4-linked pattern,
present in E. coli K4, and resemble the mammalian glycosaminoglycan chondroitin. Another
capsule type, the Vi capsule, is found in Salmonella enterica serovar Typhi,
Citrobacter
freundii,
,
 members of Burkholderiales. Vi capsule is composed of α-1,4-linked
N-acetylgalactosaminuronic acid residues with partial O-acetylation
at the C3 position and constitutes a
distinct homopolysaccharide type.8.1.1Peptide CapsulesA small number
of bacteria produce capsules composed of amino acid polymers rather
than sugars. Poly-γ-d-glutamic acid (PDGA) capsules
are composed of d-glutamic acid residues polymerized through
their γ-carboxyl groups into long, high-molecular-weight chains. PDGA capsules are produced by S. epidermidis,
B. anthracis
 and Bacillus
licheniformis.
B. subtilis
 and S. epidermidis
 produces PDGA synthesized from both
D- and l-glutamic acid. However, rather than use the polymer
as a capsule, it secretes the polymer into the environment as a biofilm. The F1 protein capsule is found only in Yersinia pestis and is composed of non-covalently aggregated
linear fibrils of a single protein, Caf1. The fibrils are translocated and assembled through the fimbrial
chaperone:usher system such as those
described in Section
.8.1.2Hybrid CapsulesSome bacteria produce
capsules that combine polysaccharide and protein components or that
do not conform to the standard definitions above. Bacillus
megaterium produces a capsule containing both polysaccharide
and polypeptide components, although the precise structural organization
of this hybrid capsule remains incompletely characterized.
Francisella tularensis produces
two distinct surface structures classified as capsules. The O-antigen
capsule is a polysaccharide composed of mannose, rhamnose, and deoxy
sugars. The capsule-like complex (CLC)
contains both protein and carbohydrate components and shares features
with a loosely associated surface coat.
8.1.3Capsule ModificationsChemical
modifications alter the physical properties, charge, and antigenicity
of CPSs without changing their core repeat unit. O-Acetylation is
one of the most widespread modifications and is often the sole determinant
of serological differences between otherwise structurally similar
capsules. In S. pneumoniae, inactivation of the WcjE O-acetyltransferase converts serotype
11A to serotype 11E, eliminating the O-acetyl epitope recognized by
the lectin ficolin-2. In S.
aureus, the O-acetylation pattern distinguishes serotype
5 from serotype 8. Pyruvylation adds
a pyruvate ketal to a hydroxyl group of a sugar residue, introducing
additional negative charge and antigenic specificity. The serotype
4 S. pneumoniae capsule contains a pyruvate modification
on its tetrasaccharide repeat unit. Phosphate
groups serve as structural bridges in some capsules, most notably
in the H. influenzae type b polyribosylribitol phosphate
capsule, where phosphodiester bonds link the ribose and ribitol units
of the repeating chain.
8.1.4Capsule AnchoringSurface anchoring
differs between monoderm and diderm bacteria, and even among diderms,
it differs by capsule biosynthesis pathway (Section
). Group 1 (Wzy-dependent) capsules are
exported through the Wza translocon and captured non-covalently at
the cell surface by the outer membrane lectin Wzi (Section
). Group 2/3 (ABC transporter-dependent) capsules, by contrast,
remain covalently attached to the phosphatidylglycerol-Kdo glycolipid
on which they were synthesized, which anchors the mature polysaccharide
in the OM (Section
). In monoderm bacteria, completed
polysaccharide chains are covalently attached to the PG layer by LCP
family enzymes. One notable exception is S. pneumoniae serotype 3, whose capsule associates with the IM non-covalently
through interactions with phosphatidylglycerol rather than through
a covalent cell wall linkage. Polypeptide
capsules have distinct anchoring strategies. The PDGA capsule of B. anthracis is tethered covalently to PG via the CapBCE
synthetase complex.

Polysaccharide Capsules

Heteropolysaccharide
capsules are the most prevalent capsule type and are distributed broadly
across both monoderm and diderm pathogens. Their repeat units typically
contain two to six monosaccharides, including common sugars such as
glucose (Glc), galactose (Gal), mannose (Man), rhamnose (Rha), fucose
(Fuc), N-acetylglucosamine (GlcNAc), N-acetylgalactosamine (GalNAc),
and N-acetylmannosamine (ManNAc). Uronic acids, including glucuronic
acid (GlcA) and galacturonic acid (GalA), are frequently incorporated
and confer negative charge to the capsule. Despite common components,
there is wide diversity in capsule composition and within serotypes
of the same bacteria. S. pneumoniae produces at least
93 antigenically distinct capsule serotypes, most of which are built
on trisaccharide repeating units, although hexasaccharide (serotype
12F) and tetrasaccharide (serotypes 4 and 6A) repeat units also occur.
,

K. pneumoniae produces at least 77 serotypes with
main-chain monosaccharides that include mannose, glucose, galactose,
fucose, and rhamnose.
E. coli produces approximately 80 antigenically distinct capsule types.
N. meningitidis is divided
into serogroups A, B, C, W, X, Y, and Z based on capsule polysaccharide
structure. Serogroups B and C carry polysialic acid capsules and are
described below. Serogroup A capsule is composed of α-1,6-linked
N-acetylmannosamine-1-phosphate. Serogroup W carries a capsule of
alternating sialic acid and galactose residues, and serogroup Y carries
alternating sialic acid and glucose residues.
Haemophilus influenzae produces six capsule
types designated a through f. Serotype b (Hib) carries a polyribose
ribitol phosphate capsule in which ribose and ribitol units are joined
by phosphodiester bonds.
,
 Serotype a capsule
consists of a polyribose ribitol phosphate polymer similar to but
antigenically distinct from the Hib capsule.
S. aureus produces at least 11 capsule types and Streptococcus agalactiae (group B Streptococcus, GBS) produces
nine serotypes (Ia, Ib, and II through VIII), which contain terminal
sialic acid residues on their side chains, and the serotype differences
arise from variation in the underlying backbone sugars. These are just a few examples; many more bacteria
and serotypes have been identified.

Homopolysaccharide capsules
are composed of a single monosaccharide or disaccharide unit polymerized
into long linear chains. A defining feature of this group is structural
identity or close resemblance to mammalian glycosaminoglycans or cell-surface
glycans, which confer immunological advantages discussed further in
the pathogenicity section. Hyaluronic acid (HA) capsules are composed
of alternating β-1,3-linked GlcNAc and β-1,4-linked GlcA
residues, an exact chemical replica of the vertebrate extracellular
matrix component hyaluronic acid. HA
is a non-sulfated glycosaminoglycan compared to sulfated glycosaminoglycans
such as heparin, heparan sulfate, chondroitin sulfate, dermatan sulfate
and keratan sulfate. HA capsules are produced by S. pyogenes (Group A Streptococcus),
Streptococcus equi,
Pasteurella multocida serogroup A, and many others. Polysialic acid (PSA) capsules
are composed of polymerized N-acetylneuraminic acid (Neu5Ac, sialic
acid) residues. E. coli K1 and N. meningitidis serogroup B have α-2,8-linked
PSA.
N. meningitidis serogroup C carries α-2,9-linked PSA.
Mannheimia haemolytica serotype A2, a
bovine respiratory pathogen, also produces a PSA capsule. Heparosan capsules are composed of alternating
α-1,4-linked GlcNAc and β-1,4-linked GlcA residues, structurally
resembling the mammalian glycosaminoglycan heparin before its sulfation. E. coli K5 is the canonical heparosan-producing strain and
serves as the industrial source for biosynthetic heparin production. Chondroitin-like capsules are composed of alternating
GlcNAc and GlcA residues in a β-1,3/β-1,4-linked pattern,
present in E. coli K4, and resemble the mammalian glycosaminoglycan chondroitin. Another
capsule type, the Vi capsule, is found in Salmonella enterica serovar Typhi,
Citrobacter
freundii,
,
 members of Burkholderiales. Vi capsule is composed of α-1,4-linked
N-acetylgalactosaminuronic acid residues with partial O-acetylation
at the C3 position and constitutes a
distinct homopolysaccharide type.

8.1.1Peptide CapsulesA small number
of bacteria produce capsules composed of amino acid polymers rather
than sugars. Poly-γ-d-glutamic acid (PDGA) capsules
are composed of d-glutamic acid residues polymerized through
their γ-carboxyl groups into long, high-molecular-weight chains. PDGA capsules are produced by S. epidermidis,
B. anthracis
 and Bacillus
licheniformis.
B. subtilis
 and S. epidermidis
 produces PDGA synthesized from both
D- and l-glutamic acid. However, rather than use the polymer
as a capsule, it secretes the polymer into the environment as a biofilm. The F1 protein capsule is found only in Yersinia pestis and is composed of non-covalently aggregated
linear fibrils of a single protein, Caf1. The fibrils are translocated and assembled through the fimbrial
chaperone:usher system such as those
described in Section
.

Bacterial Capsule Biogenesis and Assembly

The Wzx/Wzy-Dependent Pathway

The
Wzx/Wzy-dependent pathway (Figure
A) is the most widely distributed mechanism for capsular
polysaccharide synthesis in both monoderm and diderm bacteria. This pathway proceeds through the stepwise
assembly of lipid-linked repeat units on the cytoplasmic face of the
IM, followed by their flipping and polymerization on the periplasmic
face. More than 90% of S. pneumoniae serotypes rely
on this pathway, as do group I and group IV E. coli capsules and many other encapsulated pathogens.

The process begins with a phosphoglycosyltransferase
(PGT) that
transfers a sugar-1-phosphate from a nucleotide sugar donor onto the
lipid carrier undecaprenyl phosphate (Und-P), generating undecaprenyl
diphosphate (Und-PP)-linked repeat units on the cytoplasmic leaflet
of the IM. Serotype-specific glycosyltransferases (GTs) sequentially
add sugars to the lipid-linked primer. In S. pneumoniae, both CpsE (WchA) and related glycosyltransferases from the WcjG
or WcjH homologous families carry out the first sugar transfer, depending
on whether glucose is part of the repeat unit. Once the initial monomer unit is assembled, the integral
membrane flippase Wzx translocates it across the IM to the periplasmic
face. The polymerase Wzy catalyzes nonprocessive
polymerization by transferring the growing polysaccharide chain from
the existing Und-PP-linked polymer onto the new repeat unit (nearly
identical to the polymerase for LPS biosynthesis). This block polymerization mechanism regenerates Und-PP
from the chain donor, allowing that lipid carrier to re-enter the
cytoplasmic assembly cycle. Chain length is regulated by the polysaccharide
co-polymerase Wzc, an IM protein with a cytoplasmic tyrosine autokinase
(BY-kinase) domain. Wzc cycles between
a dephosphorylated octameric state that spans the IM and a phosphorylated
monomeric state. This phosphorylation cycle, which is counteracted
by the phosphotyrosine phosphatase Wzb, is essential for productive
capsule assembly and export. In S. pneumoniae, the CpsB, CpsC, and CpsD proteins form an
analogous tyrosine phosphoregulatory system.
,

In monoderm bacteria, a distinct anchoring mechanism is present:
the completed polysaccharide chain is transferred from its lipid carrier
onto a PG assembly intermediate, which is then covalently attached
to the cell wall by LCP family enzymes, as described in Sections
 and . In diderm bacteria, once polymerized, the
capsular polysaccharide is translocated across the OM through the
OM polysaccharide export (OPX) family channel Wza, which forms an
octameric ring spanning the OM and periplasm. It is interesting to note that Wza has an α-helical
barrel that spans the outer membrane in contrast to the β-barrel
structures of most OMPs (Section
).
,
 The auxiliary lectin-binding
protein Wzi then assists in organizing the exported polymer into a
non-covalently surface-associated capsule structure. Some bacteria also use these same OPX proteins to secrete
polysaccharides for biofilm formation.
,

8.2.2The Synthase-Dependent PathwayThe synthase-dependent pathway (Figure
B), present in monoderms and diderm bacteria,
differs from the ABC transporter-dependent and Wzx/Wzy-dependent pathways
in that a single multifunctional enzyme or a small enzyme complex
is responsible for initiating, polymerizing, and translocating the
polysaccharide simultaneously without a lipid-linked intermediate. This mechanism is most common for homopolysaccharide
capsules and produces simpler, often linear polymer structures. The
prototypic example of the synthase mechanism in capsule biology is
cellulose biosynthesis (Figure
B), where the IM catalytic subunit BcsA couples processive
UDP-glucose-dependent polymerization to co-translational export through
a channel across the IM. The crystal
structure of the BcsA-BcsB complex from Rhodobacter sphaeroides containing a translocating polysaccharide revealed how synthesis
and membrane translocation are mechanistically coupled, with the nascent
chain extended by one glucose residue at a time.
The HA capsule synthases of S. pyogenes (HasA) and P. multocida serogroup A (PmHAS) serve
as the canonical examples of pathogenic capsule assembly.
,
 These enzymes add alternating GlcNAc and GlcA residues to the growing
HA chain at the cytoplasmic face of the membrane while extruding the
completed polymer to the cell exterior, producing a product chemically
identical to vertebrate hyaluronic acid. Similarly, the PSA capsule of E. coli K1 is synthesized
by the polysialyltransferase NeuS, which elongates the poly-α-2,8-Neu5Ac
chain processively from the cytoplasm.
In monoderms, once they are synthesized and released into
the periplasm,
the polysaccharides can either attach to the PG or be released into
the extracellular environment. In didermic bacteria, additional proteins
are needed to transport polysaccharides to the bacterial surface.
BcsB directs the chain to the periplasmic tetratricopeptide repeat
(TPR) protein that guides the polysaccharide to an OM transporter
(BcsC). Once across the OM, the polysaccharide is released into the
extracellular environment or attached to lipid A.

The Synthase-Dependent Pathway

The synthase-dependent pathway (Figure
B), present in monoderms and diderm bacteria,
differs from the ABC transporter-dependent and Wzx/Wzy-dependent pathways
in that a single multifunctional enzyme or a small enzyme complex
is responsible for initiating, polymerizing, and translocating the
polysaccharide simultaneously without a lipid-linked intermediate. This mechanism is most common for homopolysaccharide
capsules and produces simpler, often linear polymer structures. The
prototypic example of the synthase mechanism in capsule biology is

B), where the IM catalytic subunit BcsA couples processive
UDP-glucose-dependent polymerization to co-translational export through
a channel across the IM. The crystal
structure of the BcsA-BcsB complex from Rhodobacter sphaeroides containing a translocating polysaccharide revealed how synthesis
and membrane translocation are mechanistically coupled, with the nascent
chain extended by one glucose residue at a time.

The HA capsule synthases of S. pyogenes (HasA) and P. multocida serogroup A (PmHAS) serve
as the canonical examples of pathogenic capsule assembly.
,
 These enzymes add alternating GlcNAc and GlcA residues to the growing
HA chain at the cytoplasmic face of the membrane while extruding the
completed polymer to the cell exterior, producing a product chemically
identical to vertebrate hyaluronic acid. Similarly, the PSA capsule of E. coli K1 is synthesized
by the polysialyltransferase NeuS, which elongates the poly-α-2,8-Neu5Ac

In monoderms, once they are synthesized and released into
the periplasm,
the polysaccharides can either attach to the PG or be released into
the extracellular environment. In didermic bacteria, additional proteins
are needed to transport polysaccharides to the bacterial surface.
BcsB directs the chain to the periplasmic tetratricopeptide repeat
(TPR) protein that guides the polysaccharide to an OM transporter
(BcsC). Once across the OM, the polysaccharide is released into the

8.2.3The ABC Transporter-Dependent PathwayABC transporter-dependent polysaccharide synthesis (Figure
C) is exclusive to diderm bacteria
(e.g., Group II and Group III E. coli capsules) and
differs fundamentally from the Wzx/Wzy pathway. In this pathway, the entire polysaccharide chain is assembled
by processive polymerization in the cytoplasm completely before transfer
to a lipid. The growing chain is anchored to a conserved glycolipid
acceptor containing a poly-3-deoxy-D-manno-oct-2-ulosonic acid (Kdo)
linker joined to a phosphatidylglycerol terminus. The Kdo linker is
assembled by the sequential action of two CMP-Kdo-dependent glycosyltransferases,
KpsS and KpsC. A variation of this pathway
is found in the Vi-antigen capsule of S. enterica serovar Typhi and certain Burkholderiales, which
lack the capacity to synthesize the β-Kdo oligosaccharide linker
and instead use a distinctive diacyl-HexNAc lipid terminus assembled
by the Vi-specific acyltransferase VexE.
Once polymerization is complete, the KpsMT ABC transporter
exports the lipid-linked polysaccharide chain across the IM into the
periplasm. The IMP KpsM and OMP KpsD
then cooperate to translocate the chain across the periplasm and OM,
respectively, to the cell surface. KpsD localizes to the cell pole
and forms part of a multiprotein biosynthetic and export complex. Despite mechanistic differences from the Wzx/Wzy
route, both pathways employ OMPs belonging to the OPX family for the
final translocation step. Once at the
cell surface, the polysaccharide can interact with Wzi (another OMP),
non-covalently with LPS, or be released to the extracellular environment.

The ABC Transporter-Dependent Pathway

ABC transporter-dependent polysaccharide synthesis (Figure
C) is exclusive to diderm bacteria
(e.g., Group II and Group III E. coli capsules) and
differs fundamentally from the Wzx/Wzy pathway. In this pathway, the entire polysaccharide chain is assembled
by processive polymerization in the cytoplasm completely before transfer
to a lipid. The growing chain is anchored to a conserved glycolipid
acceptor containing a poly-3-deoxy-D-manno-oct-2-ulosonic acid (Kdo)
linker joined to a phosphatidylglycerol terminus. The Kdo linker is
assembled by the sequential action of two CMP-Kdo-dependent glycosyltransferases,
KpsS and KpsC. A variation of this pathway
is found in the Vi-antigen capsule of S. enterica serovar Typhi and certain Burkholderiales, which
lack the capacity to synthesize the β-Kdo oligosaccharide linker
and instead use a distinctive diacyl-HexNAc lipid terminus assembled
by the Vi-specific acyltransferase VexE.

Once polymerization is complete, the KpsMT ABC transporter
exports the lipid-linked polysaccharide chain across the IM into the
periplasm. The IMP KpsM and OMP KpsD
then cooperate to translocate the chain across the periplasm and OM,
respectively, to the cell surface. KpsD localizes to the cell pole
and forms part of a multiprotein biosynthetic and export complex. Despite mechanistic differences from the Wzx/Wzy
route, both pathways employ OMPs belonging to the OPX family for the
final translocation step. Once at the
cell surface, the polysaccharide can interact with Wzi (another OMP),
non-covalently with LPS, or be released to the extracellular environment.

8.2.4Protein Capsule SynthesisThe biosynthetic
pathway for the PDGA capsule of B. anthracis and
related Bacillus species was first elucidated in B. licheniformis. Synthesis
is carried out by a membrane-associated polyglutamyl synthetase complex,
CapB, CapC, and CapE, in B. anthracis.
,
 The reaction proceeds through the ATP-dependent activation of l-glutamic acid to form a γ-l-glutamyl adenylate
intermediate, which is then racemized to the D-form and transferred
to a protein-bound thioester intermediate.
,
 The activated d-glutamyl unit is then transferred to the
terminal amino group of a membrane-bound poly-γ-d-glutamyl
acceptor chain, extending it by one residue.
,
 This cycle continues to generate high-molecular-weight PDGA at the
membrane surface. The completed polymer is then covalently anchored
to the PG by CapD, a γ-glutamyl transpeptidase. Export of the full-length chain to the cell
exterior and the precise mechanism of release from the membrane remain
incompletely understood.
,

Protein Capsule Synthesis

The biosynthetic
pathway for the PDGA capsule of B. anthracis and
related Bacillus species was first elucidated in B. licheniformis. Synthesis
is carried out by a membrane-associated polyglutamyl synthetase complex,
CapB, CapC, and CapE, in B. anthracis.
,
 The reaction proceeds through the ATP-dependent activation of l-glutamic acid to form a γ-l-glutamyl adenylate
intermediate, which is then racemized to the D-form and transferred
to a protein-bound thioester intermediate.
,
 The activated d-glutamyl unit is then transferred to the
terminal amino group of a membrane-bound poly-γ-d-glutamyl
acceptor chain, extending it by one residue.
,
 This cycle continues to generate high-molecular-weight PDGA at the
membrane surface. The completed polymer is then covalently anchored
to the PG by CapD, a γ-glutamyl transpeptidase. Export of the full-length chain to the cell
exterior and the precise mechanism of release from the membrane remain
incompletely understood.
,

8.2.5Regulation of Capsule SynthesisCapsule production is tightly regulated in response to environmental
cues encountered during infection, including osmotic pressure, oxygen
tension, temperature, pH, CO2 concentration, and iron availability. For example, entry into the bloodstream, with
its 0.15 M NaCl osmolarity, triggers prioritized CPS synthesis in S. enterica serovar Typhi via the TviA auxiliary protein,
which integrates osmolarity sensing into the RcsB regulon to coordinately
upregulate Vi antigen expression while repressing flagella and the
type III secretion system. In S. aureus, surface growth conditions strongly promote capsule
production relative to liquid culture growth, a distinction relevant
to the in vivo environment. In addition, CO2 downregulates synthesis of S.
aureus type 5 and type 8 capsules. Temperature is the key regulatory signal for the Y. pestis F1 capsule, with expression occurring at 37°C in the mammalian
host but not at the ambient temperature of the arthropod vector. Capsule biosynthesis in B. anthracis is controlled by the virulence plasmid pXO2 and is transcriptionally
induced by elevated CO2/bicarbonate concentrations and
37°C temperature, signals that mimic the mammalian host environment.

Regulation of Capsule Synthesis

Capsule production is tightly regulated in response to environmental
cues encountered during infection, including osmotic pressure, oxygen
tension, temperature, pH, CO2 concentration, and iron availability. For example, entry into the bloodstream, with
its 0.15 M NaCl osmolarity, triggers prioritized CPS synthesis in S. enterica serovar Typhi via the TviA auxiliary protein,
which integrates osmolarity sensing into the RcsB regulon to coordinately
upregulate Vi antigen expression while repressing flagella and the
type III secretion system. In S. aureus, surface growth conditions strongly promote capsule
production relative to liquid culture growth, a distinction relevant
to the in vivo environment. In addition, CO2 downregulates synthesis of S.
aureus type 5 and type 8 capsules. Temperature is the key regulatory signal for the Y. pestis F1 capsule, with expression occurring at 37°C in the mammalian
host but not at the ambient temperature of the arthropod vector. Capsule biosynthesis in B. anthracis is controlled by the virulence plasmid pXO2 and is transcriptionally
induced by elevated CO2/bicarbonate concentrations and
37°C temperature, signals that mimic the mammalian host environment.

8.3Capsules in PathogenesisThe defining
pathogenic function of most polysaccharide capsules is the inhibition
of opsonophagocytosis. CPS forms a thick hydrophilic layer that physically
masks underlying bacterial surface antigens, including lipoteichoic
acid, PG fragments, and OMPs, thereby preventing their recognition
by pattern recognition receptors on phagocytes. CPS also blocks the
deposition and surface accessibility of the complement opsonin C3b;
the capsule sterically prevents C3b from engaging its receptor, CR1,
on neutrophils and macrophages, thereby impeding opsonophagocytosis. Capsules containing sialic acid additionally
recruit the complement regulatory protein factor H from serum, which
acts as a cofactor for factor I-mediated cleavage of C3b to the nonopsonic
fragment iC3b, preventing membrane attack complex formation.
S. agalactiae serotypes Ia
through VIII, which all carry terminal sialic acid residues, exploit
this mechanism to survive in the bloodstream of neonates where complement
is the primary defense. Capsule charge
and hydrophilicity further contribute by creating electrostatic and
steric barriers that repel negatively charged phagocyte membranes.
S. aureus capsule types 5 and
8 inhibit opsonophagocytosis by masking surface-associated proteins
that would otherwise mediate phagocyte recognition. The antiphagocytic effect is also subject to dynamic regulation
during infection: capsule expression is upregulated in response to
host-specific signals such as entry into the bloodstream, as seen
in S. pneumoniae.
Several capsule types exploit the molecular mimicry of mammalian
glycans to evade adaptive immunity. The HA capsules of S.
pyogenes, S. equi, and P. multocida serogroup A are chemically identical to those of vertebrate hyaluronic
acid, rendering them immunologically inert and unable to elicit a
robust antibody response on their own. The HA capsule of S. pyogenes protects against
neutrophil-mediated killing and is required for efficient initial
colonization of the nasopharynx and skin. The PSA capsules of E. coli K1 and N. meningitidis serogroup B are structurally identical to the polysialic acid modification
on the mammalian neural cell adhesion molecule NCAM, imposing central
immune tolerance that historically complicated vaccine development
against serogroup B meningococcal disease. The E. coli K1 capsule is required for survival
in the bloodstream and for the bacteremic phase preceding invasion
of the blood-brain barrier, and the K1 locus has independently emerged
in at least four E. coli phylogroups, underscoring
its selective advantage during invasive infection. Similarly, heparosan (E. coli K5) and
chondroitin-like (E. coli K4) capsules mimic mammalian
glycosaminoglycans, exploiting immunological self-tolerance to increase
resistance to complement and serum-mediated killing in extraintestinal
pathogenic strains.

Capsules in Pathogenesis

The defining
pathogenic function of most polysaccharide capsules is the inhibition
of opsonophagocytosis. CPS forms a thick hydrophilic layer that physically
masks underlying bacterial surface antigens, including lipoteichoic
acid, PG fragments, and OMPs, thereby preventing their recognition
by pattern recognition receptors on phagocytes. CPS also blocks the
deposition and surface accessibility of the complement opsonin C3b;
the capsule sterically prevents C3b from engaging its receptor, CR1,
on neutrophils and macrophages, thereby impeding opsonophagocytosis. Capsules containing sialic acid additionally
recruit the complement regulatory protein factor H from serum, which
acts as a cofactor for factor I-mediated cleavage of C3b to the nonopsonic
fragment iC3b, preventing membrane attack complex formation.
S. agalactiae serotypes Ia
through VIII, which all carry terminal sialic acid residues, exploit
this mechanism to survive in the bloodstream of neonates where complement
is the primary defense. Capsule charge
and hydrophilicity further contribute by creating electrostatic and
steric barriers that repel negatively charged phagocyte membranes.
S. aureus capsule types 5 and
8 inhibit opsonophagocytosis by masking surface-associated proteins
that would otherwise mediate phagocyte recognition. The antiphagocytic effect is also subject to dynamic regulation
during infection: capsule expression is upregulated in response to
host-specific signals such as entry into the bloodstream, as seen
in S. pneumoniae.

Several capsule types exploit the molecular mimicry of mammalian
glycans to evade adaptive immunity. The HA capsules of S.
pyogenes, S. equi, and P. multocida serogroup A are chemically identical to those of vertebrate hyaluronic
acid, rendering them immunologically inert and unable to elicit a
robust antibody response on their own. The HA capsule of S. pyogenes protects against
neutrophil-mediated killing and is required for efficient initial
colonization of the nasopharynx and skin. The PSA capsules of E. coli K1 and N. meningitidis serogroup B are structurally identical to the polysialic acid modification
on the mammalian neural cell adhesion molecule NCAM, imposing central
immune tolerance that historically complicated vaccine development
against serogroup B meningococcal disease. The E. coli K1 capsule is required for survival
in the bloodstream and for the bacteremic phase preceding invasion
of the blood-brain barrier, and the K1 locus has independently emerged
in at least four E. coli phylogroups, underscoring
its selective advantage during invasive infection. Similarly, heparosan (E. coli K5) and
chondroitin-like (E. coli K4) capsules mimic mammalian
glycosaminoglycans, exploiting immunological self-tolerance to increase
resistance to complement and serum-mediated killing in extraintestinal
pathogenic strains.

8.4Capsule Therapeutic Targets8.4.1Capsule Antibiotic TargetsThe
capsule does not present a viable target for conventional antibiotics
because its biosynthesis is dispensable for viability in vitro, and
inhibiting capsule production would reduce virulence rather than kill
the bacterium, an anti-virulence rather than bactericidal strategy.
The most developed approach to therapeutically exploiting the capsule
is the use of phage-encoded polysaccharide depolymerases, enzymes
that catalytically degrade specific CPS to expose the underlying bacterial
surface. In K. pneumoniae, phage capsule depolymerases
display extraordinary specificity, with distinct enzyme families targeting
different capsule types through either intra-chain or exo-chain cleavage
mechanisms; 58 characterized depolymerases have been described to
date, with structural data revealing conserved substrate-recognition
and phage-tethering domains alongside significant catalytic diversity. Depolymerases sensitize encapsulated K. pneumoniae to complement-mediated killing, phagocytosis,
and antibiotic action, and recombinant depolymerases show efficacy
in murine infection models; however, their extreme serotype specificity
means that matched enzyme–capsule type pairing would be required
for clinical use, necessitating rapid capsule typing as a prerequisite
for treatment. The breadth of capsule
types across clinically relevant species, particularly in K. pneumoniae, Acinetobacter baumannii,
and hypervirulent strains, thus presents a significant barrier to
broad clinical deployment of depolymerase-based strategies and has
driven interest in engineering broader-spectrum variants or inhibitor
cocktails.
8.4.2Capsule Vaccine TargetsCapsules
are not essential for bacterial growth under standard conditions,
thus, they are not the primary targets for conventional bactericidal
antibiotics; instead, the therapeutic utility of capsules is realized
through vaccine development, where CPS serves as the antigen in some
of the most clinically successful vaccines in history, and through
enzymatic approaches that degrade the capsule to restore immune and
antibiotic susceptibility.
,
 The same chemical
properties that make capsular polysaccharides (CPSs) effective virulence
factors, surface exposure, conservation within serotypes, and ability
to elicit opsonizing antibodies, also make them attractive vaccine
antigens. The central immunological principle underlying CPS-based
vaccines is that antibodies directed against the capsule can restore
opsonophagocytosis that the capsule itself suppresses, converting
a survival advantage to a vulnerability. However, translating this
principle into broadly protective vaccines has required overcoming
two recurring chemical obstacles: the T-cell-independent nature of
pure polysaccharide antigens, which fail to generate immunological
memory in infants, and the serotype specificity of anti-capsule antibodies,
which limits cross-protection across the enormous structural diversity
of capsule types described in Section
.
,
 For Group B Streptococcus, correlates of protection for CPS conjugate vaccines targeting serotypes
Ia, Ib, II, III, and V have been defined, providing a clearer regulatory
pathway and accelerating several clinical programs targeting neonatal
disease.
Pure polysaccharide vaccines
were the first generation of CPS-based immunizations and remain in
use for certain populations. The 23-valent pneumococcal polysaccharide
vaccine (PPSV23) covers the serotypes responsible for most of the
invasive pneumococcal disease in adults and elicits protective IgM
and IgG responses in immunocompetent individuals through a T cell-independent
mechanism.
,
 Similarly, the quadrivalent
meningococcal polysaccharide vaccine (MPSV4) targets N. meningitidis serogroups A, C, W, and Y and is effective in adults, although both vaccines are poorly immunogenic
in children under two years of age and do not induce durable immunological
memory.
Conjugate vaccines overcome
the T-cell independence problem by
covalently linking CPS to an immunogenic carrier protein, such as
diphtheria toxoid CRM197, tetanus toxoid, or the OMP complex of N. meningitidis. Conjugation converts the T-cell-independent
polysaccharide response into a T-cell-dependent one, enabling affinity
maturation, isotype switching to IgG, and the formation of long-lived
memory B cells. The H. influenzae type b (Hib) conjugate vaccine, introduced in the late 1980s, targets
the polyribose ribitol phosphate capsule described in Section
 and has essentially eliminated
invasive Hib disease in vaccinated populations, representing one of
the most successful applications of glycoconjugate chemistry in medicine.
,
 The 7-valent pneumococcal conjugate vaccine (PCV7) and its expanded
successors PCV13 and PCV15/20 target the most prevalent invasive S. pneumoniae serotypes; widespread implementation has dramatically
reduced rates of bacteremia, meningitis, and otitis media in children,
with additional herd protection extending to unvaccinated adults.
,
 The most recent iteration, PCV21, expands coverage further but its
deliberate omission of certain serotypes creates coverage gaps relevant
to Indigenous and lower-income country populations where serotype
4 has re-emerged. Conjugate vaccines
against N. meningitidis serogroups A, C, W, and Y
and variants have reduced meningococcal disease globally; however,
emerging variants still require surveillance and development of additional
vaccines. A new pentavalent vaccine
that introduces serogroup B (Bexsero, Trumenba) was approved by the
FDA. These conjugate vaccines circumvent
the self-tolerance problem posed by the PSA capsule described in Section
 by incorporating
OMPs rather than the capsule alone.Despite these successes,
the serotype coverage remains a fundamental
limitation. Capsule replacement, the expansion of nonvaccine serotypes
following selective pressure from conjugate vaccines, has been documented
for S. pneumoniae following PCV introduction and
represents an ongoing challenge for vaccine design. The extreme serotype diversity of K. pneumoniae (at least 77 K-types) and E. coli (approximately
80 K-antigens) has so far precluded comprehensive conjugate vaccine
coverage for these pathogens, driving interest in conserved subcapsular
antigens and broadly reactive approaches. Several CPS conjugate formulations targeting the most prevalent K. pneumoniae capsular types, particularly K1, K2, K47,
and K64, which predominate among hypervirulent clinical isolates,
have demonstrated protection in preclinical models, although no licensed
product yet exists.
,
 The chemical and structural
diversity of bacterial capsules thus remain both a central mechanism
of immune evasion and a continuing challenge for the rational design
of protective vaccines.

Capsule Therapeutic Targets

8.4.1Capsule Antibiotic TargetsThe
capsule does not present a viable target for conventional antibiotics

inhibiting capsule production would reduce virulence rather than kill
the bacterium, an anti-virulence rather than bactericidal strategy.
The most developed approach to therapeutically exploiting the capsule
is the use of phage-encoded polysaccharide depolymerases, enzymes
that catalytically degrade specific CPS to expose the underlying bacterial

display extraordinary specificity, with distinct enzyme families targeting

and phage-tethering domains alongside significant catalytic diversity. Depolymerases sensitize encapsulated K. pneumoniae to complement-mediated killing, phagocytosis,
and antibiotic action, and recombinant depolymerases show efficacy
in murine infection models; however, their extreme serotype specificity
means that matched enzyme–capsule type pairing would be required
for clinical use, necessitating rapid capsule typing as a prerequisite
for treatment. The breadth of capsule
types across clinically relevant species, particularly in K. pneumoniae, Acinetobacter baumannii,
and hypervirulent strains, thus presents a significant barrier to
broad clinical deployment of depolymerase-based strategies and has
driven interest in engineering broader-spectrum variants or inhibitor
cocktails.
