9Overview of the structure and organization of
extracellular appendages: Pili, fimbriae, and curliSurface
appendages (Figure
) represent a structurally and functionally diverse
class of bacterial surface structures that extend beyond the OM (in
diderm bacteria) or the PG layer (in monoderm bacteria) into the extracellular
environment. Unlike the relatively static protein and lipid components
of the cell envelope discussed above, these filamentous structures
are dynamic, often subject to phase and antigenic variation, and represent
primary mediators of host–pathogen interaction at the earliest
stages of infection. The major categories covered here are chaperone–usher
fimbriae, type IV pili and their relatives, sortase-assembled pili
in monoderm bacteria, conjugative pili, and the functional amyloid
curli fibers.10
Assembly mechanisms of bacterial surface appendages. Four classes of surface appendages are depicted, each employing
a distinct biogenesis strategy (Conjugative pili are assembled with
T4SS which is shown in Figure
). Sortase-assembled pili (left) are found
in monoderm bacteria and built through a biphasic covalent mechanism.
Pilin precursors carrying a C-terminal cell wall sorting signal (CWSS)
are retained at the membrane, where a pilus-specific sortase (SrtA)
catalyzes sequential transpeptidation reactions that covalently crosslink
individual pilin subunits into a growing polymer, proceeding from
tip to base. The sortase SrtF anchors the completed pilus to the basal
pilin. Attachment to the PG cell wall differs between species. Chaperone/usher (CU) pili (second panel) are assembled in
diderm bacteria via a periplasmic chaperone and OM usher platform.
Pilin subunits are translocated across the IM by the Sec translocon
and captured by a periplasmic chaperone, which maintains them in a
foldedstate and delivers them to the OM usher. The usher catalyzes
donor-strand exchange between successive pilin subunits, assembling
tip and rod pilin subunits into a growing fiber that is extruded through
the usher pore to the cell surface. Type IV pili (third
panel) are dynamic filaments found in both monoderm and diderm bacteria.
Prepilins are inserted into the IM by the Sec translocon and processed
by a dedicated peptidase. The platform protein and alignment proteins
at the IM coordinate subunit addition driven by an elongation ATPase,
while a retraction ATPase powers depolymerization, both using ATP
hydrolysis. The secretin forms the OM pore through which the pilus
is extruded. Curli fibers (right panel) are functional
amyloids assembled by a dedicated nucleation-precipitation mechanism.
CsgA and CsgB are synthesized as unfolded monomers in the cytoplasm,
translocated across the IM by Sec, and exported to the cell surface
through the OM pore formed by CsgG/E, assisted by the accessory protein
CsgF. CsgB acts as a nucleator that templates the polymerization of
CsgA into amyloid fibers on the cell surface. CsgC prevents premature
intracellular aggregation of CsgA and CsgB in the periplasm. Created
in https://BioRender.com.9.1Chaperone–Usher PiliThe chaperone/usher
(CU) pathway (Figure
) is the most widespread mechanism by which Gram-negative bacteria
assemble adhesive surface filaments called pili or fimbriae.
,
 CU pili are found predominantly in Enterobacteriaceae and related
diderm pathogens, where they mediate attachment to host tissues as
a critical early step in infection. The best-characterized examples
are type 1 pili, which enable uropathogenic E. coli (UPEC) to colonize the bladder epithelium, and P pili, which mediate
kidney colonization in pyelonephritis. Within UPEC alone, multiple distinct CU systems are expressed, including
F1C pili mediating binding to renal tubular cells, S fimbriae targeting
sialic acid-containing receptors in the urinary tract and meninges,
and Dr/Afa fimbriae recognizing decay-accelerating factor on uroepithelial
and endothelial cells. CU fimbriae extend
well beyond uropathogens: colonization factor antigen I (CFA/I) and
related CS fimbriae are assembled by this pathway in enterotoxigenic E. coli (ETEC), where they mediate adhesion to the small
intestinal epithelium and are essential for diarrheal disease.
,
 CU systems have also been identified in H. influenzae, K. pneumoniae, Salmonella species,
and A. baumannii, underscoring their broad distribution
and importance across Gram-negative pathogens.
CU pili are composite structures built from a series
of protein subunits, each sharing an incomplete immunoglobulin-like
fold that lacks the seventh C-terminal β-strand. The assembled
filament consists of two morphologically distinct regions: a rigid,
helical rod composed of hundreds to thousands of copies of the major
pilin subunit (FimA in type 1 pili, PapA in P pili), and a short,
flexible tip fibrillum that terminates in a tip adhesin responsible
for receptor binding (FimH in type 1 pili, PapG in P pili).
,
 Between the rod and the adhesin, several minor subunits occupy defined
positions: in type 1 pili, FimF and FimG form the junction between
rod and tip, while in P pili, PapE, PapF, and PapK occupy equivalent
linker positions. All subunits are incorporated
through a mechanism called donor strand complementation, in which
the N-terminal extension of each incoming subunit donates a β-strand
to complete the immunoglobulin fold of its neighbor, creating an interlocked
polymer.
,
 This non-covalent but highly
stable interstrand bonding gives the rod its mechanical resilience
and its capacity to uncoil under tensile stress and recoil upon relaxation,
a property proposed to protect bacteria from detachment under shear
flow in the urinary tract.
Crystal
structures of tip complexes, including FimH-FimG-FimF-FimC
at 2.7 Å and the equivalent PapD-PapA system, reveal the geometry
of this donor strand exchange and the conformational changes each
subunit undergoes upon interlocking with its neighbor.
,,
 In the assembled rod, hundreds
to thousands of major pilin subunits are wound into a right-handed
superhelix stabilized entirely by these non-covalent interstrand contacts,
giving the filament both mechanical resilience and the capacity to
uncoil reversibly under tensile stress. The tip adhesin domains adopt a conserved elongated β-barrel
jelly-roll fold that presents the receptor-binding site at its apical
surface, a scaffold shared across structurally characterized CU adhesins
including FimH, PapG, GafD, and DraE despite minimal sequence identity.
,

9.1.1Type IV PiliType IV pili (T4P)
(Figure
) are highly
dynamic surface filaments found across a broad phylogenetic range
of both Gram-negative and Gram-positive bacteria as well as archaea,
making them one of the most widely distributed bacterial surface organelles
known.
,
 T4P mediate a remarkable diversity of cellular
functions, including adhesion to host tissues and abiotic surfaces,
twitching motility driven by cycles of filament extension and retraction,
natural competence for DNA uptake, bacteriophage infection, and biofilm
formation.
,
 T4P are divided into three subclasses:
type IVa (T4aP), type IVb (T4bP), and type IVc (T4cP, also called
Tad or tight adherence pili), which differ in pilin subunit size,
the extent of N-terminal processing, assembly machinery composition,
and functional outputs, though all share the defining features of
the superfamily.
,,
 In pathogenic bacteria, T4P are critical virulence determinants: N. gonorrhoeae and N. meningitidis use
T4P to colonize mucosal epithelium and resist phagocytosis; P. aeruginosa T4P mediate initial attachment to airway epithelium
and are required for establishing chronic lung infections in cystic
fibrosis patients; Vibrio cholerae toxin-coregulated
pilus (TCP) is essential for intestinal colonization and is among
the most potent colonization factors known; and in enteropathogenic E. coli (EPEC), the bundle-forming pilus (BFP) mediates
microcolony formation on the intestinal epithelium.
,
 T4cP (Tad pili) are found in an equally broad range of organisms
including Aggregatibacter actinomycetemcomitans, Caulobacter crescentus, Myxococcus xanthus, and Vibrio vulnificus, where they mediate surface
adhesion, biofilm formation, cell–cell aggregation, and surface
sensing.
−


The T4P filament is a homo- or hetero-polymeric
helical assembly built primarily from thousands of copies of the major
pilin subunit, designated PilA or PilE depending on the organism,
along with minor pilins that occupy the filament tip and play roles
in initiating assembly and mediating adhesion.
,
 In T4aP and T4bP systems, each pilin subunit consists of a conserved
hydrophobic N-terminal α-helix (α1) that forms the core
of the filament, packed against a globular C-terminal head domain
containing a four-stranded antiparallel β-sheet and variable
surface-exposed loops that determine receptor specificity and antigenicity.
,
 The N-terminal α1 helix partially unfolds at a conserved segment
near the helix base, and this flexible region accommodates the tight
packing geometry of subunits in the filament interior.
,
 T4cP Flp pilins are structurally distinct: they lack the globular
C-terminal β-sheet head domain entirely and are instead purely
α-helical, producing a thinner and structurally simpler filament
than those of T4aP and T4bP systems. n all three subclasses, filament stability arises from hydrophobic
interactions among the N-terminal helices of adjacent subunits rather
than from the intermolecular β-strand exchange that stabilizes
CUP pili.
,,

The atomic structure of individual pilin subunits has been
determined
for multiple T4P systems. The N. gonorrhoeae PilE
pilin crystal structure established the canonical T4aP pilin fold:
a long N-terminal α-helix packing against a globular C-terminal
domain containing a disulfide-bonded D-region loop that is critical
for receptor recognition and immune evasion through antigenic variation. Cryo-EM reconstructions of intact filaments
from N. gonorrhoeae and P. aeruginosa revealed how subunits pack in the assembled fiber, with the hydrophobic
N-terminal helices forming a tight apolar core and the globular C-terminal
domains coating the outer surface and presenting variable loops for
host interactions.
,
 A structure of M. xanthus T4aP revealed that species-specific differences in pilin length
and intersubunit contact geometry produce measurable differences in
filament mechanical properties, linking atomic-level structural variation
to the macroscopic function of motility on varied surfaces. For T4cP, the first intact in situ structure
of the complete Tad pilus machine was resolved in C. crescentus by cryo-ET and subtomogram averaging, revealing that the Tad filament
is built from purely α-helical PilA subunits lacking the globular
C-terminal head domain of T4aP and T4bP pilins, and capturing multiple
assembly intermediates of the nanomachine within the intact cell envelope.
9.1.2Sortase-Assembled Pili in Monoderm BacteriaSortase-assembled pili (Figure
) are surface appendages found exclusively in Gram-positive
bacteria, where they serve as critical mediators of adhesion, colonization,
biofilm formation, and immune evasion.
,
 This class
of pili is named after the transpeptidases (sortases) that polymerize
the pilus (class E) and attach the pilus to the cell wall (a housekeeping
sortase of class F). They are broadly distributed across clinically
significant pathogens and commensals, including S. pyogenes, S. agalactiae, S. pneumoniae, E. faecalis, E. faecium, Corynebacterium
diphtheriae, Actinomyces oris, and Bacillus cereus, as well as gut commensals such as Lactobacillus and Bifidobacterium species.
,−


 In addition, single bacterial species have more than one type of
sortase-assembled pili. There are two classes of sortase pili: heterotrimeric
and heterodimeric. Heterotrimeric sortase pili have one protein forming
the base attachment to the cell wall (basal pilin), the polymerized
subunit forming the rod (the major or backbone pilin), and the tip
protein (minor pilin adhesins). Heterodimeric lack a basal pilin but
have tip and major pilin proteins. Although bacteria all have this
architecture, the individual subunits differ in sequence and structure.
Most basal, major, and minor pilins have an immunoglobulin (Ig)-like
fold β-sandwich domain named collagen adhesin (Cna) and are
divided into two subfamilies CnaA and B. All Cna folds have isopeptides;
however, the number of Cna domains and the placement of the isopeptide
bonds differ. The structure of the S. pyogenes major
pilin has two Cna domains, each is stabilized by an intramolecular
isopeptide bond between a lysine and an asparagine that contributes
to the stability of the individual subunits.
,
 Each major pilin is connected to the next by an intermolecular isopeptide
bond between a lysine in one subunit with the carboxyl terminus of
another subunit. In contrast, the S. pneumoniae major pilin has four domains (D1-4) each with
an intermolecular isopeptide and an intermolecular isopeptide bond
between D1 and D4. The adhesion subunits
have a binding domain that adopts a classic α/β open-twisted
(or para-Rossmann) fold (also termed von Willebrand factor type A
(vWFA) fold). The vWFA domains typically have a metal ion-dependent
adhesion site (MIDAS; D-x-S-x-S-T-D) and bind extracellular matrix
proteins such as mucin, laminin, collagen, fibrinogen, and fibronectin.
,
 The vWFA domain is followed by a series of Can domains. In Lactobacillus rhamnosus GG, the vWFA is followed by a CnaA
domain and three CnaB domains. In contrast,
the minor pilin of S. pneumoniae has four domains:
the vWFA domain followed by one CnaB and two CnaA domains.
9.1.3Conjugative PiliConjugative pili
are surface appendages produced by donor bacteria that mediate the
initial cell–cell contact required for bacterial conjugation,
the direct transfer of DNA between cells, making them the primary
drivers of horizontal gene transfer across bacterial populations.
,
 They are encoded by conjugative plasmids and integrative conjugative
elements (ICEs) found across a wide range of Gram-negative and Gram-positive
bacteria, with the F plasmid-encoded system of E. coli serving as the paradigm.
,
 The conjugative pilus
is an extracellular appendage of the type IV secretion system (T4SS),
which spans the entire cell envelope and provides the channel through
which the DNA-protein transfer complex is delivered to the recipient
cell; the T4SS machinery itself is discussed in detail in section
10.0.4.
,
 Because conjugative plasmids frequently
carry antibiotic resistance and virulence genes, conjugative pili
represent a central mechanism in the dissemination of antimicrobial
resistance across bacterial communities.
,

Conjugative pili are thin, flexible tubular filaments approximately
8–9 nm in outer diameter with a hollow lumen of approximately
2.5–3 nm.
−

 The filament is built from a single major
pilin species, TraA in F-type systems and VirB2 in Agrobacterium
tumefaciens T-pili, that is processed from a propilin precursor
synthesized with an unusually long leader peptide of approximately
50 amino acids.
,
 The mature TraA pilin is approximately
64 amino acids long and folds into a purely α-helical structure
comprising three helices (α1, α2, α3), with no β-sheet
component. A defining and unexpected
structural feature revealed by cryo-EM is that pilin subunits assemble
with phospholipid molecules at a strict 1:1 stoichiometric ratio:
,
 mass spectrometry confirmed these are phosphatidylglycerol (PG)
species (primarily PG 32:1, PG 33:1, and PG 34:1) selectively incorporated
from the IM, with PG head groups exposed to the electronegative lumen
and acyl chains buried between adjacent helical segments. Mutations in the phospholipid binding sites
(Y37V and A28F in pED208) abolish pilus assembly, demonstrating that
lipid incorporation is structurally essential and not incidental. The helical symmetry of the assembled filament
varies between systems: F-type and pED208-type pili display five-fold
rotational symmetry (five-start helical filaments), while the pKpQIL
pilus from carbapenem-resistant K. pneumoniae forms
a one-start.
,
 An additional structural variant
is the H-pilus of IncH plasmids, in which the TrhA pilin undergoes
covalent cyclization connecting the N- and C-termini following cleavage
of the last five residues; this cyclization is essential for pilus
biogenesis and efficient plasmid transfer.
9.1.4Curli FibersCurli (Figure
) are functional amyloid fibers
that form the major proteinaceous component of the extracellular matrix
in biofilms produced by many Gram-negative bacteria. They were first
identified in Salmonella and E. coli and are phylogenetically widespread across at least four bacterial
phyla, including Proteobacteria and Bacteroidetes, with curli genes
detectable in environmental, clinical, and commensal metagenomes. Within the biofilm matrix, curli mediate initial
adhesion to abiotic and biotic surfaces, provide structural cohesion
by forming interbacterial bundles, and contribute to the mechanical
stability and stress resistance of the biofilm community.
,
 A notable biological feature is the capacity of curli fibers for
cross-species seeding: curli subunit homologues from E. coli, Salmonella, and Citrobacter cross-seed
one another in vitro and support interspecies fiber
formation in mixed-species biofilms, a process facilitated by the
high conservation of the curlin repeat architecture.
,

In E. coli, the curli system is encoded by
two divergently transcribed operons, csgBAC and csgDEFG, controlled by the master transcriptional regulator
CsgD and the stationary-phase sigma factor σS. CsgA is the major structural subunit and principal polymerizing
component; CsgB is a minor subunit that serves as the membrane-anchored
nucleator of CsgA amyloidogenesis; both are approximately 13 kDa polypeptides. Each subunit carries a 20-residue N-terminal
signal peptide for Sec-dependent export, followed by a 22-residue
N-terminal targeting sequence (N22) that directs the subunit to the
OM secretion machinery (CsgG), and an amyloidogenic core composed
of five imperfect pseudo-repeat sequences of approximately 22 amino
acids (R1–R5); each repeat forms a strand-arc-strand β-arch
unit built from two motifs flanked by conserved Asn and Gln residues.
,
 Cryo-EM analysis combined with ΑFold2 modeling of CsgA established
that the folded subunit adopts a left-handed β-solenoid, and
solid-state NMR had previously shown that curli fibers do not adopt
the in-register parallel β-sheet architecture of pathological
amyloids.
,
 In the assembled protofibril,
successive CsgA subunits stack head-to-tail via seamless β-solenoid
augmentation with pure translational symmetry and no helical twist,
producing a fiber with 4.8 Å inter-repeat spacing, approximately
20 Å width, and a half-unit stagger between opposing β-strands. CsgB adopts a structurally equivalent β-solenoid
compatible with CsgA fiber extension; the lower intrinsic polymerization
propensity of CsgB compared to CsgA is associated with degenerate
residues in its C-terminal repeats R4 and R5.
,
 High-speed AFM imaging at single-fiber resolution demonstrated that
curli fibers grow from both ends with asymmetric kinetics and display
stop-and-go dynamics in which bursts of steady-state elongation alternate
with periods of stagnation.
9.2Pili, Fimbriae, and Curli BiogenesisThe assembly of pili, fimbriae, and curli fibers requires dedicated
biogenesis machinery that is as structurally diverse as the appendages.
Each system must solve the same fundamental problem: transporting
structural subunits from the cytoplasm across one or two membranes
and assembling them into ordered extracellular structures without
premature aggregation or mislocalization. The solutions that have
evolved are remarkably varied, ranging from the periplasmic chaperone
and OM usher platform used by CU fimbriae, to the dynamic ATPase-powered
extension and retraction machinery of type IV pili, to the covalent
isopeptide crosslinking catalyzed by dedicated sortases in monoderm
bacteria, to the amyloid nucleation and secretion pore system that
produces curli fibers. The following subsections describe the biogenesis
machinery and assembly mechanisms for each class of surface appendages
in turn.9.2.1CU Pili BiogenesisBiogenesis of
CU pili (Figure
) requires two dedicated components: a periplasmic chaperone and
an OM usher.
,
 The chaperone, exemplified by
FimC in type 1 pili and PapD in P pili, adopts a two-domain immunoglobulin-like
fold and binds each newly translocated subunit in the periplasm by
donating a G1 β-strand to complete the subunit’s incomplete
fold, a mechanism called donor strand complementation.
,
 This interaction stabilizes the subunit in a high-energy activation
state, storing the free energy that will subsequently drive fiber
elongation. Chaperone-subunit complexes
are delivered to the OM usher, a 24-stranded β-barrel occluded
by an internal plug domain, where subunits are incorporated into the
growing fiber through donor strand exchange as described above in Section
.
,−

 The usher coordinates this process through
spatially distinct functional domains: the N-terminal domain recruits
incoming chaperone-subunit complexes, the β-barrel lumen accommodates
the translocating subunit, and the C-terminal domains anchor the elongating
fiber on the extracellular face.
,
 Assembly
is terminated when the capping subunit FimI engages the usher irreversibly,
preventing further subunit incorporation.
9.2.2Type IV Pili BiogenesisT4P biogenesis
(Figure
) requires
the coordinated action of more than a dozen proteins spanning both
membranes.
,
 Newly synthesized pre-pilins
are inserted into the IM by the Sec translocon and processed by a
dedicated prepilin peptidase (PilD in T4aP/T4bP) that cleaves the
short leader sequence and methylates the new N-terminal phenylalanine,
a modification essential for filament assembly.
,,
 Processed pilins are then extracted
from the IM by the extension ATPase PilB, which forms a cytoplasmic
hexameric ring that interacts with the IM platform protein PilC; together
these drive pilin polymerization at the base of the growing filament,
threading it through the OM secretin pore formed by the PilQ multimer.
,
 Retraction is powered by the PilT ATPase, which drives depolymerization
at the same IM interface, generating tensile forces sufficient to
pull attached objects and propel the cell across surfaces.
,
 The alignment complex, comprising PilM, PilN, PilO, and PilP in P. aeruginosa, physically bridges the IM and OM components
and is required for efficient assembly.
,
 T4cP biogenesis follows the same general logic, Flp pilins are processed
by a dedicated prepilin peptidase prior to incorporation, and a single
assembly ATPase (TadA) drives polymerization, but differs in that
the periplasmic conduit is formed by the RcpC dodecamer rather than
a canonical alignment complex, the OM channel is the phylogenetically
distinct RcpA secretin rather than PilQ, and the additional OMPs RcpB
and TadD are required for proper secretin assembly and stability.
,,
 The in situ cryo-ET structure
of the C. crescentus Tad machine further revealed
the stepwise assembly process of the complete nanomachine within the
intact cell envelope, providing the first architectural framework
for understanding how all core Tad components are spatially organized
during pilus biogenesis.
9.2.3Sortase-Assembled Pili BiogenesisSortase-assembled pilus biogenesis (Figure
) proceeds through a two-stage biphasic
mechanism.
,
 Pilin precursors are first translocated
across the cytoplasmic membrane by the Sec machinery, then properly
folded by a dedicated oxidative protein-folding machine and retained
at the membrane via their C-terminal cell wall sorting signal (CWSS),
composed of an LPXTG motif followed by a hydrophobic domain and a
cluster of positively charged residues. In the first stage of assembly, a pilus-specific class C sortase
polymerizes pilin subunits by catalyzing sequential isopeptide bond
formation at a membrane-associated platform: the enzyme cleaves the
LPXTG motif to form a thioester-linked acyl-enzyme intermediate, which
reacts with the lysine within the pilin motif of the next incoming
subunit.
,
 It is assumed that polymerization
is initiated by first crosslinking the tip adhesin pilin to the major
rod pilin, building the pilus from tip to base (the “tip first
rule”).
,
 For heterotrimeric pili, polymerization
is terminated when the base pilin outcompetes the shaft pilin for
the sortase thioester intermediate.
,
 For heterodimeric
pili, termination and pilus length are thought to be determined by
the housekeeping sortase that attaches the major pilin to the cell
wall. In the second stage, the completed
pilus polymer is transferred to the PG cell wall through transpeptidation
catalyzed by either the housekeeping sortase or the pilus-specific
sortase itself. The mechanism of cell
wall anchoring varies across systems: in S. aureus the housekeeping sortase SrtF links the pilus polymer to newly synthesized
PG via lipid II, while in E.
faecalis the Ebp pili are anchored directly to un-cross-linked
cell wall independently of newly synthesized PG, suggesting that the cell wall anchoring step is more mechanistically
diverse across Gram-positive bacteria than previously appreciated. A recently discovered regulatory layer in Actinobacteria involves SafA, a conserved transmembrane
protein that directly interacts with the housekeeping sortase and
prevents its cleavage by signal peptidase LepB2, thereby maintaining
sortase membrane homeostasis and ensuring proper pilus assembly.
9.2.4Conjugative Pili BiogenesisConjugative
pilus biogenesis is driven entirely by the T4SS machinery encoded
on the conjugative element, and the pilus is therefore an extracellular
appendage of that secretion system rather than an independent assembly
pathway; the molecular details of the T4SS are covered in Section
.
,
 PropilinTraA insertion into the IM is Sec-independent and instead
is inserted by the F plasmid-encoded transmembrane protein TraQ.
,
 The 5.5 kDa leader peptide is then cleaved by the periplasmic signal
peptidase LepB, after which the new N-terminus of the mature 7 kDa
pilin is acetylated by the F plasmid-encoded TraX.
,
 Phospholipid incorporation into the filament occurs concurrently
with polymerization, though the mechanism by which PG species are
selectively recruited into the growing pilus at 1:1 stoichiometry
remains to be elucidated.
,
 A distinctive feature
of conjugative pili is their dynamic behavior: live-cell imaging has
shown that F-pili undergo spontaneous cycles of extension and retraction,
with extension and retraction accompanied by rotation about the long
axis of the filament; this dynamic sampling of the environment increases
the probability of contact with recipient cells, and once contact
is established, the flexible pilus maintains the mating pair against
shear forces in liquid media. Retraction
is thought to draw donor and recipient cells into close enough contact
to establish the stable mating pair required for DNA transfer.
,
 Unlike CUP pili, T4P, or sortase-assembled pili, conjugative pili
require no dedicated OM usher, no periplasmic chaperones for pilin
stabilization, and no independent assembly ATPase, all these functions
are integrated within the T4SS complex.
9.2.5Curli Fibers BiogenesisCurli biogenesis
(also known as type VIII secretion) begins with the synthesis of CsgA
and CsgB with an N-terminal signal peptide. CgsA and CgsB are then
translocated to the periplasm via the SecYEG translocon (Figure
), where their
signal peptides are cleaved. In the
periplasm, the 11 kDa protein CsgC acts as a sub-stoichiometric amyloid
inhibitor that maintains CsgA and CsgB in a soluble, non-β-sheet
conformation, preventing toxic premature aggregation prior to secretion. Secretion of CsgA and CsgB across the OM is
mediated by CsgG, a nonameric β-barrel diffusion-driven peptide
channel.
,
 CsgE forms a nonameric periplasmic
cap that closes the cytoplasmic vestibule of the CsgG pore, providing
substrate specificity by directly engaging CsgA through its positively
charged head region (centered on R47) while preventing non-specific
protein passage.
,
 On the extracellular surface,
the accessory protein CsgF is essential for CsgB surface attachment
and nucleator activity: in csgF null mutants, secreted
CsgA is released as soluble monomers rather than assembling into cell-associated
fibers, because CsgB fails to localize to and remain on the OM. Once CsgB is surface-anchored and activated
by CsgF, an interaction that depends on CsgB’s C-terminal repeats,
it rapidly templates CsgA into fibers, initiating nucleation-dependent
polymerization in a head-to-tail manner that propagates through seamless
continuation of the β-arcade.
,,,,

9.3Role of Pili, Fimbriae, and Curli in PathogenesisSurface appendages are among the most direct mediators of host–pathogen
interaction, functioning as the first points of contact between a
bacterium and its target tissue. The tip adhesins determine host cell
tropism, establish intimate attachments required for colonization,
and in many cases, trigger downstream signaling cascades in host cells
that facilitate invasion or immune evasion. Curli fibers contribute
to biofilm formation on host surfaces and are potent activators of
innate immune pathways. The pathogenic contributions of these structures
are not limited to adhesion: surface appendages also participate in
horizontal gene transfer, motility, biofilm development, and subversion
of complement and phagocytic clearance. The following subsections
describe the specific pathogenic roles of each appendage class, with
emphasis on the molecular interactions that contribute to infection.9.3.1CU Fimbriae in PathogenesisFimH
mediates binding to mannosylated glycoproteins on uroepithelial cells
through a mechanosensitive catch bond: tensile force from urine flow
induces a conformational change in the FimH lectin domain that paradoxically
strengthens adhesion, allowing uropathogenic Escherichia coli (UPEC) to resist voiding. The allosteric mechanism couples the lectin
domain to the pilin domain such that force-induced separation of the
two domains shifts FimH to a high-affinity conformation for its mannose
receptor. Genetic deletion of fimH abolishes bladder colonization in murine models. P fimbriae mediate binding to globoside receptors
in the upper urinary tract via PapG, facilitating pyelonephritis;
the structural basis of PapG and GalNAcβ1-3Galα1-4Galβ1-4Glc
recognition was determined using crystallography.
Beyond UPECs, CUP fimbriae are virulence factors
across a wide range of enterobacterial pathogens. Dr fimbriae of E. coli bind decay-accelerating factor (DAF/CD55) on uroepithelial
cells, and this interaction has been shown to trigger lipid-raft-mediated
invasion. Mrp fimbriae of Proteus
mirabilis facilitate catheter-associated urinary tract infections.
K. pneumoniae type 3 fimbriae
mediate biofilm formation on host tissues and indwelling devices through
binding to collagen IV and fibronectin. The MrkD tip adhesin of type 3 fimbriae adopts a lectin fold structurally
unrelated to FimH, indicating that CUP pili have recruited diverse
adhesin architectures to engage different host receptors.
,
 CU fimbriae of enterotoxigenic E. coli (ETEC) mediate
adhesion to small intestinal epithelium and are major determinants
of diarrheal disease.
,

9.3.2Type IV Pili in PathogenesisT4P
contributes to pathogenesis through two mechanistically distinct modes:
direct receptor-mediated adhesion that establishes initial host contact
and force-generating retraction that triggers intracellular signaling
cascades facilitating invasion, immune modulation, and biofilm development.
T4P mediates initial surface attachment and, through retraction, generates
mechanical force that triggers signaling cascades in host cells. In N. gonorrhoeae and N. meningitidis, pilus
retraction activates Rac1 and cortactin-dependent actin rearrangements
in epithelial cells, facilitating bacterial engulfment. The tip-localized minor pilin PilC directly
mediates binding to epithelial cell receptors. Antigenic variation of the major pilin subunit PilE through
a G-quadruplex DNA-directed recombination mechanism generates extensive
structural diversity at the surface, constituting a major mechanism
of immune evasion and persistence in N. gonorrhoeae infection.
In P. aeruginosa, the pilus-associated protein PilY1 is required for bacterial adherence
to mucosal epithelial tissue and mediates binding to basolateral host
cell surfaces. T4P are required for
initial colonization in cystic fibrosis, where P. aeruginosa T4aP engage glycolipid receptors
on the apical surface of airway epithelial cells. Twitching motility, driven by T4P retraction, directs the
formation of structured biofilms central to chronic infection. The bundle-forming pilus (BFP) of enteropathogenic E. coli (EPEC) mediates bacterial autoaggregation and microcolony
formation on intestinal epithelial cells, which is required for the
attaching and effacing lesion.
,
 The toxin-coregulated
pilus (TCP) of V. cholerae is both the primary intestinal
colonization factor and the receptor for the CTXφ bacteriophage
that encodes cholera toxin, linking colonization factor biogenesis
directly to the acquisition of the primary virulence determinant.
T4cP (Tad pili) contributes to pathogenesis
primarily through mediating
irreversible surface adhesion and biofilm initiation rather than dynamic
retraction-based signaling. In A. actinomycetemcomitans, Tad pili are required for the initial tight adhesion to surfaces
that precede microcolony formation and biofilm development, and clinical
isolates with deletions in the tad locus are severely
attenuated for colonization of the oral cavity and virulence in animal
models of periodontal infection. The
functional versatility of T4P across pathogenic bacteria, from generating
mechanical signals that remodel host actin to serving as the entry
receptor for a phage-borne toxin gene, reflects the evolutionary plasticity
of a shared assembly platform and underscores the difficulty of predicting
the pathogenic mechanism from filament structure alone.9.3.3Sortase-Assembled Pili in PathogenesisSortase-assembled pili are critical virulence determinants in the
Gram-positive pathogens in which they are found, mediating adhesion
to host cells and matrix components, promoting biofilm formation,
and in several cases contributing directly to immune evasion. In S. agalactiae (Group B streptococcus), pili mediate adhesion
to respiratory epithelial cells under physiological flow conditions,
promote biofilm formation, and are required for virulence in neonatal
infection models.
,
 The tip pilin PilA facilitates
the interaction of GBS with endothelial cells of the human blood–brain
barrier, contributing to blood–brain barrier penetration in
neonatal meningitis. In C. diphtheriae, three distinct pilus types (SpaA, SpaD, and SpaH) each mediate
adhesion to specific host tissues including pharyngeal, laryngeal,
and lung epithelial cells.
,
 In E. faecalis, the endocarditis and biofilm-associated pilus (Ebp) is an important
virulence factor in animal models of endocarditis and catheter-associated
urinary tract infection. Relative to
the collagen adhesin Ace, Ebp pili account for most of serum-stimulated
fibrinogen adherence in E. faecalis, and deletion
of the ebpABC operon significantly attenuates experimental
urinary tract infection. In oral commensal A. oris, type 2 fimbriae mediate coaggregation with streptococci
and are essential for oral biofilm development, illustrating the role
of sortase-assembled pili beyond canonical pathogenesis. In S. pyogenes, the FCT-region
pilus mediates adhesion to multiple host matrix components and promotes
pharyngeal colonization and biofilm formation.
,
 The broad phylogenetic distribution of sortase-assembled pili across
pathogenic and commensal Gram-positive bacteria, and the conservation
of their receptor-binding adhesin domain folds across diverse organisms,
underscores their fundamental importance as a class of adhesion organelle
in the monoderm lifestyle.
9.3.4Conjugative Pili in PathogenesisThe pathogenic significance of conjugative pili lies not in direct
host interaction but in their role as the extracellular machinery
of horizontal gene transfer, which drives the dissemination of resistance
and virulence determinants across bacterial communities. Conjugative
pili are not virulence factors in the classical sense; they do not
mediate adhesion to host tissues, but their role in horizontal gene
transfer makes them central to the dissemination of antibiotic resistance
determinants and virulence plasmids across bacterial populations.
The spread of plasmids encoding extended-spectrum β-lactamases
(ESBLs), carbapenemases (KPC, NDM-1, OXA-48), and other resistance
mechanisms through conjugative transfer is a primary driver of the
global emergence of multidrug-resistant Gram-negative bacterial infections. The pKpQIL plasmid encoding KPC-2 carbapenemase
in K. pneumoniae has been shown by cryo-EM to assemble
a conjugative pilus with a one-start helical symmetry distinct from
F-type pili, making it among the structurally characterized conjugative
elements in a clinically critical resistance plasmid. Additionally, the F pilus serves as the attachment
receptor for male-specific bacteriophages including MS2 and M13, which
exploit pilus retraction for injection of their genome into the cell. The A. tumefaciens Ti plasmid
T4SS represents a remarkable evolutionary adaptation in which the
conjugation machinery has been repurposed to transfer oncogenic T-DNA
directly into plant eukaryotic cells. The structural diversity of conjugative pili, including the stoichiometric
phospholipid incorporation unique to F-type systems and the distinct
helical symmetries of different IncF plasmid pili, suggests that conjugation
machinery has undergone substantial evolutionary divergence even as
its core function in resistance gene transfer has remained constant.
9.3.5Curli Fibers in PathogenesisCurli
fibers mediate adhesion to a broad range of host substrates. CsgA
fibers bind fibronectin, laminin, and plasminogen on host cell surfaces,
contributing to colonization and biofilm formation on both biotic
and abiotic surfaces.
,
 Curli are potent innate immune
activators. CsgA fibers are recognized by a Toll-like receptor (TLR)­2-dependent
mechanism, and amyloid fibrils of both microbial and host origin stimulate
TLR2-mediated NOS2 expression in macrophages; an amyloidogenicity-lowering
substitution in CsgA reduces this TLR2 agonist activity. TLR2 alone is not sufficient for full recognition
of curli: TLR2 cooperates with TLR1 to recognize curli amyloid fibrils
from enterobacterial biofilms, and an inability to produce curli markedly
reduces TLR2-dependent responses to intact bacterial cells both in
vitro and in vivo. Curli–DNA
complexes within the biofilm matrix activate TLR9 and drive type I
interferon production via a STING-dependent pathway, a mechanism proposed
to contribute to chronic inflammatory pathology during systemic infection. Curli production is associated with invasive Salmonella and E. coli infections. The
multicellular behavior of S. enterica and E. coli is characterized by the co-expression of curli and
cellulose, which promotes surface adhesion, biofilm formation, and
environmental persistence. The capacity
of curli to simultaneously promote surface adhesion, reinforce biofilm
mechanical stability, and activate multiple innate immune receptor
pathways positions these fibers as central orchestrators of the host–pathogen
interface during enterobacterial biofilm-associated infection.9.4Pili, Fimbriae, and Curli Therapeutic Targets9.4.1Pili, Fimbriae, and Curli Antibiotic TargetsThe most clinically advanced strategy focused on pili, fimbriae,
and curli targets FimH, the mannose-binding adhesin at the tip of
type 1 pili of uropathogenic Escherichia coli (UPEC),
both as a target for small-molecule inhibitors and as a vaccine antigen.
,

Type 1 pili of UPEC mediate initial attachment to mannosylated
uroplakins on bladder epithelial cells through the lectin domain of
the tip adhesin FimH, and this interaction is required for bladder
invasion and intracellular bacterial community formation that underlies
recurrent UTI. Mannosides, orally available β-D-mannose derivatives
designed to competitively occupy the FimH mannose-binding pocket,
block this adhesion step, keep UPEC extracellular, and sensitize otherwise-resistant
strains to antibiotics by preventing tissue invasion and prolonging
exposure to urinary drug concentrations. Type 1 pili also promote UPEC persistence in the gut, oral mannoside
administration reduces UPEC gut colonization in murine models, suggesting
that FimH inhibition could address both bladder infection and its
reservoir simultaneously. Two independent
anti-virulence strategies targeting pili biogenesis itself, pilicides
that disrupt chaperone-usher pathway assembly, were characterized in vitro against both type 1 and P pili. Additionally, the
compound FN075 inhibits curli fiber assembly, classifying it as a
dual pilicide and curlicide. However, neither pilicides nor dedicated
curli inhibitors have advanced to clinical development. Curli fibers, the functional amyloid produced
by E. coli and Salmonella, contribute
to biofilm matrix integrity through synergistic interactions with
extracellular DNA. The eDNA-curli network enhances biofilm robustness,
impedes antibiotic penetration, and sequesters antimicrobial peptides,
identifying disruption of this amyloid matrix as a mechanistically
distinct therapeutic target for biofilm-associated infections.
9.4.2Pili, Fimbriae, and Curli Vaccine TargetsFimH is the most clinically advanced pilus-based vaccine antigen.
The FimCH vaccine (recombinant FimH complexed with its chaperone FimC
and formulated with a TLR4 agonist adjuvant) was evaluated in a phase
1 clinical study. All dosages were well-tolerated with no serious
adverse events, and vaccination induced both binding and functional
anti-FimH antibodies. Long-term follow-up
data from an expanded access study in high-risk patients with carbapenem-resistant
Enterobacteriaceae showed approximately a 75% reduction in symptomatic
Gram-negative UTI occurrences in the 12-month period following administration. The current Sequoia Vaccines program has not
yet advanced to Phase 2 as of mid-2026, although the Phase 1 results
and expanded access data support continued development. Beyond type
1 pili, the RrgA adhesin of pilus islet 1 in S. pneumoniae mediates binding to PECAM-1/CD31 on brain microvascular endothelial
cells and is immunogenic, providing a mechanistic basis for including
pilus antigens in next-generation pneumococcal vaccine formulations
targeting invasive disease. No vaccine
targeting curli fibers has entered clinical development, though the
immunogenicity of curli during natural infection and the conservation
of CsgA across Enterobacteriaceae have been noted
as features that could be exploited in multiantigen formulations targeting
biofilm-forming pathogens.

Overview of the structure and organization of
extracellular appendages: Pili, fimbriae, and curli

Surface
appendages (Figure
) represent a structurally and functionally diverse
class of bacterial surface structures that extend beyond the OM (in
diderm bacteria) or the PG layer (in monoderm bacteria) into the extracellular
environment. Unlike the relatively static protein and lipid components
of the cell envelope discussed above, these filamentous structures
are dynamic, often subject to phase and antigenic variation, and represent
primary mediators of host–pathogen interaction at the earliest
stages of infection. The major categories covered here are chaperone–usher
fimbriae, type IV pili and their relatives, sortase-assembled pili
in monoderm bacteria, conjugative pili, and the functional amyloid
curli fibers.

Assembly mechanisms of bacterial surface appendages. Four classes of surface appendages are depicted, each employing
a distinct biogenesis strategy (Conjugative pili are assembled with
T4SS which is shown in Figure
). Sortase-assembled pili (left) are found
in monoderm bacteria and built through a biphasic covalent mechanism.
Pilin precursors carrying a C-terminal cell wall sorting signal (CWSS)
are retained at the membrane, where a pilus-specific sortase (SrtA)
catalyzes sequential transpeptidation reactions that covalently crosslink
individual pilin subunits into a growing polymer, proceeding from
tip to base. The sortase SrtF anchors the completed pilus to the basal
pilin. Attachment to the PG cell wall differs between species. Chaperone/usher (CU) pili (second panel) are assembled in
diderm bacteria via a periplasmic chaperone and OM usher platform.
Pilin subunits are translocated across the IM by the Sec translocon
and captured by a periplasmic chaperone, which maintains them in a
foldedstate and delivers them to the OM usher. The usher catalyzes
donor-strand exchange between successive pilin subunits, assembling
tip and rod pilin subunits into a growing fiber that is extruded through
the usher pore to the cell surface. Type IV pili (third
panel) are dynamic filaments found in both monoderm and diderm bacteria.
Prepilins are inserted into the IM by the Sec translocon and processed
by a dedicated peptidase. The platform protein and alignment proteins
at the IM coordinate subunit addition driven by an elongation ATPase,
while a retraction ATPase powers depolymerization, both using ATP
hydrolysis. The secretin forms the OM pore through which the pilus
is extruded. Curli fibers (right panel) are functional
amyloids assembled by a dedicated nucleation-precipitation mechanism.
CsgA and CsgB are synthesized as unfolded monomers in the cytoplasm,
translocated across the IM by Sec, and exported to the cell surface
through the OM pore formed by CsgG/E, assisted by the accessory protein
CsgF. CsgB acts as a nucleator that templates the polymerization of
CsgA into amyloid fibers on the cell surface. CsgC prevents premature
intracellular aggregation of CsgA and CsgB in the periplasm. Created
in https://BioRender.com.

9.1Chaperone–Usher PiliThe chaperone/usher
(CU) pathway (Figure
) is the most widespread mechanism by which Gram-negative bacteria
assemble adhesive surface filaments called pili or fimbriae.
,
 CU pili are found predominantly in Enterobacteriaceae and related
diderm pathogens, where they mediate attachment to host tissues as
a critical early step in infection. The best-characterized examples
are type 1 pili, which enable uropathogenic E. coli (UPEC) to colonize the bladder epithelium, and P pili, which mediate
kidney colonization in pyelonephritis. Within UPEC alone, multiple distinct CU systems are expressed, including
F1C pili mediating binding to renal tubular cells, S fimbriae targeting
sialic acid-containing receptors in the urinary tract and meninges,
and Dr/Afa fimbriae recognizing decay-accelerating factor on uroepithelial
and endothelial cells. CU fimbriae extend
well beyond uropathogens: colonization factor antigen I (CFA/I) and
related CS fimbriae are assembled by this pathway in enterotoxigenic E. coli (ETEC), where they mediate adhesion to the small
intestinal epithelium and are essential for diarrheal disease.
,
 CU systems have also been identified in H. influenzae, K. pneumoniae, Salmonella species,
and A. baumannii, underscoring their broad distribution
and importance across Gram-negative pathogens.
CU pili are composite structures built from a series
of protein subunits, each sharing an incomplete immunoglobulin-like
fold that lacks the seventh C-terminal β-strand. The assembled
filament consists of two morphologically distinct regions: a rigid,
helical rod composed of hundreds to thousands of copies of the major
pilin subunit (FimA in type 1 pili, PapA in P pili), and a short,
flexible tip fibrillum that terminates in a tip adhesin responsible
for receptor binding (FimH in type 1 pili, PapG in P pili).
,
 Between the rod and the adhesin, several minor subunits occupy defined
positions: in type 1 pili, FimF and FimG form the junction between
rod and tip, while in P pili, PapE, PapF, and PapK occupy equivalent
linker positions. All subunits are incorporated
through a mechanism called donor strand complementation, in which
the N-terminal extension of each incoming subunit donates a β-strand
to complete the immunoglobulin fold of its neighbor, creating an interlocked
polymer.
,
 This non-covalent but highly
stable interstrand bonding gives the rod its mechanical resilience
and its capacity to uncoil under tensile stress and recoil upon relaxation,
a property proposed to protect bacteria from detachment under shear
flow in the urinary tract.
Crystal
structures of tip complexes, including FimH-FimG-FimF-FimC
at 2.7 Å and the equivalent PapD-PapA system, reveal the geometry
of this donor strand exchange and the conformational changes each
subunit undergoes upon interlocking with its neighbor.
,,
 In the assembled rod, hundreds
to thousands of major pilin subunits are wound into a right-handed
superhelix stabilized entirely by these non-covalent interstrand contacts,
giving the filament both mechanical resilience and the capacity to
uncoil reversibly under tensile stress. The tip adhesin domains adopt a conserved elongated β-barrel
jelly-roll fold that presents the receptor-binding site at its apical
surface, a scaffold shared across structurally characterized CU adhesins
including FimH, PapG, GafD, and DraE despite minimal sequence identity.
,

9.1.1Type IV PiliType IV pili (T4P)
(Figure
) are highly
dynamic surface filaments found across a broad phylogenetic range
of both Gram-negative and Gram-positive bacteria as well as archaea,
making them one of the most widely distributed bacterial surface organelles
known.
,
 T4P mediate a remarkable diversity of cellular
functions, including adhesion to host tissues and abiotic surfaces,
twitching motility driven by cycles of filament extension and retraction,
natural competence for DNA uptake, bacteriophage infection, and biofilm
formation.
,
 T4P are divided into three subclasses:
type IVa (T4aP), type IVb (T4bP), and type IVc (T4cP, also called
Tad or tight adherence pili), which differ in pilin subunit size,
the extent of N-terminal processing, assembly machinery composition,
and functional outputs, though all share the defining features of
the superfamily.
,,
 In pathogenic bacteria, T4P are critical virulence determinants: N. gonorrhoeae and N. meningitidis use
T4P to colonize mucosal epithelium and resist phagocytosis; P. aeruginosa T4P mediate initial attachment to airway epithelium
and are required for establishing chronic lung infections in cystic
fibrosis patients; Vibrio cholerae toxin-coregulated
pilus (TCP) is essential for intestinal colonization and is among
the most potent colonization factors known; and in enteropathogenic E. coli (EPEC), the bundle-forming pilus (BFP) mediates
microcolony formation on the intestinal epithelium.
,
 T4cP (Tad pili) are found in an equally broad range of organisms
including Aggregatibacter actinomycetemcomitans, Caulobacter crescentus, Myxococcus xanthus, and Vibrio vulnificus, where they mediate surface
adhesion, biofilm formation, cell–cell aggregation, and surface
sensing.
−


The T4P filament is a homo- or hetero-polymeric
helical assembly built primarily from thousands of copies of the major
pilin subunit, designated PilA or PilE depending on the organism,
along with minor pilins that occupy the filament tip and play roles
in initiating assembly and mediating adhesion.
,
 In T4aP and T4bP systems, each pilin subunit consists of a conserved
hydrophobic N-terminal α-helix (α1) that forms the core
of the filament, packed against a globular C-terminal head domain
containing a four-stranded antiparallel β-sheet and variable
surface-exposed loops that determine receptor specificity and antigenicity.
,
 The N-terminal α1 helix partially unfolds at a conserved segment
near the helix base, and this flexible region accommodates the tight
packing geometry of subunits in the filament interior.
,
 T4cP Flp pilins are structurally distinct: they lack the globular
C-terminal β-sheet head domain entirely and are instead purely
α-helical, producing a thinner and structurally simpler filament
than those of T4aP and T4bP systems. n all three subclasses, filament stability arises from hydrophobic
interactions among the N-terminal helices of adjacent subunits rather
than from the intermolecular β-strand exchange that stabilizes
CUP pili.
,,

The atomic structure of individual pilin subunits has been
determined
for multiple T4P systems. The N. gonorrhoeae PilE
pilin crystal structure established the canonical T4aP pilin fold:
a long N-terminal α-helix packing against a globular C-terminal
domain containing a disulfide-bonded D-region loop that is critical
for receptor recognition and immune evasion through antigenic variation. Cryo-EM reconstructions of intact filaments
from N. gonorrhoeae and P. aeruginosa revealed how subunits pack in the assembled fiber, with the hydrophobic
N-terminal helices forming a tight apolar core and the globular C-terminal
domains coating the outer surface and presenting variable loops for
host interactions.
,
 A structure of M. xanthus T4aP revealed that species-specific differences in pilin length
and intersubunit contact geometry produce measurable differences in
filament mechanical properties, linking atomic-level structural variation
to the macroscopic function of motility on varied surfaces. For T4cP, the first intact in situ structure
of the complete Tad pilus machine was resolved in C. crescentus by cryo-ET and subtomogram averaging, revealing that the Tad filament
is built from purely α-helical PilA subunits lacking the globular
C-terminal head domain of T4aP and T4bP pilins, and capturing multiple
assembly intermediates of the nanomachine within the intact cell envelope.
9.1.2Sortase-Assembled Pili in Monoderm BacteriaSortase-assembled pili (Figure
) are surface appendages found exclusively in Gram-positive
bacteria, where they serve as critical mediators of adhesion, colonization,
biofilm formation, and immune evasion.
,
 This class
of pili is named after the transpeptidases (sortases) that polymerize
the pilus (class E) and attach the pilus to the cell wall (a housekeeping
sortase of class F). They are broadly distributed across clinically
significant pathogens and commensals, including S. pyogenes, S. agalactiae, S. pneumoniae, E. faecalis, E. faecium, Corynebacterium
diphtheriae, Actinomyces oris, and Bacillus cereus, as well as gut commensals such as Lactobacillus and Bifidobacterium species.
,−


 In addition, single bacterial species have more than one type of
sortase-assembled pili. There are two classes of sortase pili: heterotrimeric
and heterodimeric. Heterotrimeric sortase pili have one protein forming
the base attachment to the cell wall (basal pilin), the polymerized
subunit forming the rod (the major or backbone pilin), and the tip
protein (minor pilin adhesins). Heterodimeric lack a basal pilin but
have tip and major pilin proteins. Although bacteria all have this
architecture, the individual subunits differ in sequence and structure.
Most basal, major, and minor pilins have an immunoglobulin (Ig)-like
fold β-sandwich domain named collagen adhesin (Cna) and are
divided into two subfamilies CnaA and B. All Cna folds have isopeptides;
however, the number of Cna domains and the placement of the isopeptide
bonds differ. The structure of the S. pyogenes major
pilin has two Cna domains, each is stabilized by an intramolecular
isopeptide bond between a lysine and an asparagine that contributes
to the stability of the individual subunits.
,
 Each major pilin is connected to the next by an intermolecular isopeptide
bond between a lysine in one subunit with the carboxyl terminus of
another subunit. In contrast, the S. pneumoniae major pilin has four domains (D1-4) each with
an intermolecular isopeptide and an intermolecular isopeptide bond
between D1 and D4. The adhesion subunits
have a binding domain that adopts a classic α/β open-twisted
(or para-Rossmann) fold (also termed von Willebrand factor type A
(vWFA) fold). The vWFA domains typically have a metal ion-dependent
adhesion site (MIDAS; D-x-S-x-S-T-D) and bind extracellular matrix
proteins such as mucin, laminin, collagen, fibrinogen, and fibronectin.
,
 The vWFA domain is followed by a series of Can domains. In Lactobacillus rhamnosus GG, the vWFA is followed by a CnaA
domain and three CnaB domains. In contrast,
the minor pilin of S. pneumoniae has four domains:
the vWFA domain followed by one CnaB and two CnaA domains.
9.1.3Conjugative PiliConjugative pili
are surface appendages produced by donor bacteria that mediate the
initial cell–cell contact required for bacterial conjugation,
the direct transfer of DNA between cells, making them the primary
drivers of horizontal gene transfer across bacterial populations.
,
 They are encoded by conjugative plasmids and integrative conjugative
elements (ICEs) found across a wide range of Gram-negative and Gram-positive
bacteria, with the F plasmid-encoded system of E. coli serving as the paradigm.
,
 The conjugative pilus
is an extracellular appendage of the type IV secretion system (T4SS),
which spans the entire cell envelope and provides the channel through
which the DNA-protein transfer complex is delivered to the recipient
cell; the T4SS machinery itself is discussed in detail in section
10.0.4.
,
 Because conjugative plasmids frequently
carry antibiotic resistance and virulence genes, conjugative pili
represent a central mechanism in the dissemination of antimicrobial
resistance across bacterial communities.
,

Conjugative pili are thin, flexible tubular filaments approximately
8–9 nm in outer diameter with a hollow lumen of approximately
2.5–3 nm.
−

 The filament is built from a single major
pilin species, TraA in F-type systems and VirB2 in Agrobacterium
tumefaciens T-pili, that is processed from a propilin precursor
synthesized with an unusually long leader peptide of approximately
50 amino acids.
,
 The mature TraA pilin is approximately
64 amino acids long and folds into a purely α-helical structure
comprising three helices (α1, α2, α3), with no β-sheet
component. A defining and unexpected
structural feature revealed by cryo-EM is that pilin subunits assemble
with phospholipid molecules at a strict 1:1 stoichiometric ratio:
,
 mass spectrometry confirmed these are phosphatidylglycerol (PG)
species (primarily PG 32:1, PG 33:1, and PG 34:1) selectively incorporated
from the IM, with PG head groups exposed to the electronegative lumen
and acyl chains buried between adjacent helical segments. Mutations in the phospholipid binding sites
(Y37V and A28F in pED208) abolish pilus assembly, demonstrating that
lipid incorporation is structurally essential and not incidental. The helical symmetry of the assembled filament
varies between systems: F-type and pED208-type pili display five-fold
rotational symmetry (five-start helical filaments), while the pKpQIL
pilus from carbapenem-resistant K. pneumoniae forms
a one-start.
,
 An additional structural variant
is the H-pilus of IncH plasmids, in which the TrhA pilin undergoes
covalent cyclization connecting the N- and C-termini following cleavage
of the last five residues; this cyclization is essential for pilus
biogenesis and efficient plasmid transfer.
9.1.4Curli FibersCurli (Figure
) are functional amyloid fibers
that form the major proteinaceous component of the extracellular matrix
in biofilms produced by many Gram-negative bacteria. They were first
identified in Salmonella and E. coli and are phylogenetically widespread across at least four bacterial
phyla, including Proteobacteria and Bacteroidetes, with curli genes
detectable in environmental, clinical, and commensal metagenomes. Within the biofilm matrix, curli mediate initial
adhesion to abiotic and biotic surfaces, provide structural cohesion
by forming interbacterial bundles, and contribute to the mechanical
stability and stress resistance of the biofilm community.
,
 A notable biological feature is the capacity of curli fibers for
cross-species seeding: curli subunit homologues from E. coli, Salmonella, and Citrobacter cross-seed
one another in vitro and support interspecies fiber
formation in mixed-species biofilms, a process facilitated by the
high conservation of the curlin repeat architecture.
,

In E. coli, the curli system is encoded by
two divergently transcribed operons, csgBAC and csgDEFG, controlled by the master transcriptional regulator
CsgD and the stationary-phase sigma factor σS. CsgA is the major structural subunit and principal polymerizing
component; CsgB is a minor subunit that serves as the membrane-anchored
nucleator of CsgA amyloidogenesis; both are approximately 13 kDa polypeptides. Each subunit carries a 20-residue N-terminal
signal peptide for Sec-dependent export, followed by a 22-residue
N-terminal targeting sequence (N22) that directs the subunit to the
OM secretion machinery (CsgG), and an amyloidogenic core composed
of five imperfect pseudo-repeat sequences of approximately 22 amino
acids (R1–R5); each repeat forms a strand-arc-strand β-arch
unit built from two motifs flanked by conserved Asn and Gln residues.
,
 Cryo-EM analysis combined with ΑFold2 modeling of CsgA established
that the folded subunit adopts a left-handed β-solenoid, and
solid-state NMR had previously shown that curli fibers do not adopt
the in-register parallel β-sheet architecture of pathological
amyloids.
,
 In the assembled protofibril,
successive CsgA subunits stack head-to-tail via seamless β-solenoid
augmentation with pure translational symmetry and no helical twist,
producing a fiber with 4.8 Å inter-repeat spacing, approximately
20 Å width, and a half-unit stagger between opposing β-strands. CsgB adopts a structurally equivalent β-solenoid
compatible with CsgA fiber extension; the lower intrinsic polymerization
propensity of CsgB compared to CsgA is associated with degenerate
residues in its C-terminal repeats R4 and R5.
,
 High-speed AFM imaging at single-fiber resolution demonstrated that
curli fibers grow from both ends with asymmetric kinetics and display
stop-and-go dynamics in which bursts of steady-state elongation alternate
with periods of stagnation.

Chaperone–Usher Pili

The chaperone/usher
(CU) pathway (Figure
) is the most widespread mechanism by which Gram-negative bacteria
assemble adhesive surface filaments called pili or fimbriae.
,
 CU pili are found predominantly in Enterobacteriaceae and related
diderm pathogens, where they mediate attachment to host tissues as
a critical early step in infection. The best-characterized examples
are type 1 pili, which enable uropathogenic E. coli (UPEC) to colonize the bladder epithelium, and P pili, which mediate
kidney colonization in pyelonephritis. Within UPEC alone, multiple distinct CU systems are expressed, including
F1C pili mediating binding to renal tubular cells, S fimbriae targeting
sialic acid-containing receptors in the urinary tract and meninges,
and Dr/Afa fimbriae recognizing decay-accelerating factor on uroepithelial
and endothelial cells. CU fimbriae extend
well beyond uropathogens: colonization factor antigen I (CFA/I) and
related CS fimbriae are assembled by this pathway in enterotoxigenic E. coli (ETEC), where they mediate adhesion to the small
intestinal epithelium and are essential for diarrheal disease.
,
 CU systems have also been identified in H. influenzae, K. pneumoniae, Salmonella species,
and A. baumannii, underscoring their broad distribution
and importance across Gram-negative pathogens.

CU pili are composite structures built from a series
of protein subunits, each sharing an incomplete immunoglobulin-like
fold that lacks the seventh C-terminal β-strand. The assembled
filament consists of two morphologically distinct regions: a rigid,
helical rod composed of hundreds to thousands of copies of the major
pilin subunit (FimA in type 1 pili, PapA in P pili), and a short,
flexible tip fibrillum that terminates in a tip adhesin responsible
for receptor binding (FimH in type 1 pili, PapG in P pili).
,
 Between the rod and the adhesin, several minor subunits occupy defined
positions: in type 1 pili, FimF and FimG form the junction between
rod and tip, while in P pili, PapE, PapF, and PapK occupy equivalent
linker positions. All subunits are incorporated
through a mechanism called donor strand complementation, in which
the N-terminal extension of each incoming subunit donates a β-strand
to complete the immunoglobulin fold of its neighbor, creating an interlocked
polymer.
,
 This non-covalent but highly
stable interstrand bonding gives the rod its mechanical resilience
and its capacity to uncoil under tensile stress and recoil upon relaxation,
a property proposed to protect bacteria from detachment under shear
flow in the urinary tract.

Crystal
structures of tip complexes, including FimH-FimG-FimF-FimC
at 2.7 Å and the equivalent PapD-PapA system, reveal the geometry
of this donor strand exchange and the conformational changes each
subunit undergoes upon interlocking with its neighbor.
,,
 In the assembled rod, hundreds
to thousands of major pilin subunits are wound into a right-handed
superhelix stabilized entirely by these non-covalent interstrand contacts,
giving the filament both mechanical resilience and the capacity to
uncoil reversibly under tensile stress. The tip adhesin domains adopt a conserved elongated β-barrel
jelly-roll fold that presents the receptor-binding site at its apical
surface, a scaffold shared across structurally characterized CU adhesins
including FimH, PapG, GafD, and DraE despite minimal sequence identity.
,

9.1.1Type IV PiliType IV pili (T4P)
(Figure
) are highly
dynamic surface filaments found across a broad phylogenetic range
of both Gram-negative and Gram-positive bacteria as well as archaea,
making them one of the most widely distributed bacterial surface organelles
known.
,
 T4P mediate a remarkable diversity of cellular
functions, including adhesion to host tissues and abiotic surfaces,
twitching motility driven by cycles of filament extension and retraction,
natural competence for DNA uptake, bacteriophage infection, and biofilm
formation.
,
 T4P are divided into three subclasses:
type IVa (T4aP), type IVb (T4bP), and type IVc (T4cP, also called
Tad or tight adherence pili), which differ in pilin subunit size,
the extent of N-terminal processing, assembly machinery composition,
and functional outputs, though all share the defining features of
the superfamily.
,,
 In pathogenic bacteria, T4P are critical virulence determinants: N. gonorrhoeae and N. meningitidis use
T4P to colonize mucosal epithelium and resist phagocytosis; P. aeruginosa T4P mediate initial attachment to airway epithelium
and are required for establishing chronic lung infections in cystic
fibrosis patients; Vibrio cholerae toxin-coregulated
pilus (TCP) is essential for intestinal colonization and is among
the most potent colonization factors known; and in enteropathogenic E. coli (EPEC), the bundle-forming pilus (BFP) mediates
microcolony formation on the intestinal epithelium.
,
 T4cP (Tad pili) are found in an equally broad range of organisms
including Aggregatibacter actinomycetemcomitans, Caulobacter crescentus, Myxococcus xanthus, and Vibrio vulnificus, where they mediate surface
adhesion, biofilm formation, cell–cell aggregation, and surface
sensing.
−


The T4P filament is a homo- or hetero-polymeric
helical assembly built primarily from thousands of copies of the major
pilin subunit, designated PilA or PilE depending on the organism,
along with minor pilins that occupy the filament tip and play roles
in initiating assembly and mediating adhesion.
,
 In T4aP and T4bP systems, each pilin subunit consists of a conserved
hydrophobic N-terminal α-helix (α1) that forms the core
of the filament, packed against a globular C-terminal head domain
containing a four-stranded antiparallel β-sheet and variable
surface-exposed loops that determine receptor specificity and antigenicity.
,
 The N-terminal α1 helix partially unfolds at a conserved segment
near the helix base, and this flexible region accommodates the tight
packing geometry of subunits in the filament interior.
,
 T4cP Flp pilins are structurally distinct: they lack the globular
C-terminal β-sheet head domain entirely and are instead purely
α-helical, producing a thinner and structurally simpler filament
than those of T4aP and T4bP systems. n all three subclasses, filament stability arises from hydrophobic
interactions among the N-terminal helices of adjacent subunits rather
than from the intermolecular β-strand exchange that stabilizes
CUP pili.
,,

The atomic structure of individual pilin subunits has been
determined
for multiple T4P systems. The N. gonorrhoeae PilE
pilin crystal structure established the canonical T4aP pilin fold:
a long N-terminal α-helix packing against a globular C-terminal
domain containing a disulfide-bonded D-region loop that is critical
for receptor recognition and immune evasion through antigenic variation. Cryo-EM reconstructions of intact filaments
from N. gonorrhoeae and P. aeruginosa revealed how subunits pack in the assembled fiber, with the hydrophobic
N-terminal helices forming a tight apolar core and the globular C-terminal
domains coating the outer surface and presenting variable loops for
host interactions.
,
 A structure of M. xanthus T4aP revealed that species-specific differences in pilin length
and intersubunit contact geometry produce measurable differences in
filament mechanical properties, linking atomic-level structural variation
to the macroscopic function of motility on varied surfaces. For T4cP, the first intact in situ structure
of the complete Tad pilus machine was resolved in C. crescentus by cryo-ET and subtomogram averaging, revealing that the Tad filament
is built from purely α-helical PilA subunits lacking the globular
C-terminal head domain of T4aP and T4bP pilins, and capturing multiple
assembly intermediates of the nanomachine within the intact cell envelope.

9.1.2Sortase-Assembled Pili in Monoderm BacteriaSortase-assembled pili (Figure
) are surface appendages found exclusively in Gram-positive
bacteria, where they serve as critical mediators of adhesion, colonization,
biofilm formation, and immune evasion.
,
 This class
of pili is named after the transpeptidases (sortases) that polymerize
the pilus (class E) and attach the pilus to the cell wall (a housekeeping
sortase of class F). They are broadly distributed across clinically
significant pathogens and commensals, including S. pyogenes, S. agalactiae, S. pneumoniae, E. faecalis, E. faecium, Corynebacterium
diphtheriae, Actinomyces oris, and Bacillus cereus, as well as gut commensals such as Lactobacillus and Bifidobacterium species.
,−


 In addition, single bacterial species have more than one type of
sortase-assembled pili. There are two classes of sortase pili: heterotrimeric
and heterodimeric. Heterotrimeric sortase pili have one protein forming
the base attachment to the cell wall (basal pilin), the polymerized
subunit forming the rod (the major or backbone pilin), and the tip
protein (minor pilin adhesins). Heterodimeric lack a basal pilin but
have tip and major pilin proteins. Although bacteria all have this
architecture, the individual subunits differ in sequence and structure.
Most basal, major, and minor pilins have an immunoglobulin (Ig)-like
fold β-sandwich domain named collagen adhesin (Cna) and are
divided into two subfamilies CnaA and B. All Cna folds have isopeptides;
however, the number of Cna domains and the placement of the isopeptide
bonds differ. The structure of the S. pyogenes major
pilin has two Cna domains, each is stabilized by an intramolecular
isopeptide bond between a lysine and an asparagine that contributes
to the stability of the individual subunits.
,
 Each major pilin is connected to the next by an intermolecular isopeptide
bond between a lysine in one subunit with the carboxyl terminus of
another subunit. In contrast, the S. pneumoniae major pilin has four domains (D1-4) each with
an intermolecular isopeptide and an intermolecular isopeptide bond
between D1 and D4. The adhesion subunits
have a binding domain that adopts a classic α/β open-twisted
(or para-Rossmann) fold (also termed von Willebrand factor type A
(vWFA) fold). The vWFA domains typically have a metal ion-dependent
adhesion site (MIDAS; D-x-S-x-S-T-D) and bind extracellular matrix
proteins such as mucin, laminin, collagen, fibrinogen, and fibronectin.
,
 The vWFA domain is followed by a series of Can domains. In Lactobacillus rhamnosus GG, the vWFA is followed by a CnaA
domain and three CnaB domains. In contrast,
the minor pilin of S. pneumoniae has four domains:
the vWFA domain followed by one CnaB and two CnaA domains.

Sortase-Assembled Pili in Monoderm Bacteria

Sortase-assembled pili (Figure

bacteria, where they serve as critical mediators of adhesion, colonization,
biofilm formation, and immune evasion.

of pili is named after the transpeptidases (sortases) that polymerize

diphtheriae, Actinomyces oris, and Bacillus cereus, as well as gut commensals such as Lactobacillus and Bifidobacterium species.

fold β-sandwich domain named collagen adhesin (Cna) and are
divided into two subfamilies CnaA and B. All Cna folds have isopeptides;
however, the number of Cna domains and the placement of the isopeptide
bonds differ. The structure of the S. pyogenes major
pilin has two Cna domains, each is stabilized by an intramolecular

to the stability of the individual subunits.

bond between a lysine in one subunit with the carboxyl terminus of
another subunit. In contrast, the S. pneumoniae major pilin has four domains (D1-4) each with
an intermolecular isopeptide and an intermolecular isopeptide bond
between D1 and D4. The adhesion subunits
have a binding domain that adopts a classic α/β open-twisted
(or para-Rossmann) fold (also termed von Willebrand factor type A
(vWFA) fold). The vWFA domains typically have a metal ion-dependent

domain and three CnaB domains. In contrast,

the vWFA domain followed by one CnaB and two CnaA domains.

9.1.3Conjugative PiliConjugative pili
are surface appendages produced by donor bacteria that mediate the
initial cell–cell contact required for bacterial conjugation,
the direct transfer of DNA between cells, making them the primary
drivers of horizontal gene transfer across bacterial populations.
,
 They are encoded by conjugative plasmids and integrative conjugative
elements (ICEs) found across a wide range of Gram-negative and Gram-positive
bacteria, with the F plasmid-encoded system of E. coli serving as the paradigm.
,
 The conjugative pilus
is an extracellular appendage of the type IV secretion system (T4SS),
which spans the entire cell envelope and provides the channel through
which the DNA-protein transfer complex is delivered to the recipient
cell; the T4SS machinery itself is discussed in detail in section
10.0.4.
,
 Because conjugative plasmids frequently
carry antibiotic resistance and virulence genes, conjugative pili
represent a central mechanism in the dissemination of antimicrobial
resistance across bacterial communities.
,

Conjugative pili are thin, flexible tubular filaments approximately
8–9 nm in outer diameter with a hollow lumen of approximately
2.5–3 nm.
−

 The filament is built from a single major
pilin species, TraA in F-type systems and VirB2 in Agrobacterium
tumefaciens T-pili, that is processed from a propilin precursor
synthesized with an unusually long leader peptide of approximately
50 amino acids.
,
 The mature TraA pilin is approximately
64 amino acids long and folds into a purely α-helical structure
comprising three helices (α1, α2, α3), with no β-sheet
component. A defining and unexpected
structural feature revealed by cryo-EM is that pilin subunits assemble
with phospholipid molecules at a strict 1:1 stoichiometric ratio:
,
 mass spectrometry confirmed these are phosphatidylglycerol (PG)
species (primarily PG 32:1, PG 33:1, and PG 34:1) selectively incorporated
from the IM, with PG head groups exposed to the electronegative lumen
and acyl chains buried between adjacent helical segments. Mutations in the phospholipid binding sites
(Y37V and A28F in pED208) abolish pilus assembly, demonstrating that
lipid incorporation is structurally essential and not incidental. The helical symmetry of the assembled filament
varies between systems: F-type and pED208-type pili display five-fold
rotational symmetry (five-start helical filaments), while the pKpQIL
pilus from carbapenem-resistant K. pneumoniae forms
a one-start.
,
 An additional structural variant
is the H-pilus of IncH plasmids, in which the TrhA pilin undergoes
covalent cyclization connecting the N- and C-termini following cleavage
of the last five residues; this cyclization is essential for pilus
biogenesis and efficient plasmid transfer.

Conjugative Pili

Conjugative pili
are surface appendages produced by donor bacteria that mediate the
initial cell–cell contact required for bacterial conjugation,
the direct transfer of DNA between cells, making them the primary
drivers of horizontal gene transfer across bacterial populations.
,
 They are encoded by conjugative plasmids and integrative conjugative
elements (ICEs) found across a wide range of Gram-negative and Gram-positive
bacteria, with the F plasmid-encoded system of E. coli serving as the paradigm.
,
 The conjugative pilus
is an extracellular appendage of the type IV secretion system (T4SS),
which spans the entire cell envelope and provides the channel through
which the DNA-protein transfer complex is delivered to the recipient
cell; the T4SS machinery itself is discussed in detail in section
10.0.4.
,

carry antibiotic resistance and virulence genes, conjugative pili
represent a central mechanism in the dissemination of antimicrobial
resistance across bacterial communities.

8–9 nm in outer diameter with a hollow lumen of approximately
2.5–3 nm.
−

pilin species, TraA in F-type systems and VirB2 in Agrobacterium
tumefaciens T-pili, that is processed from a propilin precursor
synthesized with an unusually long leader peptide of approximately
50 amino acids.
,
 The mature TraA pilin is approximately
64 amino acids long and folds into a purely α-helical structure
comprising three helices (α1, α2, α3), with no β-sheet
component. A defining and unexpected
structural feature revealed by cryo-EM is that pilin subunits assemble
with phospholipid molecules at a strict 1:1 stoichiometric ratio:
,
 mass spectrometry confirmed these are phosphatidylglycerol (PG)
species (primarily PG 32:1, PG 33:1, and PG 34:1) selectively incorporated
from the IM, with PG head groups exposed to the electronegative lumen
and acyl chains buried between adjacent helical segments. Mutations in the phospholipid binding sites
(Y37V and A28F in pED208) abolish pilus assembly, demonstrating that

varies between systems: F-type and pED208-type pili display five-fold
rotational symmetry (five-start helical filaments), while the pKpQIL
pilus from carbapenem-resistant K. pneumoniae forms
a one-start.
,
 An additional structural variant
is the H-pilus of IncH plasmids, in which the TrhA pilin undergoes
covalent cyclization connecting the N- and C-termini following cleavage
