10.1.2Type 3 Secretion SystemVirulence-associated
type 3 secretion systems (T3SSs) are specialized, syringe-like nanomachines
in diderm bacteria that mediate the direct translocation of effector
proteins from the bacterial cytoplasm into eukaryotic host cells (Figure
).
−




 Its architecture spans both bacterial membranes and extends outward
as a needle-like projection that can dock onto host membranes.
−




 The basal structure consists of a multiring scaffold embedded in
the inner and OMs, connected to a central export conduit.
−

 On the cytosolic side, a sorting platform has been proposed to coordinate
substrate recognition and delivery, while an associated ATPase complex
facilitates substrate processing and engagement.
−


 Extending from the OM is a rigid needle filament, which terminates
in a tip complex that mediates host cell contact and assembly of a
translocon pore within the host membrane, thereby completing a continuous
channel from the bacterial cytoplasm into the host cytosol.
,
 Substrates include effector proteins that manipulate host cell signaling
pathways, as well as structural components required for system assembly
and function. These effectors typically
lack cleavable signal peptides and are instead recognized through
N-terminal secretion signals and chaperone-mediated targeting mechanisms.
The T3SS was first identified in the 1990s through studies of bacterial
pathogenesis,
,
 and its structural organization
has been resolved in remarkable detail by cryo-electron microscopy
and cryo-electron tomography, revealing near-atomic models of the
basal body, needle, and associated cytosolic components.
−



,

The T3SS operates as a
highly coordinated protein export machine that couples ATP hydrolysis
and the proton motive force to drive substrate unfolding and translocation
through a narrow (∼2–3 nm) channel.
,,
 Substrates are maintained in
an unfolded or partially unfolded state, often through interactions
with specific chaperones that guide them to the secretion apparatus.
,−

 Upon engagement, substrates are threaded
through the export conduit in a process that involves sequential unfolding
and linearization, followed by rapid passage through the needle into
the host cell.
,
 The ATPase is proposed to function
in substrate recognition, chaperone release, and initial unfolding,
,
 while the proton motive force contributes to sustained translocation,
although the precise division of labor between these energy sources
remains an active area of investigation.
,,,
 Additional dynamic processes include hierarchical substrate switching
during assembly (from structural components to translocators to effectors),
−

 gating of the export apparatus,
,
 and dynamic
turnover of T3SS subunits or complexes thereof.
−


 Outstanding questions include how substrates are selectively prioritized,
which path(s) the substrates take prior to being threaded into the
needle, and how energy transduction is coordinated across the multi-component
needle complex.
−


 In comparison to other secretion systems, T3SS is distinguished
by its ability to inject proteins directly into host cells through
a continuous trans-kingdom conduit, its reliance on a needle-like
delivery apparatus, and its central role in bacterial virulence, setting
it apart from systems that primarily mediate extracellular secretion
or periplasmic export.

Type 3 Secretion System

−

10.1.3Type 4 Secretion SystemThe Type
4 secretion system (T4SS) is a versatile trans-envelope machine found
in both diderm and monoderm bacteria that mediates the transfer of
macromolecules, including proteins and nucleoprotein complexes, across
cellular membranes (Figure
).
−


 In diderm bacteria, the system spans the IM and OM and often extends
outward as a pilus-like appendage that can contact recipient cells.
High-resolution structures revealed large, multi-layered assemblies
with pronounced architectural complexity.
,−


 The core architecture consists of an IM assembly platform containing
multiple ATPases, a periplasm-spanning scaffold, and an OM pore complex
that together form a continuous conduit. Many Type 4 systems also
contain an extracellular pilus or filament that facilitates cell–cell
contact and exchange of genetic material.
,
 Substrates therefore include DNA–protein complexes involved
in conjugation, effector proteins delivered into host cells, and enzymes
or toxins associated with intercellular interactions. Substrate targeting is frequently mediated
by C-terminal or internal secretion signals, often in conjunction
with adaptor proteins that couple substrates to the secretion machinery.
−

 The T4SS was first characterized in the context of bacterial conjugation
in the mid-20th century, and subsequent work has revealed its broader
role in horizontal gene transfer and pathogenesis.
,,,

Mechanistically, T4SS function is driven by ATP hydrolysis
at multiple cytosolic ATPase sites, which coordinate substrate processing,
channel opening, and, in some systems, pilus biogenesis.
−

 Substrate transfer is initiated by recruitment to the IM platform,
where conformational changes associated with ATP binding and hydrolysis
are thought to promote engagement with the translocation channel.
−

 Secretion of DNA substrates is often coupled to relaxase-mediated
processing, whereas protein substrates are translocated in a manner
that may involve partial unfolding and threading through the conduit.
,,
 In systems that form pili, cycles
of extension and retraction may contribute to establishing contact
with recipient cells and facilitating transfer, although the relationship
between pilus dynamics and substrate movement remains incompletely
defined.
,
 Despite extensive structural
and biochemical characterization, key questions remain, including
how T4SSs assemble in vivo,
,
 how substrates of different classes are selectively recognized and
which assembly state(s) facilitate their secretion,
,
 how substrates traverse the cell envelope,
,
 how energy transduction is coordinated across multiple ATPases, and how channel gating is regulated to maintain
envelope integrity.
,,,,
 In comparison to other secretion systems, T4SS is distinguished
by its ability to transport both proteins and DNA, its frequent role
in cell-to-cell transfer, and its evolutionary relationship to conjugative
machinery, setting it apart from systems that are dedicated solely
to protein export or host cell intoxication.

10.1.4Type 5 Secretion SystemThe Type
5 secretion system (T5SS) is a widespread pathway in diderm bacteria
that mediates the export of proteins across the OM following prior
translocation into the periplasm via the Sec system (Figure
).
−

 This pathway has historically been termed the autotransporter pathway
,
 although this designation is mechanistically misleading for several
T5SS subfamilies because passenger translocation is not autonomous.
In classical Type Va autotransporters, the passenger and a C-terminal
β-barrel domain are encoded within the same polypeptide; however,
the β-barrel assembly machinery (BAM) participates directly
in secretion. During assembly, the nascent substrate β-barrel
engages the lateral gate of BamA to form a transient BamA–substrate
hybrid barrel, and experimental mapping of a translocation intermediate
demonstrated that the passenger traverses the lumen of BamA rather
than the lumen of the fully assembled substrate β-barrel. Type Vc trimeric autotransporters likewise
depend on BAM for assembly and surface exposure, although three substrate
polypeptides first associate to form a partially folded trimeric β-barrel
intermediate in the periplasm before OM insertion and passenger translocation.
,
 In contrast, Type Vb two-partner secretion systems contain a separately
encoded Omp85-family transporter whose mature β-barrel constitutes
the dedicated OM translocation channel for its cognate passenger protein.
Type Vd proteins combine an Omp85-family β-barrel with an N-terminal
effector domain, but recent structural work indicates that this subfamily
may differ substantially from classical autotransporters, and the
extent to which its effector domains undergo extracellular translocation
remains unresolved. The Type Ve subfamily,
also known as inverse autotransporters, represents an important pathogenic
variant in which the topology is inverted relative to classical autotransporters:
the β-barrel translocation unit is N-terminal and the passenger
domain is C-terminal and surface-exposed; canonical examples include
intimin from enteropathogenic E. coli and invasin
from Yersinia species, which are discussed in the
context of OM adhesins in Section
. Substrates
are typically adhesins, proteases, toxins, and surface-exposed virulence
factors, many of which function in host interaction, immune evasion,
or biofilm formation.
,
 The system was first described
in the late 1980s and early 1990s through studies of secreted virulence
proteins, and structural insights from
X-ray crystallography and cryo-electron microscopy have revealed the
architecture of both β-barrel domains and assembled complexes.
,,−


T5SS operates without direct consumption of ATP or coupling
to the proton motive force at the OM. In Type Va autotransporters,
Sec-dependent delivery to the periplasm is followed by engagement
of the passenger-bearing precursor with BAM. The substrate β-barrel
forms a transient hybrid barrel with BamA, while the passenger enters
and traverses the BamA lumen in a largely extended conformation. Passenger
translocation proceeds from the C-terminus toward the N-terminus and
is coupled to progressive folding on the extracellular side, providing
a thermodynamic and kinetic bias that favors vectorial export and
prevents backward movement.
−

 In the Type Ve inverse autotransporters
intimin and invasin, the same thermodynamic principles presumably
apply, but in reverse orientation: the C-terminal passenger domain
folds progressively on the extracellular face to drive translocation
through the N-terminal β-barrel. Of note, however, the passenger
domains of inverse autotransporters are structurally different from
those of classical autotransporters, and it remains unclear how the
passenger domains of inverse autotransporters fold progressively to
enable secretion. Despite extensive study, several important mechanistic
questions remain unresolved, including how passenger engagement and
release are coordinated with formation and resolution of the transient
BamA–substrate hybrid barrel and how extracellular folding
is kinetically coupled to vectorial translocation.
,
 In comparison to other secretion systems, the T5SS is distinguished
by its minimalist architecture and reliance on periplasmic intermediates,
as well as its lack of a dedicated energy source at the OM, contrasting
with ATP- or proton motive force-driven systems that actively power
substrate export.

 The system was first described
in the late 1980s and early 1990s through studies of secreted virulence
proteins, and structural insights from
X-ray crystallography and cryo-electron microscopy have revealed the
architecture of both β-barrel domains and assembled complexes.
,,−

T5SS operates without direct consumption of ATP or coupling
to the proton motive force at the OM. In Type Va autotransporters,
Sec-dependent delivery to the periplasm is followed by engagement
of the passenger-bearing precursor with BAM. The substrate β-barrel
forms a transient hybrid barrel with BamA, while the passenger enters
and traverses the BamA lumen in a largely extended conformation. Passenger
translocation proceeds from the C-terminus toward the N-terminus and
is coupled to progressive folding on the extracellular side, providing
a thermodynamic and kinetic bias that favors vectorial export and
prevents backward movement.
−

 In the Type Ve inverse autotransporters
intimin and invasin, the same thermodynamic principles presumably
apply, but in reverse orientation: the C-terminal passenger domain
folds progressively on the extracellular face to drive translocation
through the N-terminal β-barrel. Of note, however, the passenger
domains of inverse autotransporters are structurally different from
those of classical autotransporters, and it remains unclear how the
passenger domains of inverse autotransporters fold progressively to
enable secretion. Despite extensive study, several important mechanistic
questions remain unresolved, including how passenger engagement and
release are coordinated with formation and resolution of the transient
BamA–substrate hybrid barrel and how extracellular folding
is kinetically coupled to vectorial translocation.
,
 In comparison to other secretion systems, the T5SS is distinguished
by its minimalist architecture and reliance on periplasmic intermediates,
as well as its lack of a dedicated energy source at the OM, contrasting
with ATP- or proton motive force-driven systems that actively power
substrate export.

10.1.5Type 6 Secretion SystemThe Type
6 secretion system (T6SS) is a contractile, phage tail-like apparatus
in diderm bacteria that mediates the direct delivery of effector proteins
into neighboring bacterial or eukaryotic cells (Figure
).
−



 Its architecture spans the cell envelope and comprises a membrane-anchored
baseplate complex connected to a contractile sheath that surrounds
an inner tube, capped by a sharp spike complex.
−


 The baseplate is embedded in the IM via a multicomponent membrane
complex, which also provides a docking platform for assembly.
,
 The inner tube is built from stacked ring-like protein subunits
and terminates in a trimeric spike sharpened by a penetrating tip,
while the surrounding sheath forms a spring-loaded contractile structure.
,,
 Upon contraction, the sheath
drives the inner tube and spike outward, puncturing target cell membranes. Substrates include a diverse repertoire of
toxic effector proteins, such as cell wall-degrading enzymes, membrane-disrupting
toxins, nucleases, and other antibacterial or anti-eukaryotic factors,
which are either attached to the spike/tube or loaded into its lumen.
,,,
 The T6SS was discovered in the mid-2000s through studies of virulence
and interbacterial competition,
−


 and its structure has been elucidated
in detail using cryo-electron microscopy and cryo-electron tomography,
revealing striking similarity to contractile bacteriophage tails,
which is consistent with their proposed common origin.
,−





,

The T6SS functions through
a rapid contraction event that converts stored mechanical energy into
forceful propulsion of the inner tube and the associated effectors.
Assembly begins with formation of the baseplate and membrane complex,
followed by polymerization of the inner tube and extension of the
surrounding sheath in a high-energy, metastable state.
,
 Effector proteins are either fused to structural components or non-covalently
associated with the tube and spike, ensuring co-delivery during firing.
,,
 Triggering of sheath contraction
results in a dramatic conformational change that shortens and thickens
the sheath, driving the tube-spike complex outward across the bacterial
envelope and into target cells.
,
 After contraction,
the sheath is disassembled and recycled by a cytoplasmic ATPase, allowing
repeated cycles of assembly and firing.
,,,
 Despite extensive structural and functional characterization, key
questions remain, including how assembly and firing is spatially and
temporally regulated in response to environmental or competitive cues,
,−

 how diverse effectors are selectively loaded
and targeted.
,,
 In comparison to other secretion systems, T6SS is distinguished
by its use of a contractile nanomachine that delivers effectors via
mechanical force, rather than translocating substrates through a continuously
open channel, and by its prominent role in interbacterial competition
and microbial community dynamics.

10.1.6Type 7 Secretion SystemThe Type
7 secretion system (T7SS) is a specialized export pathway found predominantly
in monoderm bacteria with thick, hydrophobic cell envelopes as well
as in mycobacteria (Figure
).
−


 The T7SS mediates the translocation of small, helix-rich proteins
and associated complexes across the cytoplasmic membrane and the cell
wall/mycomembrane. The core architecture
is centered on a multimeric IM complex composed of several transmembrane
helices that assemble into a gated export conduit, flanked on the
cytosolic side by a multi-subunit ATPase assembly that organizes substrate
recruitment and energizes export.
−



 In mycobacteria, this membrane complex is
further connected to a periplasmic/cell envelope-associated scaffold
that spans the thick cell wall, forming a continuous path to the cell
surface.
,,
 Substrates are typically small, α-helical proteins (often
secreted as heterodimers), virulence factors, membrane-permeabilizing
toxins, and proteins involved in nutrient acquisition and metal homeostasis;
many are secreted in pairs or complexes that depend on one another
for stability and targeting.
−

 Five paralogous T7SSs, referred
to as ESX-1 through ESX-5, have been identified in mycobacteria, with
each exporting distinct substrates relevant to pathogenesis. The T7SS was first identified in the early
2000s through studies of major virulence determinants in pathogenic
mycobacteria, and structural characterization
by cryo-electron microscopy and X-ray crystallography has revealed
large, multi-protein assemblies with defined cytosolic, membrane,
and periplasmic domains.
,−

,−
,

T7SS-mediated export is
driven by ATP hydrolysis within the cytosolic ATPase assembly, which
undergoes nucleotide-dependent conformational changes that are thought
to coordinate substrate recognition, unfolding (when required), and
engagement with the membrane-embedded conduit.
,,,,,
 Substrate targeting involves short C-terminal or internal sequence
motifs and obligate partner interactions, and in many cases substrates
are delivered as pre-formed complexes that must be selectively recognized
by the secretion machinery.
,−


 Current models propose that ATP-driven conformational cycling opens
and closes the transmembrane channel and may also remodel substrate
complexes to facilitate passage through a relatively narrow conduit.
,,
 In mycobacteria, additional
factors are thought to mediate transport across the outer, lipid-rich
cell envelope, although the precise mechanism by which substrates
traverse this barrier remains incompletely understood.
,
 Outstanding questions include how substrates are crossing the mycobacterial
OM after reaching the periplasm,
,
 how different
T7SS subtypes achieve specificity for distinct substrate groups, and how energy transduction is coupled to transport
across both the membrane and the extended cell envelope.
,,,
 In comparison to other secretion systems, T7SS is distinguished
by its adaptation to thick, low-permeability cell envelopes, its reliance
on ATP-driven cytosolic assemblies rather than proton motive force,
and its specialization for small, structured protein substrates that
are often secreted as complexes, highlighting a mechanistic niche
distinct from both Sec/Tat and the multi-component systems of diderm
bacteria.

10.1.7Type 8 Secretion SystemThe Type
8 secretion system (T8SS) is a specialized OM export pathway in diderm
bacteria that mediates the assembly and secretion of extracellular
amyloid fibers, most notably curli (Figure
).
,−

 It functions as a two-step system: structural subunits are first
translocated across the IM via the Sec pathway and accumulate in the
periplasm, after which they are exported across the OM through a dedicated
β-barrel pore (OM secretin-like channel).
,,
 The core architecture consists
of this OM pore-forming β-barrel, a periplasmic chaperone network
that prevents premature aggregation, and surface-localized nucleator
proteins that template fiber assembly.
,,,,,
 Together, these components form
a coordinated system in which secretion is tightly coupled to extracellular
polymerization. Substrates are primarily amyloidogenic fiber subunits
and associated accessory proteins, which assemble into extracellular
fibrils that contribute to surface adhesion and cell-to-cell cohesion
in biofilms.
,,
 The pathway was elucidated in the late 1990s and early 2000s through
genetic and biochemical studies of curli biogenesis,
,,
 and structural insights from
X-ray crystallography, solid-state NMR, and cryo-electron microscopy
have revealed both the β-barrel secretion pore and the cross-β
architecture of the assembled fibers.
,,,,,

T8SS operates without
direct ATP consumption or coupling to the proton motive force at the
OM, instead relying on Sec-dependent delivery to the periplasm and
assembly of amyloid fibers at the cell surface to drive secretion.
In the periplasm, dedicated chaperones bind subunits and maintain
them in a soluble secretion-competent state, preventing premature
aggregation. Upon reaching the OM pore, amyloidogenic substrates are
translocated in an unfolded or partially structured conformation and
are immediately incorporated into growing extracellular fibers through
a templated polymerization process, in which a surface-exposed nucleator
protein seeds assembly.
,,−

 This coupling of secretion to polymerization
provides a thermodynamic bias that ensures directionality and minimizes
backsliding.
,
 Despite substantial advances,
several key questions remain, including how the secretion pore accommodates
substrates with repetitive, aggregation-prone sequences without clogging,
how substrate flux is regulated, and how specificity is maintained
among multiple amyloid subunits and accessory proteins.
,,,,,
 In comparison to other secretion systems, T8SS is distinguished
by its tight coupling of secretion to extracellular self-assembly,
its reliance on periplasmic chaperone control rather than cytosolic
targeting, and its role in producing functional amyloids rather than
soluble effector proteins.

10.1.8Type 9 Secretion SystemThe Type
9 secretion system (T9SS) is a specialized protein export pathway
found in members of the Bacteroidetes, where it mediates the translocation
of proteins from the periplasm across the OM to the cell surface or
extracellular milieu (Figure
).
−




 As with several OM secretion systems, T9SS operates downstream of
the Sec pathway, which delivers substrates into the periplasm in an
unfolded state, after which they fold prior to secretion. The architecture
spans the cell envelope and includes an OM translocon that forms a
gated secretion pore, a large periplasmic and IM-associated scaffold,
and a cytoplasmic/IM energy-transducing complex.
,−






 These components are organized into a coordinated assembly that
couples energy input at the IM to secretion at the OM over a considerable
periplasmic distance. A hallmark of T9SS substrates is the presence
of a conserved C-terminal targeting signal, which directs proteins
to the system and is often cleaved or modified during secretion. Substrates include cell-surface adhesins, gliding
motility proteins, proteases, s-layer proteins, polysaccharide-degrading
enzymes, and virulence factors, many of which are either retained
on the cell surface through covalent attachment to cell envelope components
or released extracellularly.
,,
 The system was first delineated in the late 2000s through genetic
analyses of gliding motility and surface protein, which identified
a conserved set of envelope-associated components required for both
processes. Subsequent structural studies
using cryo-electron microscopy, X-ray crystallography, and cryo-electron
tomography have revealed that the OM translocon forms a large β-barrel
channel with a gated lumen, while IM-associated complexes assemble
into motor-like structures that couple the proton motive force to
secretion.
,−





,,,

Substrates of the T9SS
bearing the C-terminal targeting signal are recognized in the periplasm
and delivered to the secretion machinery, where they are translocated
through the OM pore.
,
 In many cases, secretion is
coupled to post-translocation processing events, including cleavage
of the targeting signal and covalent attachment of the protein to
cell surface glycoconjugates, effectively anchoring the substrate
to the OM.
−


 The available information supports a model in which T9SS exports
and anchors large adhesins to the cell surface, where they are translocated
along helical tracks to drive gliding motility.
,
 However, the overall organization and full 3D architecture of the
complete T9SS and the precise molecular mechanism by which energy
derived from the proton motive force is transmitted across the periplasm
remain incompletely understood. Additional open questions include
how substrate specificity is achieved given the diversity of exported
proteins, what is the sequence of interactions and conformational
changes during substrate translocation, and how secretion is coordinated
with surface attachment and motility functions in response to environmental
signals.
,
 In comparison to other secretion
systems, T9SS is distinguished by its restriction to a specific bacterial
phylum, its reliance on a C-terminal targeting signal, and its integration
with cell surface remodeling and motility processes, setting it apart
from systems that primarily mediate soluble protein secretion or direct
injection into target cells.

systems, T9SS is distinguished by its restriction to a specific bacterial

10.1.9Type 10 Secretion SystemThe Type
10 secretion system (T10SS) is a recently described export mechanism
in diderm bacteria that mediates the release of proteins from the
cytoplasm to the extracellular environment through controlled disruption
of the cell envelope (Figure
).
−

 Unlike canonical secretion systems that
form dedicated trans-envelope channels, T10SS is composed of IM pore-forming
proteins that oligomerize to create large lesions, together with periplasmic
or envelope-targeted cell wall–degrading enzymes that locally
remodel or cleave the PG layer.
−

 In some cases, these
activities are coordinated with OM destabilization or vesiculation
processes, resulting in the release of cytoplasmic contents without
the assembly of a continuous protein-conducting conduit.
−



 Substrates include cytoplasmic enzymes, toxins, and other proteins
lacking classical secretion signals, many of which are released in
response to environmental cues or during specific developmental or
stress conditions.
,
 The system was identified in
the early 2020s through genetic and functional studies of toxin release
pathways, and structural characterization
of its components by cryo-electron microscopy and time lapse fluorescence
microscopy has revealed oligomeric membrane pores and enzymatic domains
consistent with phage-derived lysis modules.
−


T10SS function is driven by the regulated formation of large
membrane pores, which permit the passage of proteins that would otherwise
be excluded by size or folding state.
,
 Activation
typically involves controlled oligomerization of the IM pore-forming
proteins, leading to rapid permeabilization of the cytoplasmic membrane,
followed by enzymatic degradation of the PG layer, thereby relieving
mechanical constraints on the envelope.
,
 In some systems, this process results in partial or complete cell
lysis, while in others, it appears to be more tightly regulated, allowing
transient permeability or localized envelope disruption.
,,,
 Because substrate release does not require unfolding or threading
through a narrow channel, T10SS can accommodate fully folded proteins
and large macromolecular assemblies.
,
 However,
this mode of secretion raises important questions regarding how cells
regulate how T10SS pores assemble and select cargo, how nonlytic vs
lytic outcomes are specified, how diverse the phage-derived modules
are, and how broadly T10SSs contribute to bacterial interaction with
hosts and environments.
,,,
 In comparison to other secretion
systems, T10SS is distinguished by its reliance on phage-like lytic
modules rather than structured secretion machines, its ability to
release proteins without a defined trans-envelope conduit, and its
frequent coupling to stress responses or programmed cell lysis, highlighting
a fundamentally different strategy for protein export.

10.1.10Type 11 Secretion SystemThe
Type 11 secretion system (T11SS) is a recently defined pathway in
diderm bacteria that mediates the export and surface display of lipoproteins
across the OM (Figure
).
,
 It functions downstream of the Sec pathway,
which delivers substrates to the periplasm and in conjunction with
the Lol pathway (“localization of lipoproteins” pathway),
which enables substrate processing as lipid-anchored proteins tethered
to the inner leaflet of the OM.
−

 The T11SS mediates
the surface exposure of lipoproteins across the OM.
,
 The defining structural feature of T11SS is an OM β-barrel
translocon that assembles into a gated pore, often accompanied by
periplasmic accessory domains or partner proteins that facilitate
substrate engagement.
,,,
 Together, these proteins are
known as the Slam (Surface Lipoprotein Assembly Modulator) family
of proteins. T11SS substrates are typically lipoproteins that remain
membrane-anchored via lipid modifications while being translocated
through the OM to the cell surface.
,
 These substrates
include surface-exposed adhesins, nutrient acquisition proteins, immune
modulators, and host interaction factors, many of which play central
roles in colonization and symbiosis.
,,
 The system was delineated in the early 2020s through
comparative genomics and functional studies that identified the Slam
family as a conserved class of OMPs required for lipoprotein surface
exposure. Biochemical and reconstitution experiments demonstrated
that Slam proteins function as OM translocons for surface lipoproteins,
consistent with predicted β-barrel architectures capable of
forming protein-conducting pores.
,

T11SS
operates without direct ATP consumption at the OM, instead it is thought
to rely on conformational dynamics of the OM translocon to drive export.
,
 Substrate lipoproteins are first inserted into the OM inner leaflet,
after which they are recognized by the T11SS machinery and guided
through the β-barrel pore to the cell surface.
,
 Current models suggest that translocation may involve partial unfolding
or reorientation of the lipoprotein, followed by refolding or stabilization
once exposed extracellularly, although the extent of unfolding required
remains unclear. The energetic basis
of this process is not fully resolved and may involve contributions
from periplasmic chaperones, membrane insertion energetics, and substrate
folding on the cell surface. Key open
questions include how the translocation pore carries out its function,
,
 how specificity is achieved among diverse lipoproteins,
,
 and how T11SS activity integrates with other pathways for surface
exposure of OM lipoproteins and LPS.
,−

 In comparison to other secretion systems, T11SS is distinguished
by its specialization for lipoprotein surface exposure, its reliance
on OM translocation without a continuous trans-envelope channel, and
its integration with lipidation and membrane trafficking pathways,
setting it apart from systems that export soluble proteins or inject
effectors into target cells.

Type 11 Secretion System

The
Type 11 secretion system (T11SS) is a recently defined pathway in
diderm bacteria that mediates the export and surface display of lipoproteins
across the OM (Figure
).
,
 It functions downstream of the Sec pathway,
which delivers substrates to the periplasm and in conjunction with
the Lol pathway (“localization of lipoproteins” pathway),
which enables substrate processing as lipid-anchored proteins tethered
to the inner leaflet of the OM.
−

 The T11SS mediates
the surface exposure of lipoproteins across the OM.
,
 The defining structural feature of T11SS is an OM β-barrel
translocon that assembles into a gated pore, often accompanied by
periplasmic accessory domains or partner proteins that facilitate
substrate engagement.
,,,
 Together, these proteins are
known as the Slam (Surface Lipoprotein Assembly Modulator) family
of proteins. T11SS substrates are typically lipoproteins that remain
membrane-anchored via lipid modifications while being translocated
through the OM to the cell surface.
,
 These substrates
include surface-exposed adhesins, nutrient acquisition proteins, immune
modulators, and host interaction factors, many of which play central
roles in colonization and symbiosis.
,,
 The system was delineated in the early 2020s through
comparative genomics and functional studies that identified the Slam
family as a conserved class of OMPs required for lipoprotein surface
exposure. Biochemical and reconstitution experiments demonstrated
that Slam proteins function as OM translocons for surface lipoproteins,
consistent with predicted β-barrel architectures capable of
forming protein-conducting pores.
,

T11SS
operates without direct ATP consumption at the OM, instead it is thought
to rely on conformational dynamics of the OM translocon to drive export.
,
 Substrate lipoproteins are first inserted into the OM inner leaflet,
after which they are recognized by the T11SS machinery and guided
through the β-barrel pore to the cell surface.
,
 Current models suggest that translocation may involve partial unfolding
or reorientation of the lipoprotein, followed by refolding or stabilization
once exposed extracellularly, although the extent of unfolding required
remains unclear. The energetic basis
of this process is not fully resolved and may involve contributions
from periplasmic chaperones, membrane insertion energetics, and substrate
folding on the cell surface. Key open
questions include how the translocation pore carries out its function,
,
 how specificity is achieved among diverse lipoproteins,
,
 and how T11SS activity integrates with other pathways for surface
exposure of OM lipoproteins and LPS.
,−

 In comparison to other secretion systems, T11SS is distinguished
by its specialization for lipoprotein surface exposure, its reliance
on OM translocation without a continuous trans-envelope channel, and
its integration with lipidation and membrane trafficking pathways,
setting it apart from systems that export soluble proteins or inject
effectors into target cells.

10.2Role of Secretion in PathogenesisBacterial secretion systems contribute to pathogenesis not through
the machinery itself but through the effectors, toxins, and other
macromolecules that they deliver. Across the 11 named specialized
systems described in Section
 and , the pathogenic roles range
from direct injection of effector proteins into host cells to release
of degradative enzymes into the extracellular environment to dissemination
of antibiotic resistance genes through bacterial populations.
,
 The systems most directly linked to virulence during infection are
T3SS, T4SS, T6SS, and T7SS, each of which delivers substrates that
manipulate host cell biology in distinct ways. T1SS, T2SS, and T5SS
contribute to pathogenesis primarily by secreting toxins, proteases,
and adhesins into the extracellular environment. T8SS contributes
through biofilm formation on host surfaces, with curli fiber assembly
described in Section
. T9SS, T10SS, and T11SS have more specialized or restricted roles,
which are discussed briefly below.The T3SS is among the best-characterized
virulence delivery systems in diderm pathogens, injecting effector
proteins directly into the host cell cytoplasm, where they subvert
signaling, cytoskeletal organization, and immune responses.
,
 In S. enterica, the SPI-1 T3SS injects effectors
including that collectively remodel the host actin cytoskeleton to
drive bacterial uptake into nonphagocytic cells.
−

 A second T3SS encoded by SPI-2 is expressed in later stages of infection
and injects effectors that remodel the Salmonella-containing vacuole to prevent lysosomal fusion and support intracellular
replication. In Yersinia species, the Ysc T3SS injects effectors that collectively disrupt
phagocytosis, inhibit inflammatory signaling, and induce macrophage
apoptosis, allowing the bacteria to resist clearance in lymphoid tissue. In enteropathogenic E. coli, the LEE-encoded T3SS injects Tir into the host membrane, where
it acts as the receptor for the bacterial adhesin intimin (Section
), driving
intimate attachment and actin pedestal formation beneath the bacterium.
The T4SS contributes to pathogenesis
through two distinct mechanisms:
direct effector injection into host cells and horizontal transfer
of virulence and resistance determinants between bacteria. In H. pylori, the Cag pathogenicity island encodes a T4SS that
injects the oncoprotein CagA directly into gastric epithelial cells,
where it is phosphorylated by host Src and Abl kinases and disrupts
cell polarity, promotes proliferation, and inhibits apoptosis, contributing
to the progression from chronic gastritis to gastric cancer. In L. pneumophila, the Dot/Icm
T4SS injects over 300 effector proteins into the host macrophage that
collectively manipulate vesicle trafficking, prevent phagosome maturation,
and remodel the Legionella-containing vacuole into
an endoplasmic reticulum-derived compartment permissive for intracellular
replication. The PG remodeling enzyme
PgdA, described in Section
, is required for proper assembly of the L. pneumophila Dot/Icm apparatus, illustrating how cell envelope components directly
support secretion system function. T4SS also drives pathogenesis indirectly
through conjugative transfer of virulence and antibiotic resistance
plasmids, as described in Section
, contributing to the spread of resistance determinants,
including extended-spectrum β-lactamases and carbapenemases,
across clinical pathogen populations.
The T6SS delivers its effectors through a contractile phage
tail-like
apparatus that punctuates target cell membranes directly, as described
in Section
. In P. aeruginosa, T6SS effectors include phospholipases,
cell-wall hydrolases, and nucleases that kill competing bacteria in
polymicrobial infections and in the chronic lung infection environment
of cystic fibrosis, providing a competitive fitness advantage. The T6SS also has direct antihost roles: Burkholderia species deploy T6SS to escape macrophage phagosomes
and gain access to the host cell cytoplasm, facilitating intracellular
survival and cell-to-cell spread.
V. cholerae T6SS delivers effectors that lyse competing
bacteria and can damage intestinal epithelial cells during infection. The competitive interbacterial function of
the T6SS is unique and is increasingly recognized as relevant to infection
outcomes because it shapes the composition of polymicrobial communities
at infection sites, with implications for the severity and persistence
of chronic infections.
The T7SS
is the primary virulence secretion pathway in mycobacteria,
where five paralogous systems ESX-1 through ESX-5 each export distinct
substrates relevant to pathogenesis, as introduced in Section
 and described in the context
of the mycobacterial cell envelope in Section
. ESX-1 secretes
the heterodimeric complex EsxA (ESAT-6) and EsxB (CFP-10), which permeabilizes
phagosomal and plasma membranes, allowing M. tuberculosis to access the host cytoplasm and trigger NLRP3 inflammasome activation
and type I interferon responses.
,
 ESX-3 is
essential for iron and zinc acquisition during infection and is required
for M. tuberculosis viability within the host, making
it a target of interest for antimicrobial development. ESX-5 is the primary export pathway for the
large PE/PPE protein family described in Section
, many of which modulate TLR2 and TLR4 signaling,
suppress inflammatory cytokine production, and facilitate immune evasion
during chronic infection. In some strains
of the monoderm pathogen S. aureus, T7SS secretes
the effector EsaD, a nuclease toxin, and a companion antitoxin, EsaG.
T1SS, T2SS, and T5SS contribute to pathogenesis
primarily through
extracellular secretion of toxins and degradative enzymes rather than
direct host cell injection. T1SS secretes large, unfolded substrates
including the E. coli pore-forming hemolysin HlyA,
which lyses erythrocytes and epithelial cells to release nutrients
during urinary tract infection, and Serratia marcescens lipase and hemolysin, which degrade
host cell membranes and contribute to opportunistic infection. T2SS secretes folded enzymes of broad virulence
relevance, including cholera toxin in V. cholerae, which ADP-ribosylates the Gs α subunit to drive constitutive
adenylate cyclase activity and the secretory diarrhea that defines
cholera,
,
 and exotoxin A in P.
aeruginosa, which ADP-ribosylates elongation factor 2 to
halt host cell protein synthesis. T5SS
autotransporters and two-partner secretion systems export surface-exposed
and secreted virulence factors including the serine protease autotransporters
of Enterobacteriaceae such as EspP and Pet, which
cleave host complement proteins, coagulation factors, and mucosal
barrier components to promote colonization, and the trimeric adhesins YadA and NadA discussed in Section
.
,
 T8SS contributes to pathogenesis through curli fiber assembly, which
promotes biofilm formation on host tissues and implanted devices,
and through curli-induced co-activation of TLR2 and TLR1 that can
drive systemic inflammatory responses during bacteremia. T9SS, expressed in Bacteroidetes including
the periodontal pathogen P. gingivalis, secretes
gingipain cysteine proteases that degrade host immunoglobulins, complement
components, and connective tissue matrix proteins, contributing to
the tissue destruction characteristic of severe periodontitis. T10SS and T11SS have pathogenic roles that
remain less fully characterized; T11SS in Neisseria species mediates the surface display of lipoproteins including the
transferrin-binding protein TbpB described in Section
, contributing to iron acquisition and
immune evasion during invasive infection.
,,

10.3Secretion System Therapeutic TargetsThe 11 classified secretion systems (T1SS–T11SS) all share
one defining therapeutic property: they are required for the delivery
of virulence factors and are therefore attractive anti-virulence targets
whose inhibition would disarm the pathogen. Because secretion system
components are absent from mammalian cells and are surface-exposed
or periplasm-accessible, they are also exploitable as vaccine antigens.
Therapeutic development has concentrated overwhelmingly on T3SS, T4SS,
and the mycobacterium-specific T7SS (ESX systems), where the molecular
architecture and pathogenic mechanisms are best characterized, while
T1SS, T2SS, T5SS–T9SS, T10SS, and T11SS remain at the target
validation stage with no clinical programs specifically targeting
the secretion machinery.
The type
III secretion system is the most extensively pursued antivirulence
target among the secretion systems. Multiple small-molecule inhibitor
classes have been identified that disrupt T3SS assembly, needle polymerization,
or effector translocation in vitro and in animal
models of infection. However, none have advanced to clinical trials,
in part because T3SS is dispensable for bacterial growth, raising
concerns about the stringency of selective pressure and the achievable
therapeutic window. Anti-PcrV monoclonal
antibodies have a well-established preclinical track record: the foundational
murine anti-PcrV antibody MAb166 demonstrated that blocking PcrV prevents
T3SS toxin delivery and protects against lethal P. aeruginosa lung infection. And, combination of
anti-PcrV antibodies with antibiotics provides a synergistic survival
benefit beyond either alone. The bispecific
antibody MEDI3902 (MedImmune/AstraZeneca), targeting both PcrV and
the Psl exopolysaccharide, provided dose-dependent protection in both
murine and rabbit pneumonia models and entered phase 2 clinical trials. The more recently characterized humanized antibody
COT-143 (INFEX702, Shionogi) demonstrates the same mechanism with
additional evidence of neutrophil protection and facilitated phagocytosis
confirmed by the crystal structure of the antibody-PcrV complex. AAV-vectored delivery of anti-PcrV antibodies
has also been explored as a long-term prophylactic strategy for high-risk
populations including those with cystic fibrosis.
The type IV secretion system, exemplified by the H. pylori Cag pathogenicity island apparatus, translocates
the oncoprotein
CagA directly into gastric epithelial cells, where it triggers signaling
cascades associated with gastric carcinogenesis. The T4SS complex
is therefore a validated virulence target, and several small-molecule
inhibitors of T4SS ATPase activity and assembly have been identified
in preclinical studies, although none are in clinical development. In mycobacteria, the ESX-1 T7SS secretes proteins
that permeabilize the phagosomal membrane, enabling M. tuberculosis to escape into the cytoplasm and prevent phagolysosomal maturation.
ESX-1 is essential for full virulence and is absent from the BCG vaccine
strain, identifying it as a high-priority antivirulence target. Virtual
screening campaigns and cell infection models have identified candidate
ESX-1 inhibitors, although the field remains at the early preclinical
stage.
10.3.1Secretion System Vaccine TargetsSecretion system structural proteins are immunogenic during natural
infection and are vaccine antigens, most notably in the context of Shigella and P. aeruginosa. In Shigella, the T3SS needle tip protein IpaD and the translocon
protein IpaB are surface exposed during infection, highly conserved
across Shigella serotypes, and elicit protective
immune responses in animal models. As conserved structural components
independent of serotype, they are particularly attractive for serotype-independent
protection, addressing one of the fundamental limitations of polysaccharide-based Shigella vaccines. Despite
more than a century of research, no globally licensed Shigella vaccine has been developed. Multiple candidates have progressed
to phase 2 and phase 3 clinical trials, including live attenuated
strains, conjugate polysaccharide formulations, and protein subunit
approaches incorporating IpaB, IpaD, and other T3SS-associated proteins.
The T3SS component PcrV of P. aeruginosa is immunogenic
in infected patients and is included as an antigen in multicomponent P. aeruginosa vaccine formulations under investigation.
However, the most clinically advanced anti-PcrV program, the bispecific
antibody MEDI3902 targeting both PcrV and the Psl exopolysaccharide,
did not meet its primary end point in the Phase 2 trial. This disappointing
result emphasizes that surface exposure and preclinical efficacy do
not guarantee clinical translation for T3SS-targeted approaches. In M. tuberculosis, the ESX-1
substrates, ESAT-6 and CFP-10, are among the most immunodominant T
cell antigens in natural infection and are included in multiple next-generation
subunit TB vaccine formulations, most notably M72/AS01E. The M72/AS01E
Phase 3 trial initiated first doses in March 2024 and continues to
be followed. Across T1SS through T11SS
other than T3SS, T4SS, and T7SS, the structural components have not
yielded clinical vaccine candidates because the apparatus components
are generally not surface-exposed in a manner accessible to antibody-mediated
neutralization.

11Summary and Future DirectionsThe
bacterial surface is one of the most chemically and architecturally
diverse interfaces in biological world. As this Review has described,
the layers that constitute this surface, from the IM outward through
the PG, teichoic acids, S-layer, OM, mycomembrane, capsule, and surface
appendages, each represent distinct evolutionary solutions to the
shared challenge of maintaining cellular integrity while navigating
a hostile host environment. The remarkable variation in lipid composition,
protein architecture, carbohydrate modification, and secretion machinery
documented across the Gracilicutes and Terrabacteria clades underscores how deeply membrane chemistry is intertwined
with the mechanisms of infection, from initial adhesion and colonization
to immune evasion and persistence.Several themes emerge from
the survey of this diversity. First,
layering and redundancy are recurring organizational principles: the
cell envelope is not a single barrier but a series of functionally
distinct compartments, each with dedicated biogenesis machinery and
each contributing independently to pathogenesis. Second, chemical
modifications are a universal adaptation strategy. Whether through
lipid A remodeling to attenuate TLR4 activation, d-alanylation
of teichoic acids to resist antimicrobial peptides, O-acetylation
of CPS to alter antigenicity, or antigenic variation of S-layer proteins
to evade adaptive immunity, bacteria continuously tune the chemistry
of their surfaces in response to host pressures. Third, biogenesis
and pathogenicity are inseparable: the machinery that builds the surface
(the BAM complex, the Lol pathway, the sortases, the secretion systems,
and the chaperone networks) is simultaneously the machinery that equips
it for infection. Understanding one therefore informs the other.These insights have direct therapeutic implications. The components
described in this Review represent a rich landscape of potential antimicrobial
targets, many of which are either absent from or structurally distinct
from their eukaryotic counterparts. The LPS transport machinery, sortase-mediated
surface protein display, OMP biogenesis via the BAM complex, lipoprotein
trafficking through the Lol pathway, capsule biosynthetic enzymes,
pilus assembly systems, and type III through type VII secretion systems
have all been explored as targets for small molecules, biologics,
or vaccine antigens with varying degrees of preclinical and clinical
success. Capsular polysaccharide conjugate vaccines against H. influenzae, S. pneumoniae, and N. meningitidis stand as proof of concept that a deep mechanistic
understanding of surface architecture can be translated into transformative
public health interventions.Significant gaps remain. The biogenesis
pathways for the integral
proteins of the mycomembrane have not been identified. The mechanisms
by which many surface lipoproteins are flipped to the cell exterior
remain poorly understood. The full structural diversity of BAM complexes
across bacterial phyla, as recently highlighted by divergent architectures
in Bacteroidota and spirochetes, is just beginning to be appreciated.
The regulation of capsule expression in response to host signals,
the molecular basis of curli-mediated innate immune activation, and
the precise mechanisms by which type IX and type X secretion systems
couple energy to substrate export are areas of active investigation
where much remains to be learned. In addition, the contributions of
surface structures to polymicrobial infections, biofilm communities,
and interbacterial competition represent an expanding frontier that
the field is just beginning to address systematically.As antibiotic
resistance continues to erode the efficacy of existing
therapeutics, a deeper molecular understanding of bacterial surface
diversity will be essential for the development of next-generation
interventions. The chemical determinants of surface assembly, stability,
and host recognition described throughout this Review provide a framework
for that effort, and the breadth of mechanisms now recognized across
bacterial lineages ensures that this remains one of the most fertile
areas of inquiry in microbiology and chemical biology.

Summary and Future Directions

The
bacterial surface is one of the most chemically and architecturally
diverse interfaces in biological world. As this Review has described,
the layers that constitute this surface, from the IM outward through
the PG, teichoic acids, S-layer, OM, mycomembrane, capsule, and surface
appendages, each represent distinct evolutionary solutions to the
shared challenge of maintaining cellular integrity while navigating
a hostile host environment. The remarkable variation in lipid composition,
protein architecture, carbohydrate modification, and secretion machinery
documented across the Gracilicutes and Terrabacteria clades underscores how deeply membrane chemistry is intertwined
with the mechanisms of infection, from initial adhesion and colonization
to immune evasion and persistence.

Several themes emerge from
the survey of this diversity. First,
layering and redundancy are recurring organizational principles: the
cell envelope is not a single barrier but a series of functionally
distinct compartments, each with dedicated biogenesis machinery and
each contributing independently to pathogenesis. Second, chemical
modifications are a universal adaptation strategy. Whether through
lipid A remodeling to attenuate TLR4 activation, d-alanylation
of teichoic acids to resist antimicrobial peptides, O-acetylation
of CPS to alter antigenicity, or antigenic variation of S-layer proteins
to evade adaptive immunity, bacteria continuously tune the chemistry
of their surfaces in response to host pressures. Third, biogenesis
and pathogenicity are inseparable: the machinery that builds the surface
(the BAM complex, the Lol pathway, the sortases, the secretion systems,
and the chaperone networks) is simultaneously the machinery that equips
it for infection. Understanding one therefore informs the other.

These insights have direct therapeutic implications. The components
described in this Review represent a rich landscape of potential antimicrobial
targets, many of which are either absent from or structurally distinct
from their eukaryotic counterparts. The LPS transport machinery, sortase-mediated
surface protein display, OMP biogenesis via the BAM complex, lipoprotein
trafficking through the Lol pathway, capsule biosynthetic enzymes,
pilus assembly systems, and type III through type VII secretion systems
have all been explored as targets for small molecules, biologics,
or vaccine antigens with varying degrees of preclinical and clinical
success. Capsular polysaccharide conjugate vaccines against H. influenzae, S. pneumoniae, and N. meningitidis stand as proof of concept that a deep mechanistic
understanding of surface architecture can be translated into transformative
public health interventions.

Significant gaps remain. The biogenesis
pathways for the integral
proteins of the mycomembrane have not been identified. The mechanisms
by which many surface lipoproteins are flipped to the cell exterior
remain poorly understood. The full structural diversity of BAM complexes
across bacterial phyla, as recently highlighted by divergent architectures
in Bacteroidota and spirochetes, is just beginning to be appreciated.
The regulation of capsule expression in response to host signals,
the molecular basis of curli-mediated innate immune activation, and
the precise mechanisms by which type IX and type X secretion systems
couple energy to substrate export are areas of active investigation
where much remains to be learned. In addition, the contributions of
surface structures to polymicrobial infections, biofilm communities,
and interbacterial competition represent an expanding frontier that
the field is just beginning to address systematically.

As antibiotic
resistance continues to erode the efficacy of existing
therapeutics, a deeper molecular understanding of bacterial surface
diversity will be essential for the development of next-generation
interventions. The chemical determinants of surface assembly, stability,
and host recognition described throughout this Review provide a framework
for that effort, and the breadth of mechanisms now recognized across
bacterial lineages ensures that this remains one of the most fertile
areas of inquiry in microbiology and chemical biology.
